#3720. You Should Update Your Garmin Watch's Heart Rate Zones

Zone 2 training is still having a moment—even if no one can agree on what it actually means. Legend has it that your easy, conversational-pace cardio—aka your Zone 2 heart rate—is the secret to unlocking everything from a bigger aerobic engine, to faster race times, and even to a longer life.

Whether Zone 2 deserves quite this much hype is debatable, but still, there are plenty of good reasons to master heart rate training more broadly. To train by heart rate, you aim to keep your heart rate in the "zone" that gives you your desired workout. Even if you believe every word of the Zone 2 gospel, it's completely pointless if your watch has the wrong idea of what your Zone 2 is. If you train with a Garmin watch, here’s how—and why—you should reset your heart rate zones now (and periodically).

When you first set up your Garmin, it asked for your age, weight, and a few other details. From there it generated heart rate zones, often using a default formula based on a maximum heart rate estimated from your age (typically some version of 220 minus your age). But these formulas are often wrong, since they give a single number for everybody at the same age, while real maximum heart rates vary from person to person.

Plus, there isn’t one universally agreed-upon heart rate zone system. So if your watch assumed a max of 180 and yours is actually 195, every zone it gave you is shifted. Your "Zone 2" might really, for you, be Zone 1 (AKA a pace so easy it barely feels like a workout). Or your "Zone 2" might actually be a moderate-to-hard effort that's digging you into a hole, while you think you're taking it easy. At every step of the way, there’s a different formula and definition that determines what heart rate zone shows up on your watchface.

To complicate things further, Garmin gives multiple options to set up your zones:

Percent of max heart rate. This is the simplest approach. You enter (or let the watch estimate) your max, and each zone is a slice of that number. It's only as good as your max heart rate figure, which, as noted above, is often a guess.

Percent of heart rate reserve (HRR). This method factors in both your max and your resting heart rate, so zones are based on the range your heart works across rather than just the top of it. It tends to produce more personalized zones, but it means your resting heart rate needs to be current, too.

Percent of lactate threshold heart rate (LTHR). Here, your zones are anchored to the effort level where your body starts producing lactate faster than it can clear it, a point that's closely tied to how you actually perform. Many coaches consider this the most meaningful anchor, because it reflects your real physiology rather than a formula.

That third option raises its own question: Where does your lactate threshold number come from?

In the past, getting a lactate threshold estimate from a Garmin meant wearing a compatible chest strap and doing a guided test, which involved a structured, hard run on your part. On newer models, however, that's no longer the case. Garmin has been rolling out an updated lactate threshold detection that works from the wrist's optical heart rate sensor, with no chest strap and no dedicated test. Instead, the watch estimates your threshold automatically from regular outdoor runs. This feature is typically on by default and generally requires a baseline VO2 max estimate and a run or trail run recorded with heart rate. If you'd rather not leave it up to the algorithm, you can turn auto-detection off and enter your own lactate threshold heart rate manually in the heart rate zone settings. Which watches have this varies by model, so check your specific device.

Even if you set your zones carefully once, they don't stay accurate forever. Several things change over time:

Your fitness changes. As you get fitter, your resting heart rate often drops, and your lactate threshold heart rate can shift. Zones based on where you were a year or two ago may not reflect where you are now.

You age. Your maximum heart rate tends to decline gradually over the years.

You change sports. Running, cycling, and swimming each have different heart rate behavior, and Garmin lets you set sport-specific zones for exactly this reason. If you've never touched them, you may be using running-based numbers on the bike, or vice versa.

You switched watches or imported old data. Zones can carry over from a previous device or a profile you set up years ago.

Your resting heart rate has changed. An outdated resting number could be throwing things off.

None of these have dramatic consequences, but if you’re focused on heart rate training, it’s worth trying to be as accurate and up-to-date as possible. If your zones are set too high, you may not be getting the aerobic stimulus you think you are. You'll also underestimate how hard your harder sessions really are, and your training load and recovery metrics can end up skewed.

On the flip side, if your zones are set too low, your "easy" runs could actually become the classic trap of training in the gray zone: too hard to recover from well, too easy to drive big gains. And because Garmin's training status, recovery time, and load features lean on your heart rate data, bad zones can quietly nudge those numbers off as well.

The good news is that fixing all this should take less than 10 minutes. First, decide whether you want zones based on max heart rate, heart rate reserve, or lactate threshold. There’s a good chance you can trust the lactate threshold your watch gives you, or you can find your actual max heart rate.

The informal way is to just notice what number you see on your watch when you can no longer speak comfortably; the more formal way is this treadmill test. You can also look through your recent hard efforts, like races, hill repeats, or all-out intervals, and see the highest sustained heart rate you've recorded. If you want a more deliberate test, a coach or a structured max-effort protocol can help, but only attempt hard testing if you're healthy and your doctor is comfortable with it. Also keep in mind that reading from a wrist-based sensor can also spike unreliably, so a chest strap gives more trustworthy numbers when accuracy matters.

Next, check your recent average resting heart rate in Garmin Connect and make sure the number your zones are using is current.

In your watch's user profile or in Garmin Connect, find the heart rate zone settings. From there you can adjust your max, change how zones are calculated, and set different zones for different sports.

Finally, put these new zones to the test. Go for an easy run or ride at what your watch calls Zone 2. Can you hold a conversation, breathing comfortably in full sentences? Does it feel sustainable for a long time? If it feels either laughably easy or surprisingly taxing, your zones probably need another look.

Maybe Zone 2 is the cornerstone of your training, or maybe you think it's completely overrated. Either way, the principle is the same: You should be able to train by feel, and your watch can only help you do that if the numbers behind it are right.

Garmin gives you more ways to set your zones than most brands do. Spend 10 minutes figuring out which method your zones are using, confirming the number behind it is current, and updating it in your settings. It’ll make every easy run, hard interval, and recovery metric on your wrist more useful.

#3719. Alfonso Ribeiro Responds After DWTS Alum Alexa PenaVega Claims It’s ‘Rigged’

The co-host was ready to defend the show.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Alfonso Ribeiro went on the offensive when former contestant Alexa PenaVega called Dancing With The Stars rigged. The co-host wasn't entertaining the idea that the show finds ways to keep certain contestants in the game, and said it all came down to "simple logic."

Ribeiro took a break from preparing for DWTS' "Horror Movie Night" episode to hit the 2026 TV schedule, and hopped in the comments of a viral TikTok. In said video, PenaVega recalled back in Season 21 when fellow contestant Hayes Grier accused the show of being rigged. The viral Vine star said that he likely had millions of votes coming in compared to competitors, and yet was eliminated fifth. PenaVega hinted she agreed with him and mentioned the show does have producers that could influence the result, but Ribeiro jumped in and shut it down quickly:

No chance. We want everyone who’s popular to stay on. It’s basic logic.

PenaVega stressed in a reply back to Ribeiro that she was just telling a story, and the drama seemingly ended there. In fairness, it's not like no one has ever made this accusation before. Part of this is because Dancing With The Stars never reveals the breakdown of each week's voting, outside of occasional announcements about how many total viewers voted.

DWTS’ Connor Woods Can’t Get Away From Calling Jennifer Lawrence His ‘Crush,’ And Even His Pro Is Trolling

We don't see the voting, but we do know how much power the audience vote has. Showrunner Conrad Green told EW it's a 50/50 split between the judges' scores and the audience vote. Based on that breakdown, there's really no leeway for producers to get a vote. If so, one would think they would've gotten rid of Guillermo Rodriguez a little quicker this season.

As for Hayes Grier, it's also fair to say that follower counts don't always equate to votes on Dancing With The Stars. He might have the same follower count as Season 21 winner Bindi Irwin on Instagram, but that doesn't mean his audience on Vine was tuned into DWTS. It's also worth saying that follower counts can be inflated on most social media platforms, and it's possible Grier didn't have as large a following as he thought.

Back in 2026, Dancing With The Stars has a double elimination on the way. The odds of a fan-favorite going home unexpectedly are greater than ever, which usually is when the "rigging" accusations start to flood in. I'm sure the judges are used to it by now, and would sooner deal with that than feuds involving lifts.

Speaking of which, Val Chmerkovskiy is back on Dancing With The Stars, fresh off his week-long suspension following comments on social media about Carrie Ann Inaba. In short, this is about to be can't-miss television, especially if Alfonso ends up shading Alexa PenaVega during the live episode! We can only cross our fingers and hope.

#3718. Slayyyter Cancels Remaining UK and European Tour Dates

Slayyyter is calling off the last shows of her ongoing UK and European tour on doctor's orders. In an Instagram story viewed by Pitchfork, the pop singer wrote, “I am heartbroken to share after multiple doctor visits i have laryngitis and vocal cord tears,” adding, “Because of this, i am forced to cancel the remaining UK/EU dates to avoid permanent damage to my voice.” Among the scrapped Wor$t Girl in America tour stops are Copenhagen, Hamburg, Warsaw, Prague, Berlin, Antwerp, Manchester, Birmingham, Glasgow, Brighton, and London. Slayyyter has promised full refunds to fans and is “already working on rebooking shows for next year.” You can read her full note below.

Speaking of next year, Slayyyter is still slated to play dates in 2027 across Australia, New Zealand, and South America. Before that, she’ll debut her Wor$t Girl companion album, Wor$t Man in America, on December 4.

hey guys, i am heartbroken to share after multiple doctor visits i have laryngitis and vocal cord tears and have been ordered to go on medication and full vocal rest for at least two weeks. Because of this, i am forced to cancel the remaining UK/EU dates to avoid permanent damage to my voice. full refunds will be available at point of sale.

my team and i are already working on rebooking shows for next year and i hope to have news to share very soon to make it up to you. I know how much time and effort is spent to come out to a show. some of you have traveled, some of you have made costumes, and i am completely devastated to not be able to give you the shows you were planning for. we are working hard to figure out a time to return so i can deliver an even bigger and better show for you all. i hope you know i will make this up to you . lots of love to you all. thank you for making this tour so spcial.”

#3716. Kate Middleton Welcomes Prince George Back From Boarding School: “I Can’t Wait to Have Them All Home.”

When your teenage child leaves home, nothing can make the separation easy. Kate Middleton knows this all too well. She confessed to missing her eldest son, Prince George, who returned home for school break after his first few weeks at Eton College. If the Princess of Wales set aside her proverbial British composure for once, it was for a reason all mothers would understand: the joy of finally having the family reunited.

She made this revelation during a visit to a charity shop run by East Anglia’s Children’s Hospices in Norfolk. Between chatting with volunteers and meeting fans, Kate opened up a bit about family life. As Denise Ashton, who spoke with her, recounted, the princess couldn’t wait for George to come home. A simple statement, reported by the Press Association and picked up by People and Tyla, speaks volumes more than many official speeches.

George, second in line to the British throne, began attending Eton last September, following in his father Prince William’s footsteps. Founded in 1440, the prestigious institution is one of the most famous and selective schools in the United Kingdom, attended over the centuries by prime ministers, aristocrats, and future monarchs. But even the most exclusive boarding schools have vacations. From October 23 to November 8, the prince will enjoy what’s known as “long leave,” a 16-day break that will allow him to reunite with his family at Forest Lodge in Windsor.

Kate has been enthusiastically looking forward to the break. When a child told her that school vacation was about to begin, the princess replied, “My children break up [come home] tomorrow, and I can’t wait to have them all home.” A spontaneous remark that offered a decidedly down-to-earth glimpse of the future queen.

For Middleton and Kate, George’s departure marked a small revolution. Their eldest son, who turned 13 last July, now lives at boarding school, while Charlotte, 11, and Louis, 8, continue to attend Lambrook School.

This new family dynamic resembles that of many families that have a first child who has left home. For the Windsors, however, George is only a few kilometers from the family home, and the boarding school resembles a castle. But nostalgia, evidently, makes no distinction based on social class. Who knows—perhaps Kate has already planned some special activities for her three children over the past few days. Ashton hinted that the family might take a trip during the school break. However, no destination has been confirmed.

At any rate, Prince George can set aside his books, uniforms, and schoolwork for a bit and go back to simply being William and Kate’s eldest son. Growing up is inevitable—even when you’re destined to be king—but to your own mom, you’ll always be a little kid. That probably holds just as true at Buckingham Palace as any other home in the world.

#3715. How Sophia Bush Learned of Chad Michael Murray's Alleged Cheating

Sophia Bush alleged in her new memoir And I Will Tell You Mine that Chad Michael Murray’s close friend told her about his cheating before their 2006 divorce: "I see the heat map of red flags.”

Sophia Bush is sharing insight into a difficult chapter.

Nearly two decades after the One Tree Hill alum finalized her divorce from costar Chad Michael Murray, she alleged in her new memoir And I Will Tell You Mine that learning of his infidelity from one of his close friends is what ultimately led to their split.

“It had always bothered me that one of [Murray’s] best friends had fallen mysteriously ill the night before the [wedding] day and not shown up to stand up there as a groomsman,” Bush wrote in the book, out now. “Thinking about it that summer gave me the chills. I needed to know why.”

And when Bush called the no-show in the months following her and Murray’s 2005 nuptials, when the John Tucker Must Die star was filming movies back-to-back, she says that the A Cinderella Story star’s pal sighed and asked why she hadn’t called earlier.

“And then he told me. Everything he could about all the girls he knew of,” Bush wrote. “Girls I knew and plenty of girls I didn’t. The number and the details are not important now. But at the time, they were shocking. Unimaginable, really. And brutal.”

“Including the fact that another woman was living in my spouse’s house in L.A. when I had met him in North Carolina,” she wrote. “Not a bats--t-crazy ex who’d shown up unannounced at all. Another partner. So much for the cute single guy at work.”

Bush, 44, and Murray, 45, met on the set of One Tree Hill in 2003, getting engaged in May 2004 during a trip to Australia. They went on to tie the knot in an intimate ceremony in Santa Monica, California, in April the following year when Bush was 21 and Murray was 23.

But, just five months later, in September 2005, the pair separated. After Bush unsuccessfully sought to annul their marriage citing fraud, the pair finalized their divorce in December 2006.

Even after their tumultuous breakup, they continued working together on OTH until he left in in 2009, three years before the she wrapped.

“Looking back now, I see the heat map of red flags,” she wrote. “I didn’t then. Love exists in real time, and in its absence, all the things that it blurs become clearer. Hindsight is a mother--ker.”

Bush—who went on to marry now-ex Grant Hughes and quietly wed wife Ashlyn Harris in 2025—goes on to write that she sees it “in perfect focus now” as she reflects on Murray’s “boys’ nights” and other instances that made her feel “the most searing humiliation I’d ever felt.”

“I finally understood why, at our rehearsal dinner, his father asked my mother if the female costar from Australia had been invited to the wedding and laughed when I’d responded, ‘No, she’s nice, but they’re not that close,’” she wrote. “I realized in the moment why he chuckled and looked at the floor then.”

E! News has reached out to a rep for Murray for comment but has not yet heard back.

Previously, Murray—dad to three kids with wife Sarah Roemer—shared he looks back on his marriage to Bush with no ill will, though he admitted to having some growing up to do at the time.

"I was a baby. I didn't know up, down, left, right," he explained to The Cut in 2024. "You move out there and you go, ‘Well, what am I supposed to do now? Get married? That's exciting!'"

#3714. Harry Potter HBO TV Series Casts James And Lily Potter, Hinting At Major Change From The Movies

Ahead of the premiere of "Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone" (the inaugural outing of HBO's Harry Potter TV series, which will set out to faithfully adapt each of the seven books about the titular boy wizard), we've gotten a steady trickle of casting news for future seasons, like Kit Harington's upcoming role as the fraudulent wizarding professor and author Gilderoy Lockhart. This latest update, though, is about the first season and shows just how the series will differentiate itself from the Harry Potter films ... by including characters and flashbacks we haven't seen before.

According to Variety and other outlets, the series, overseen by Francesca Gardiner (who previously worked on HBO's "Succession"), has announced three key roles for Season 1 that were previously kept under wraps. Harry Gilby will play Charlie Weasley, the second-eldest son of the wizarding family the Weasleys who works as a dragon tamer in Romania and helps Harry Potter (Dominic McLaughlin), Hermione Granger (Arabella Stanton), and his own younger brother Ron Weasley (Alastair Stout) deal with a dragon at one point. In addition, Jasper Talbot and Hannah Chinn will appear in "Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone" as James and Lily Potter, Harry's parents who are killed by the Dark Lord Voldemort when Harry is just one year old and approximately 10 years before the narrative properly begins. (Voldemort's casting, as of this writing, remains a secret.)

This is pretty big news to anyone who's familiar with the first Harry Potter book and movie. Why? Charlie, James, and Lily are all small but pivotal parts of the story, and Charlie isn't even in the first film in the Harry Potter franchise. Not only that, but this could right a major narrative wrong that concerns James and Lily in the movies.

It's wild, in retrospect, that Charlie Weasley never shows up in the Harry Potter film franchise. Not only are the Weasleys a huge part of the narrative — both because Ron is a main character and because they become a surrogate family to the orphaned Harry — but every single other Weasley gets an on-screen appearance. The powers that be, though, decided to cut Charlie from the first movie, "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" (which retained the title "Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone" in most territories around the world), which means he also doesn't appear in the fourth film, "Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire," during another dragon-centric storyline where he pops up in the books.

As for James and Lily, if you look closely during a scene in "Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows — Part 1" where Harry and Hermione (Daniel Radcliffe and Emma Watson in the movies) go to visit Harry's parents' graves in Godric's Hollow, you can see their gravestone clarifies that they were both born in 1960. Canonically, the entire narrative of the Harry Potter books takes place in the 1990s. Harry himself was born in 1980, which means James and Lily were barely 20 years old when they had their son, but in the movies, they're seriously aged up in all the flashbacks. While this makes them look more like stock-footage parents, I guess, it's completely wrong when it comes to the actual text. Jasper Talbot and Hannah Chinn are both decidedly on the younger side, so it looks like the series will right this small wrong.

"Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone" starts airing on HBO and HBO Max on Christmas Day 2026.

#3713. These Sony Over-Ear Headphones Are Just $88 Right Now

Not everybody needs, wants, or can afford the "best" headphones available, especially when they can easily cost half a grand. If the $450+ Sony XM6s, which I called the best over-ear headphones of 2025 (and 2026 so far), are not in your budget, then perhaps the more affordable Sony WH-CH720Ns could be. While they're not quite as premium, they're significantly cheaper and offer good enough audio quality for most people.

Right now, you can get them for $88 (originally $179.99) on Amazon, their second-lowest price ever according to price tracking tools. Thopugh they've dropped below $100 before, all three colorways have not been available at their current price at the same time until now.

These are Sony's entry-level over-ear headphones, and the company mostly made cuts in the design and build. When you hold them, they might feel hollow and light, but they're still comfortable since the earcups are well-cushioned, as noted in CNET's review. However, Sony did not make as many sacrifices in the audio or ANC department, which, at the end of the day, is what's most important.

These headphones use the same Sony Sound Connect app as the flagship models and share some of the same features, like multipoint technology to connect to up to two devices. They also come with Google Fast Pair and Find My Device support, nice features to have for convenience, especially if you misplace them. You can get 35 hours of battery life with ANC on and 50 hours with ANC off, and the charging is quick, with three minutes giving you three hours of juice.

The ANC is not as good as the latest XM6, but it works great for under $100, according to Mashable's review, and it will block most ambient noise outdoors or indoors. Transparency mode is also great, allowing you to have conversations and hear bikes or cars passing by. The adjustable EQ in the app means you can tweak the crisp and vibrant sound of the WH-CH720N to your liking.

#3712. Hubbell (HUBB) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Hubbell Incorporated (HUBB -5.47%) reported a 25% increase in third quarter net sales, driven by demand for critical infrastructure and data center solutions. Management raised its full-year 2026 financial outlook for organic sales and earnings, citing outperformance in the Electrical Solutions segment and the successful integration of the NSI acquisition. The company reported that its order backlog has expanded by more than $500 million year to date, as utility customers plan new transmission and distribution projects to support load growth. Management indicated it is doubling its quarterly capital expenditure rate for capacity expansion to $50 million as it enters the next fiscal year to meet this visible demand.

After the speaker's presentation, there will be a question-and-answer session. To ask a question during the session, you will need to press star one on your telephone. To remove yourself from the queue, you may press star one again. I would now like to hand the call over to Dan Innamorato, Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

The press release and slides are posted at the investor section of our website at hubbell.com. I am joined today by our Chairman, President, and CEO, Gerben Bakker, and our CFO, Joe Capozzoli. Please note our comments this morning may include statements related to the expected future results of our company. They are forward-looking statements as defined by the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995. Please note the discussion of forward-looking statements in our press release and consider it incorporated by reference into this call.

Additionally, comments may also include non-GAAP financial measures. Those measures are reconciled to the comparable GAAP measures, which are included in the press release and slides. Now, let me turn the call over to Gerben.

Gerben Bakker: Good morning and thank you for joining us to discuss Hubbell's third quarter 2026 results. I will start my comments by summarizing three main themes we are seeing across the business in 2026. The first is that demand for Hubbell's critical infrastructure solutions remains strong. We see this clearly in our order book, year-to-date backlog build, and in long-term planning discussions with our utility and electrical end customers. Second, we are proactively investing to serve this strong demand. These investments have varying profiles across businesses, product lines, and factory footprint. Some areas, such as data center balance of system, electrical components, and certain transmission and substation product categories, are coming online quickly and are driving outperformance in 2026.

While some investment in these same high-growth markets will enable us to serve longer-term visible project demand in 2027 and beyond. What is consistent across the capital investment program as a whole is a highly attractive return profile accretive to our overall ROIC, which will enable us to grow our leading position in our core end markets over the next several years. Finally, we are managing effectively through a dynamic supply chain and inflationary environment. While broad-based inflation persists, we continue to aggressively drive price and productivity actions across our portfolio.

Our recent track record, our unique positions in our end markets, and our visibility on actions taken and in progress give us confidence in our ability to continue to manage the price cost productivity equation to neutral or better through the cycle. Looking more specifically at the third quarter, Hubbell delivered strong financial performance with double-digit growth in sales, adjusted operating profit, adjusted earnings per share, and free cash flow in the quarter, as well as on a year-to-date basis.

Third quarter performance was highlighted by 22% organic growth in Electrical Solutions, driven by data center sales increasing more than 80% in the quarter on an organic basis, as well as continued progress on our segment unification efforts and vertical market strategy. The NSI acquisition is off to a strong start, with performance and integration efforts trending ahead of our initial expectations. Utility Solutions results were solid in the quarter, with strong core T&D performance offsetting a temporary component availability headwind in our meters business. We are raising our full year 2026 outlook this morning, primarily driven by stronger growth outlook in the Electrical Solutions.

As we look ahead to 2027, we see an attractive growth outlook across our largest, highest margin end markets, and I will talk further about the preliminary views on next year at the end of this call. For now, let me turn the call over to Joe to provide some details on the third quarter.

Joe Capozzoli: Thank you, Gerben, and good morning, everyone. I am going to start my comments on slide four. Hubbell's overall financial performance was strong with double-digit growth across sales, adjusted operating profit, adjusted earnings per share, and free cash flow, both in the quarter and on a year-to-date basis. Net sales of $1.873 billion in the quarter increased by 25% as compared to the prior year. Organic growth of 11% was driven by robust 22% organic growth in Electrical Solutions and solid 5% organic growth in Utility Solutions. Acquisitions contributed 13% to growth in the quarter, with NSI off to a strong start in the electrical segment, as Gerben indicated, and DMC Power contributing nicely to our utility segment.

From an operational standpoint, Hubbell generated $477 million of adjusted operating profit in the third quarter, representing 33% growth versus the prior year, and delivered adjusted operating margins of 25.5%, representing 160 basis points of year-over-year expansion. Growth in adjusted operating profit was primarily driven by strong volume and high-margin businesses, as well as the impact of acquisitions, which were accretive to adjusted operating margins. Price cost productivity was approximately neutral on a dollar basis in the third quarter, including the net impact of IEEPA tariff refunds.

While broad-based cost inflation has persisted as 2026 has progressed, we have driven strong price and productivity realization over the last several quarters, and we are executing additional actions as we exit the year, which we are confident will position us well for 2027. Adjusted earnings per diluted share were $5.91 in the third quarter. This represented a 14% increase versus the prior year, driven by adjusted operating profit growth and a modestly lower share count as a result of prior share repurchase activity, partially offset by higher interest expense associated with the NSI and DMC Power acquisitions, and a higher year-over-year tax rate. Free cash flow of $280 million was up 10% relative to the prior year.

We continue to anticipate approximately 90% conversion of free cash flow to adjusted net income on a full year basis, which absorbs the impact from increased CapEx, acquisition costs and working capital investments to serve strong current and future sales growth. Our balance sheet remains healthy, and we remain committed to deleveraging over the next two years, which will drive incremental adjusted EPS accretion in 2027 and position our strong balance sheet for further accretive M&A investment over the next few years. Turning to page five to review our performance by segment.

Utility Solutions delivered 10% adjusted operating profit growth in the quarter and 9% sales growth, which was comprised of 5% organic growth and four points of contribution from the DMC Power acquisition. DMC Power's performance will be reflected in organic growth starting in the fourth quarter of 2026, which we anticipate will be accretive to the segment's organic growth rate. Grid Infrastructure grew 8% organically in the third quarter, led by strong growth in distribution and transmission markets. Substation sales also grew strongly in the quarter, but modestly below our prior expectation, driven by timing of capacity expansion in our substation control house business.

From a demand perspective, we continue to see broad-based order strength across substation, transmission, and distribution markets, leading to further backlog build in the quarter as utility customers plan new projects supporting load growth and continue to invest in hardening and resiliency initiatives. Grid Automation declined 4% organically in the third quarter, driven by a temporary component availability headwind in our meters business, which we anticipate will be resolved by the end of the fourth quarter and results in a shift of approximately $15 million in revenue from the third quarter into the fourth quarter.

Protection and Controls delivered strong double-digit growth in the quarter, led by continued strength in substation switching products, where project interconnecting load growth and recent capacity expansion investments are bolstering our performance. Operationally, Utility Solutions delivered $267 million of adjusted operating profit in the third quarter, representing 10% growth versus the prior year, with adjusted operating margins up 30 basis points year-over-year. Adjusted operating profit growth was primarily driven by strong volume growth in high-margin businesses and acquisitions, while higher cost inflation and raw material costs were largely offset by price realization, productivity, and a net tariff benefit in the quarter.

We also remain focused on executing and investing in capacity expansion initiatives to serve customer demand in high-growth areas of our utility business, which we are confident will drive strong organic growth and deliver attractive returns on shareholder capital, both in the near term and over the next several years. Moving to page six. Electrical Solutions results were very strong in the quarter. On the top line, Electrical Solutions generated net sales of $845 million, which represented growth of 51% versus the prior year and ahead of our prior expectation, driven by outperformance in both our organic business and in NSI.

Starting with NSI, as we highlighted at the time of the acquisition, this business is a strong strategic fit with our portfolio and enables us to double down on our attractive core of critical infrastructure components with complementary products and brands, similar channels to market, and clear opportunities for revenue and cost synergies. We are encouraged by the early integration efforts and strong performance, with NSI contributing 29 points to the Electrical Solutions segment revenue growth in the third quarter at accretive adjusted operating margins. From an organic revenue standpoint, 22% organic revenue growth was again highlighted by strength in data center and light industrial markets.

Data center sales increased over 80% on an organic basis in the third quarter as content gains, new product introductions, and capacity additions drove outgrowth in a strong underlying market. Most notably, our wiring device and pin and sleeve product lines have driven accelerated data center penetration through our vertical market strategy, and recent product innovations and recent capacity investments in these areas are enabling us to capitalize on strong customer demand. Outside of data center, light industrial markets remain strong, bolstered by U.S. manufacturing activity, and electric T&D and renewables markets also grew strongly in the quarter. Non-residential markets were solid in the quarter, while heavy industrial markets remained relatively soft.

Across our HES portfolio, our initiatives around cross-selling and channel conversions have driven broad-based commercial success, and we see further opportunities to accelerate this strategy through the integration of NSI. Operationally, Electrical Solutions delivered $210 million of adjusted operating profit in the third quarter, representing 80% growth versus the prior year, an adjusted operating margin of 24.8% expanded 400 basis points year-over-year. Adjusted operating profit growth was driven by strong volume growth and the NSI acquisition, while higher cost inflation and raw material costs were largely offset by price realization, productivity, and a net tariff benefit in the quarter. Turning to page seven to discuss our full-year outlook.

We are raising our 2026 outlook this morning, driven by stronger sales growth and adjusted operating profit growth. On sales, we are raising our growth outlook to +18% to 19%, reflecting an increase in organic growth expectations to 10%-11% and stronger NSI contribution now expected to contribute approximately 7.5% to full-year sales growth. Our increased organic growth expectations are driven by a stronger outlook in Electrical Solutions, which we now expect to grow 17%-18% organically in 2026, while we have slightly lowered our outlook in Utility Solutions to +6% to 7% on a full-year basis. Broadly, orders and end market demands have exceeded our expectations across our largest end markets in both segments as 2026 has progressed.

The timing of project deployments and internal capacity expansions is driving stronger relative growth within Electrical Solutions segment near term. Operationally, we anticipate over 20% year-over-year growth in adjusted operating profit on a full-year basis at the midpoint of our outlook range, an increase versus our prior expectation, driven largely by stronger volume growth, while the impact of incremental cost inflation, price, and productivity actions result in an anticipated adjusted operating margin range of 23.0%-23.2% for the full year.

Our expectations for below-the-line items remain unchanged, and we continue to anticipate approximately 90% free cash flow conversion on adjusted net income on a full year basis, which includes the impact of elevated levels of inflation, increased year-over-year capital investment, and NSI acquisition costs. We are raising our full-year outlook for adjusted earnings per share to a range of $20.40-$20.60. We are confident in our ability to deliver on this increased outlook in 2026, which represents year-over-year earnings growth of 12%-13%. Now let me turn the call back over to Gerben to give some preliminary views on our attractive 2027 setup.

Gerben Bakker: Thanks, Joe. We see an attractive end market setup for Hubbell in 2027. Our standing long-term outlook targets mid-single-digit organic growth through the cycle. As we have indicated throughout this year, everything we see in our end markets, order trends, and conversations with customers has given us incremental confidence in the strength and duration of the long-term investment cycle ahead of us. Given the shorter cycle nature of many of our products, we intend to remain conservative as we update both our annual and long-term growth targets next year. We will be exiting 2026 with stronger than typical backlog visibility, and we are confident in our ability to deliver an attractive year of organic growth in 2027.

In Utility Solutions, we continue to see broad-based strength across distribution, substation, and transmission markets. Orders throughout 2026 have continued to exceed our initial expectations, with particular strength in transmission and substation project activity driving backlog build in excess of $500 million year to date. We continue to believe utility T&D markets are in the early stages of an attractive multi-year investment cycle, driven by aging infrastructure and bolstered by load growth and data center interconnections. We have strong specified positions with our end customers, along with leading brands and installed base, and our primary focus as a management team is on executing the capacity expansion required to serve this broad-based demand for our utility customers.

In our smaller end markets, we anticipate more modest growth in the approximately 20% of our portfolio exposed to gas, telecoms, and meters and AMI. More broadly, conversations with distributor partners on large project activities have been constructive as electrification, U.S. reshoring, and data center build-outs drive tailwinds across the broader industrial economy.

We also anticipate continued strength in electric T&D and renewable markets in 2027, while our expectations for non-residential and heavy industrial remain more modest. If you break that down, the components actually grew even better. This is stuff like connectors and bushings and arresters and switches, and that was in excess of 20% growth. We were expecting stronger growth in those control houses, going from the first half to the second half. This is a business that's grown very attractively over the last couple of years. If you look at those two events, it's really what caused us to pull back a little bit on the organic growth this year. But I would say it's a really solid setup for next year as we continue to add capacity to our portfolio.

You can only keep adding so many machines before you run out of roof line. Our Systems Control business is one of those that we're doing that as well. So those are primarily the areas how we're adding capacity into our business. Tommy Moll: Gerben, I wanted to start with a question on your Electrical Solutions margin approaching 25% for the quarter. You did call out NSI integration as ahead of plan, so that's got to be part of it. If you pull that away, what other factors were favorable this quarter? As we think toward next year, before we all put 25% in our model for 2027, is there any temporary benefit you had this quarter that we should be mindful may fall away going forward? Thank you.

Joe Capozzoli: Yeah. Good morning, Tommy. I would say the anchor of that margin performance in the quarter was strong organic volume contribution, and that strong volume has come in key product lines, high margin product lines that are serving data center, in addition to the contribution, the nice contribution we were anticipating from NSI. Certainly, third quarter is seasonally the strongest revenue and margin performance quarter of the year for us. We're very pleased with the margin performance of electrical in the quarter, but certainly this isn't the ongoing run rate performance. It certainly is the seasonal performance. But we do continue to see strong margin in electrical.

Tommy, we've talked about this long-term margin expansion program that Mark Mikes and the electrical segment team have been driving, which is really multifaceted and includes the segment unification strategy, our go-to-market strategy with our broad Electrical Solutions-focused commercial team. We've talked about portfolio management, we've talked about business simplification and management delayering and consolidation, new product intros in key areas. So a lot of variables driving this long-term margin expansion program through electrical, and we really see us in the middle innings and continuing that margin expansion program.

So while you are seeing a real strong third quarter margin, we think about it more broadly that the Electrical Solutions segment will continue to drive margin expansion in 2027 and beyond for all those reasons.

Tommy Moll: Thank you, Joe. As a follow-up, I wanted to ask about the capacity adds, particularly in your transmission and substation businesses. Noted that it should unlock some revenue for you into next year. Assuming that occurs, are you in that case fully meeting the demand, or should we still think of this as a capacity-constrained market?

Gerben Bakker: Yeah, I would say this is not taking the form of you add a huge plant, you have an incremental step, and then you're done, or you're planning for multi years out. Some areas of this market, if you think about some of the other equipment, that's exactly how you have to plan capacity adds. For us, still the capacity adds are incremental, even when we do roof line extensions. It's a question of staying ahead of it. Certainly the capacity that we're adding next year isn't to solve for multi-year. We're going to have to continue to add capacity in 2027 for 2028 and 2028 for 2029.

We view that actually as a good thing because it limits you a little bit from getting too far ahead of yourself. I would see it in that kind of construct of how we're thinking about capacity adds multi-year.

Tommy Moll: Thank you, Gerben. I appreciate it, and I'll turn it back.

Operator: Thank you. Once again, to ask a question, please press star one on your telephone. Our next question comes from the line of Alexander Virgo of Evercore ISI. Your line is open, Alexander.

Alexander Virgo: Great. Thanks very much, gentlemen. Good morning. I wondered if you could just talk a little bit about two things for me. The first one, the $15 million in Grid Automation. If I adjust that back, it looks like automation was kind of flat year-on-year instead of building on something of the momentum that you saw in Q2 against an easier comp. So I'm just wondering why we should be feeling better about that as we go forward into next year. And then secondly, the data center business, obviously very strong, and your comments on backlog are helpful, and the 2027 sort of initial view is helpful.

But obviously a dark green arrow masks a lot of potential growth next year. So I wondered if you could just give us a little bit of a hand on what that might end up looking like in HES, again, in the interest of putting a sensible sort of number into the model to start with. Thank you.

Joe Capozzoli: Good morning, Alexander. So yeah, I'll start with your first question. That's a $15 million flip in Grid Automation and normalizing 3Q, 4Q. You're thinking about that the right way. And that $15 million flip would have been very consistent with our expectation for the back half 90 days ago. So just that movement from 3Q to 4Q normalizes the expectation of Grid Auto that would have been positive in the third quarter. And we're anticipating now with that $15 million falling into the fourth quarter, that we see double-digit growth in Grid Auto in the fourth quarter because of it. The second question on the data center business and the backlog.

Certainly, data center's been really strong for our electrical business, and we're anticipating that we exit the year with about 30%, sorry, 15% of our electrical segment revenues tied to data center. We will provide more color and insight on our 2027 guidance on data center come January. At this point, we are anticipating that data center continues to grow at very attractive levels in 2027 off of a higher base for us as we exit this year.

Operator: I would now like to turn the conference back to Dan Innamorato for closing remarks. Sir?

Dan Innamorato: Great. Thanks, everybody, for joining us. I will be around all day for follow-up calls.

Operator: This concludes today's conference call. Thank you for participating. You may now disconnect.

#3711. The $15,000 (Or $15 Million Bird) Is Way, Way In Demand After Kelly Clarkson Drama

Clarkson isn't the only one in the market for such a bird.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The saga involving Kelly Clarkson and Brooklyn-based novelty shop Art of Play has taken some interesting turns, as it’s played out in recent weeks. It all began when the singer entered the establishment and reportedly wanted to buy a singing bird automaton. Clarkson then took to social media to say that she was denied the chance to purchase the bird, though management later refuted that claim, saying the bird wasn’t for sale. That aside, though, it seems the bird’s original artist, Bliss Kolb, is now receiving a lot of requests.

Clarkson specifically wanted to buy an animatronic bird named Molly and was taken aback when she was allegedly treated as though she couldn’t afford it. While store owner Adam Rubin and co., said the bird was exclusively used at Art of Play for events, Clarkson responded by sharing a photo of the bird with a $15,000 price tag (which has been swapped to $15 million as a joke by the staff). The Wall Street Journal now reports Kolb has received roughly 100 commissions since Clarkson visited AoP on October 10.

What’s ironic is that this all comes at a time at which Kolb – who’s based in Seattle – had planned to cut down on the number of requests. However, he may be looking to create a few more of the singing automatons down the road. If that happens, the items could reportedly range between $4,000 and $15,000. Kolb seems to be taking the hype all in stride, however and, when she spoke to WSJ, she said she appreciated that Kelly Clarkson “liked” her work. This represents a more positive side of a story that’s seemingly had negative implications.

After Clarkson’s visit to Art of Play, security footage was obtained by Pablo Torre (who bought a second automaton) and provided a better look at how things went down. The “Breakaway” performer seemed displeased with her interactions with the store’s staff at the time. Clarkson subsequently asserted that she’d been treated like Julia Roberts’ character in Pretty Woman and, since then, a number of fans and celebrities have come to her defense. Per reports, AoP has been feeling the effects of that support in a not-so-positive way.

The Unintentionally Perfect Bird Post Shared By Brooklyn Store Weeks Before Kelly Clarkson Showed Up

Sources have alleged that since American Idol’s inaugural winner came to the shop, AoP has been lambasted by Clarkson fans. It’s also been said that amid the social media “hatred,” Adam Rubin has reportedly been trying to get in touch with Clarkson to talk things out. At the same time, some locals have been defending the store and have been met with backlash on social media as well.

Those within Clarkson’s inner circle claim she’s “happy” amid the blowback and “doesn’t think she did anything wrong.” On top of that, it’s been claimed that Clarkson is “living in a bubble of applause” because no one is apparently disputing the notion of her having done anything incorrect. Still, other insiders claim people close to Clarkson are surprised by how this situation has escalated.

It’s fair to say Bliss Kolb finds herself in a very interesting position, given that her work is at the center of this back-and-forth between Kelly Clarkson and Art of Play. Whether Kolb will actually produce more of those bird automatons is unclear, but it seems she stands to make a sizable amount of money if she can fulfill some requests.

#3710. Turnstile Pack Washington Mall for Palestine, Sudan, and Lebanon Benefit Show

Turnstile took over the National Mall in Washington, D.C. on October 24 with a massive free show raising funds for Human Concern International’s relief efforts in Palestine, Sudan, and Lebanon. The benefit concert concluded Turnstile’s second North American tour in support of their Grammy-winning album Never Enough. Check out footage from the show—and a full set list—below.

Per the Baltimore Banner, the first two fans in line for the 3 p.m. show arrived at 5 a.m. All told, the National Park Service estimated around 60,000 people were in the crowd. Turnstile aimed to raise $150,000 for Human Concern International; as of this writing, the show’s official LaunchGood page has raised over $250,000.

In the middle of the set, frontman Brendan Yates thanked the crowd for showing out and supporting their cause: “We’re so grateful to have been raised by this scene, one that taught us that music not only reflects the artist making it but the people and the world and the suffering and the joy that surrounds it.” He concluded by leading a chant: “Loud enough for the White House to hear it: Free Palestine! Free Sudan! Free Lebanon!”

The Washington benefit show marked Turnstile’s biggest yet, but it’s not the first time they’ve played free of charge for a good cause. In 2025, the Baltimore band hosted a hometown set in Wyman Park Dell to raise money for the nonprofit Health Care for the Homeless, a Maryland nonprofit that provides medical care and housing assistance for people experiencing homelessness. They funded the production out of pocket, and the show raised over $35,000 for the organization.

The Saturday show only added to the list of milestones Turnstile has accomplished this year. In February, the band won their first two Grammy Awards, for Best Rock Album (for Never Enough) and Best Metal Performance (for “Birds”). They also made history that night as the first band to earn nominations across the Rock, Metal, and Alternative music categories. In August, they released an album of Never Enough remixes, with features from Elton John, Panda Bear, Hayley Williams, Oklou, Slayyyter, Chanel Beads, and more. Earlier month, they made their Saturday Night Live debut, becoming the first hardcore group to play Studio 8H since 1981.

However, the yera has not been without its trials. In March, the band’s former guitarist Brady Ebert was arrested for attempted murder after allegedly hitting Yates’ father with his car. William Yates was hospitalized after suffering “severe physical trauma” to his legs in the alleged attack, the band said in a statement to Pitchfork. The band parted ways with Ebert in 2022, noting in their statement that this decision was made "in response to a consistent pattern of harmful behavior affecting himself, the band, and the community.” The statement concluded, ”We have no language left for Brady.”

Although Turnstile’s ’s Washington Monument performance concluded the Never Enough tour, they already have a few forthcoming festival dates lined up. They’ll play Tyler, the Creator’s Camp Flog Gnaw Carnival in November, and have also been confirmed to Primavera Sound’s 2027 bill.

Learn all about Turnstile’s rise in Lawrence Burney’s cover story Turnstile Blew Up. Now What?

#3709. Laika and Fathom Take Legal Action to Stop ‘Wildwood’ Screenings at L.A.’s Bruin Theatre

L.A.’s historic Bruin Theatre met its ambitious fall reopening target on Friday, October 23, but its return is already facing a challenge from the companies behind its first attraction. Laika and Fathom Entertainment say they have taken legal action to stop the Bruin from showing “Wildwood.”

The film was previously advertised as a week-long run at the newly reopened Westwood venue. But in a joint statement provided exclusively to IndieWire, the animation studio and its theatrical distribution partner said concerns about operator Rui Pereira‘s history of controversial comments and behavior prompted them to seek an end to screenings. Related Stories Luchino Visconti’s Tragic, Beautiful Final Film ‘L’Innocente’ Restored in 4K — Watch the Trailer Watch ‘My Undesirable Friends’ Director Julia Loktev Recall the Week Russia Invaded Ukraine

“When Laika became aware of the Bruin engagement, we immediately raised concerns with Fathom Entertainment, our theatrical distribution partner. Laika and Fathom commenced legal action to end the film’s screenings at the theatre,” the companies said in an email.

“We have not yet received confirmation that the screenings have stopped. We are continuing to explore our options, as the concerns regarding the theatre operator are incompatible with the values of our two companies.” IndieWire independently confirmed that “Wildwood” screenings had continued at the Bruin through at least Sunday afternoon, October 25, and as of publication, the theater’s website still advertised daily screenings at 1:30 p.m., 4 p.m., 7 p.m., and 9:30 p.m.

Laika and Fathom Entertainment did not specify what kind of legal action they had taken to stop showings of their film. On a phone call with IndieWire, Pereira asked how we had obtained a cease-and-desist letter. IndieWire had neither received nor reviewed such a letter and told Pereira we had communicated directly with the companies. He continued, “You guys are assholes. Everything you have written is false.”

The dispute introduces an immediate programming problem for Pereira’s revived movie palace, which reopened with “Wildwood” after sitting dark since Regency Theatres departed the space in July 2024. It also follows months of scrutiny involving Pereira, whose rocky history operating Toronto’s Kingsway Theatre became the subject of an IndieWire investigation on September 3.

IndieWire’s earlier reporting examined allegations of assault, nonpayment, racial discrimination, online harassment, and unauthorized film exhibition. The reporting drew on interviews with more than 25 sources in the U.S. and Canada, as well as court records, digital correspondence, social media posts, and years of advertised Kingsway screenings some rights holders say they did not authorize. Pereira has broadly disputed those allegations and characterized the ongoing scrutiny of his businesses as an effort to undermine his work as an exhibitor.

“There are no controversies and any attempt to generate them is dishonest and disingenuous,” Pereira said then. “The Bruin Theater is a gift, not only to Los Angeles, but the world — a cultural heritage site that unites generations of movie goers in the experience of cinema.”

Several journalists have described being targeted by Pereira after publishing critical coverage or omitting the Kingsway from their reporting. Speaking with IndieWire on the phone and in a subsequent email on October 26, Pereira referred to IndieWire staff as “presstitutes” and accused the publication of getting paid to write about him negatively.

Two volunteers left the Bruin restoration project in August after posts attributed to the Kingsway account circulated among Los Angeles moviegoers. Screenshots reviewed by IndieWire included racist and antisemitic remarks, as well as exchanges targeting individual patrons. Sources also described threats and a pattern of prolonged retaliation, alleging that Pereira continued targeting some critics online for months or years after their initial disputes.

When asked by The Hollywood Reporter in an August 23 report about some specific Kingsway posts — including one telling a target to “Go back to Poland” and another describing a swastika painted on a Jewish family’s home as “the proper symbol for zionazis” — Pereira acknowledged that the comments were “offensive” and said he “must take responsibility.”

The current dispute over “Wildwood” also follows separate questions about the Kingsway’s programming. IndieWire previously identified 55 advertised screenings of 17 films that three rights holders said they had not authorized at Pereira’s Toronto theater. Those findings came from a limited sample and did not establish that all or even most Kingsway screenings were unlicensed. “There are no issues with film licensing or any disputes,” Pereira said previously.

Notably, Laika and Fathom’s statement does not suggest that the Bruin’s “Wildwood” booking was unauthorized from the outset. Rather, the companies say they sought to end the Bruin screenings over specific concerns about Pereira as operator. “Wildwood” opened in nearly 3,000 theaters nationwide, and a booking on that scale is hardly an endorsement of every exhibitor.

Still, independent theaters often build their identities around the taste and reputations of the people running them. At a beloved landmark like the Bruin, affection for an old movie palace can translate into intense trust in whoever promises to save it. For now, the location has achieved the reopening its new operator promised. Whether it can continue showing the film that brought audiences back is in dispute.

#3708. Dallas Cowboys Cheerleaders Win Legal War With My Chemical Romance Over Fake Uniforms

The Dallas Cowboys cheerleaders have something new to cheer about ... they've scored a legal W against My Chemical Romance.

TMZ has learned DCC and MCR reached an agreement ... and it's a clear win for the famous cheer squad ... because the emo band agreed not to dress their cheerleaders up like the DCC for their upcoming concerts.

MCR can still do their act with cheerleaders dancing on stage ... they just can't make them look like DCC hotties, which they'd been doing before the company that owns the DCC brand -- Pro Silver Star -- had their legal team file for an injunction against MCR.

MCR's dancers do wave pom-poms, but then pick up prop guns to pretend to protect the band with a fake firefight ... before ultimately getting shot and crawling offstage.

There was an injunction filed Friday to get a judge to order the band to stop dressing dancers in the DCC uniforms, claiming it could cause "irreparable harm" to their brand.

But an agreement was reached before a court hearing ... MCR folded and DCC won big.

#3707. The Child-Actor Conundrum That’s Plagued Hollywood Forever—and How to Fix It (Maybe)

“No matter how good the union safeguards get, no matter the policing of grooming behavior, none of it will change the fact that acting while so young strips us of our childhoods and leaves us to suffer the consequences—alone.”

Alison Pill had wanted to express this message for decades. But she didn’t publicly articulate it until this summer—less than two weeks after Hayden Panettiere, who shot her first commercial before she celebrated her first birthday, died of an accidental overdose at 36. Months after Daveigh Chase, star of The Ring, died at 35 from AIDS complications following a long period of homelessness. A year and a half after Harriet the Spy star Michelle Trachtenberg died at 39 due to diabetes complications, though the full story may be more complicated.

Pill laid out her blunt assessment in a New York Times op-ed headlined “I Was a Child Actor. No One Deserves That Fate.” The conclusion she reached was stark: “The greatest storytellers should find a way to capture the world we live in without child actors, for all our sakes.”

“It took me a while to figure out what, exactly, I wanted to say,” Pill tells Vanity Fair. Once she did, though, she made her point simply and elegantly: that it’s basically impossible to pay a child to act without exploiting them in some way, even unintentionally. That kids who are set up to succeed—kids with loving parents, financial security, on-set support systems, and a burning and internally motivated desire to perform—still grow up to become adults who feel they were taken advantage of and not protected in the way they should have been.

Pill counts herself among that group. “My parents are wonderful people. I didn’t have my money stolen,” she says. Yet in the years since her own child-acting days—when she played a young Lorna Luft in a celebrated Judy Garland miniseries, and Katie Holmes’s little sister in Pieces of April, and Lindsay Lohan’s best friend in Confessions of a Teenage Drama Queen—she’s grown increasingly uncomfortable with the realities of what it means to put children onscreen. Especially now that she’s both a mother in real life and often playing one in her work. “If we can’t protect the most innocent of innocents in any meaningful way, or even attempt to,” says Pill, “what are we doing?”

I spoke with five other former child actors for this story—and while not all of them take the same hard-line position that Pill does, everyone in the group tends to agree on two things.

The first is that acting in a professional setting inherently hurts kids. “There will 100% be consequences to a child in an adultified, high-stress, and high-stakes industry—both in terms of the potential harm to that kid and just the developmental loss,” says Alex Winter, who made his Broadway debut at the age of 12. “That’s just a given. You cannot develop normally as a child if you are a working child.” Mara Wilson, of Mrs. Doubtfire and Matilda fame, puts a finer point on it: “I think that you could probably be a somewhat healthy child actor. I do not think you could be a healthy child star.”

The second is that there’s more to be done to keep those kids safe—simple, concrete changes productions could enact now. “I think we could protect children without necessarily taking away their opportunities,” says Who’s the Boss? alum Alyssa Milano.

“There’s this mysticism surrounding what there is to be done, and there’s a lot of theories and a lot of outside opinion,” former Disney star Christy Carlson Romano adds. “And I think this is an industry issue that needs to be handled effectively inside.”

“I have been asked many times which of my pictures I liked best,” Charlie Chaplin wrote in the pages of this magazine in 1921. “Perhaps the one I most enjoyed making is my last picture, The Kid, written expressly to bring out the qualities of another actor, a five-year-old actor this time, Jack Coogan.” Chaplin said he was charmed by Coogan from the moment they met: “He was with his parents in a Los Angeles hotel, sleeping, as a child will, in a chair. He was roused in order to meet me. He rubbed his eyes, jumped up, made his politest bow, and promptly went back to sleep.”

So Chaplin built an entire feature around that sleepy little boy, thus launching the career of the kid generally agreed to be Hollywood’s very first bona fide child star. Coogan would inadvertently set the template for those following in his wake.

Long before adolescence, Coogan earned millions from his films and the merch that bore his face. But after aging out of cute-kid roles and flunking out of college, he learned that his profligate parents had squandered his fortune. His legacy is a litany of stories with headlines like “The Very Sad Story of Jackie Coogan”—and California’s Coogan Law, which requires employers to deposit 15% of a minor’s gross wages into a trust account that only the child can access when they turn 18. Coogan also played Uncle Fester in the original Addams Family, making a triumphant return to the screen as an adult—older and wiser.

Child actors aren’t a monolith. Some, like Coogan, end up traumatized by their time in the industry. Others have overwhelmingly positive experiences before growing into highly functioning adults. Many fall somewhere in the middle. Armchair critics have a proclivity to focus on the negative outcomes, which “tends to stigmatize the business in a way that isn’t particularly helpful for anyone,” says Winter, who’s spoken openly about the sexual abuse he survived as a child actor. As an adult, says Wilson, “I see a lot of people making the assumption that any child who was in the industry must have been abused on sets.”

Since his Bill & Ted days, Winter has largely traded acting for directing, working frequently on TV series for kids. And according to him, those sets are generally much healthier places to spend time than they once were. “Despite the MAGA discourse that they’re all pedophiles—which is really, really not true—most people who go into that environment are family-oriented by nature,” he says. “The days of the really crass, bullish, patriarchal, male-run sets of the ’70s and ’80s into the early ’90s—that is really a bygone era. You would not get away with being that person today.”

Alyssa Milano and Tony Danza, Who's the Boss?, 1985.

In some cases, growing up on a set might actually be better for a kid’s well-being than the alternative. “I was dyslexic,” Milano says. “I don’t know that I would’ve been okay in a normal classroom, under normal social structures.” She points out that performing is one of the few ways that a talented but underprivileged kid can earn enough money to change their circumstances.

Yet for every kid actor who becomes a successful and seemingly well-adjusted adult, every Daniel Radcliffe or Drew Barrymore, there seems to be a River Phoenix: a bright light snuffed out too soon, a cautionary tale. And even the Radcliffes and Barrymores hardly seem to emerge into adulthood unscathed.

So yes, it’s hard to know definitively whether child actors have worse adult outcomes than any other group. “There’s this spectrum of experience,” says Romano, “so there’s no clarity.” But what we know for sure is that, especially in the post-MeToo era, former child actors who did have negative experiences have been speaking more publicly about them: in pieces like Pill’s, in documentaries like ID’s Quiet on Set and Winter’s own Showbiz Kids, in blockbuster books like Jennette McCurdy’s I’m Glad My Mom Died and Alyson Stoner’s Semi-Well-Adjusted Despite Literally Everything. (Via reps, both McCurdy and Stoner politely declined interview requests.) Romano recently released her own memoir about her Disney days, titled Once Upon a Trainwreck: The Fall and Rise of a Child Star.

These onetime kid actors have spoken movingly about facing isolation, external pressure, intense public scrutiny, and the psychic pain of constant rejection on the audition circuit. In an article Romano published after Panettiere’s death, she compared early fame to CTE: “Not as a diagnosis,” she wrote. “As a way of thinking about repeated impact.”

Alex Winter and Keanu Reeves in Bill & Ted's Excellent Adventure, 1989.

It seems high time, then, that the entertainment industry followed the NFL’s lead by enacting stricter metaphorical concussion protocols. Nobody I spoke to believes that banning child actors altogether is realistic; everyone similarly agrees that, say, replacing actual kids with AI-generated ones isn’t the answer. “I know that’ll be the argument: What if instead of adding staff to a production and considering the human element of any of this, we just AI them? I think it’s depraved,” says Pill.

Even Bryn Mooser, the cofounder of Asteria—a company that brands itself as Hollywood’s ethical AI studio—is with them. “Replacing kids might help alleviate having less kids on set,” he says, “but it’s certainly not going to solve another massive problem, which is that people don’t want virtual actors in a movie.” And though Mooser allows that de-aging technology has gotten vastly more sophisticated than it once ws, he maintains that “it’s so much easier to have a child playing a child. You know what I’m saying?”

This informal conclave suggests more practical solutions. Wilson proposes taking the Coogan Law further—ensuring that more than 15% of a child’s earnings will be secured for the child—and making sure it’s enacted nationally. Romano wants to see more investment in the Entertainment Community Fund’s Looking Ahead program—which offers trainings, resources, and support to young actors—as well as a formal study that can actually determine how many former child actors are struggling, and in which ways. Milano would like to see action from SAG-AFTRA: “I think the unions could do a better job of training our young performers about what it means to have self-care and to protect themselves.” More than one source notes that the union might have a vested interest in this issue particularly because its current president, Sean Astin, is a former child actor himself.

Pill suggests what might be the most radical idea: that sets where kids are working should hire designated child welfare advocates. They would operate sort of like intimacy coordinators do, acting as a buffer between the child actor, the rest of the production, and, if necessary, the child’s parent or guardian—who “must be present at all times while a minor is working,” according to SAG-AFTRA policy.

Christy Carlson Romano attending The Kid premier in 2000.

It’s true that film staffers have unofficially served functions like this one in the past. “On the film sets I was on, I had studio teachers who basically acted like that,” says Wilson—referring to the educators who are required to be employed on any set where a minor is working for at least three consecutive days, according to SAG-AFTRA policy. But there’s a difference between ad hoc advocacy and the kind that’s mandatory. Especially when it comes to studio teachers, who “are easily corrupted, in my opinion,” says Romano, “because they are the liaison between production and the child.”

Few people understand this better than Maggie Blake. She too was once a child actor, appearing in ’90s and ’00s-era Canadian productions like Emily of New Moon. She now works as a designated child safety and well-being consultant on film and TV sets. “When I was a kid, I had a therapist on set—but I didn’t know who she was,” says Blake. “She was a random lady in the corner. And I don’t want to be that random lady.”

Her ethos is straightforward: “I want to prepare the production for the child, rather than expecting the child to adapt to an adult system,” Blake says. “So I’m there talking to ADs and producers and directors, saying, ‘How are we going to support this child?’ I want to meet them before they come to set and understand their developmental needs. I want to talk to their parents and caregivers and understand where they’re at, then build a relationship.”

Blake helps prepare kids emotionally to perform violent or mature material. She speaks out when her charges are too tired during a night shoot to do their best work. She counsels young performers who are despondent after losing out on a part. She works with their parents too, urging them to release the pressure valve.

For an industry that’s already forced to navigate competing union interests, corporate headwinds, and endless miles of red tape, adding another layer of compliance may seem impossible. To the people who think that way, “I would say, ‘Risk mitigation, risk mitigation, risk mitigation,’” says Blake. “We have intimacy coordinators. We have stunt directors for adults. And yet the most vulnerable population that is working on sets has no one.” Intimacy and stunt coordinators aren’t infallible, of course—but most would agree they’re better than nothing.

Mara Wilson and Robin Williams, Mrs. Doubtfire, 1993.

Like almost all of the former child actors in this story, Blake is a parent herself. Like most of them, she will not let her own kids act professionally. “I think that it’s telling that there aren’t very many second-generation child actors,” says Wilson—who doesn’t have kids, but wouldn’t want them to act in a professional capacity if she did. Most child actors whose parents were also child actors, she points out, “tend to be in projects with their families.”

Milano’s daughter, 12-year-old Elizabella, is one of them. This summer, the two of them shot a film together in Canada.

“Before she was born, I was like, Oh, what am I going to do if I want a kid in this business? But then after she was born, I realized she came out who she is,” Milano says. Former child actors can recognize the type. “There are kids who really want to be onstage, performing and taking dancing and singing lessons, and that’s who they are,” says Winter. “That’s absolutely who I was, and my parents kind of had to get out of the way.”

But Milano didn’t. She was close by as her daughter filmed on what sounds like a particularly nurturing set. “Bella had a social worker…and there was someone there making sure she had her breaks, and was comfortable, and didn’t work longer than the hours that she was supposed to work,” she says.

When asked how it felt to work with Bella, her mother’s voice turns dreamy. “It was amazing,” Milano says. “My most memorable moment on set—I think ever in my entire career—was watching them shoot her close up from the monitor. It was really beautiful.”

A Deep Dive Into How Larry Ellison Built His Influence

#3706. Taika Waititi's Sci-Fi Movie Starring Jenna Ortega Flopped At The Box Office

Taika Waititi is an Oscar winner and a director who has delivered some beloved movies over the years. Unfortunately, his latest effort is going to go down as a box office flop. Starring Jenna Ortega, "Klara and the Sun" adapts some treasured sci-fi source material but something was evidently lost in translation.

Released by Sony Pictures over the weekend, "Klara and the Sun" opened to just $725,000 on 880 screens, giving it a lousy $824 per-screen average. It debuted well outside of the top ten and has little upside to speak of. Meanwhile, "Clayface" topped the box office and firmed up the future for James Gunn's DC Universe with a $24 million domestic debut. That, along with the unexpected word-of-mouth success of A24's "You Can See Everything," overshadowed Waititi's latest. It's dead on arrival.

To make matters worse, it holds a very poor 25% critical approval rating on Rotten Tomatoes. That means word of mouth is likely going to be bad if not nonexistent.

The movie is based on the 2021 book of the same name by Kazuo Ishiguro. It centers on Klara (Ortega), an Artificial Friend who meets Josie (Mia Tharia). Each immediately senses a kindred spirit in the other. Klara's innocent wonder and unwavering loyalty begin to heal the family and bring light to Josie's complicated world.

This makes two misfires in a row for Waititi. "Next Goal Wins" was a box office bomb in 2023 when it hit theaters, taking in less than $19 million worldwide on a $14 million budget. "Klara" reportedly carries a similarly small $20 million budget. A lower budget offers some security as that money can be made up on VOD/streaming, but it doesn't bode very well for Waititi as a commercial director in Hollywood.

Taika Waititi was on top of the world for a while. In 2017, he helped to save one of Marvel's biggest franchises. "Thor: The Dark World" set a low bar for the Marvel Cinematic Universe but his reinvention of the franchise in 2017 with "Thor: Ragnarok" completely changed the game. It made more than $850 million worldwide and was a huge hit, one that remains one of the most roundly-acclaimed MCU movies to date. That made Waititi an A-list filmmaker, but repeating that success has been far more difficult in recent years.

"Jojo Rabbit" netted Waititi a win at the 2020 Oscars for his work on the screenplay while also earning nearly $90 million on a $14 million budget. While 2022's "Thor: Love and Thunder" took in over $760 million globally, it was a step down critically and commercially compared to "Ragnarok." That was the start of the slide.

"Next Goal Wins" and "Klara and the Sun" makes it two disappointments in a row for Waititi. It doesn't help that he directed episodes of "Time Bandits" in addition to serving as an executive producer on that show, which was canceled after just one season. He's seemingly lost the juice.

Looking at it on paper, it appears as though Waititi is on the verge of winding up in director's jail, if he's not there already. Waititi was previously unsure if his "Star Wars" movie will get made. It almost surely won't now. He needs to set up a sure thing and get back to movies like "What We Do in the Shadows" that helped establish him as a voice in the business in the first place.
