#3705. Inside Pregnant Savannah Chrisley's Family: Her Fiancé Jason & More

The Chrisley family is about to get a little bigger. Not only did Savannah Chrisley recently announce that she's engaged to a man named Jason, but she also shared that they're expecting a baby.

They say mother knows best, and Chrisley Knows Best’s Savannah Chrisley is about to find out if that’s true.

Because the reality star recently revealed she’s expecting a baby with her fiancé Jason. Yes, fiancé as Savannah, in addition to sharing her expanding family, announced she's engaged.

"We are unbelievably excited and cannot wait for this baby to get here," she shared on the Oct. 27 episode of her Unlocked podcast, also posting a video of herself cradling her baby bump, holding the sonogram and kissing her partner on Instagram. “I am almost 21 weeks, which is absolutely insane.”

For months, Savannah had been teasing a romance but never disclosed her mystery man’s identity. And while followers still don’t know Jason’s last name, the Growing Up Chrisley alum did share some insights on their love story.

The couple began dating in July 2025 following the end of her two-year relationship with Robert Shiver, with Savannah noting she met Jason through the “world of politics.”

“I went through a really sad breakup, and I wasn’t expecting to meet someone," she recalled. “And then I met someone right in the middle of my heartache and heartbreak and confusion. So, I didn’t know what to do. I just knew I felt this insane attraction and emotional poll to someone.”

A poll so strong that Jason ended up giving Savannah a ring five months later while they were on a trip to the Cayman Islands.“When he gave me the ring, he was like, ‘Before I open this, I just want you to know that this is just an expression of love to you. This can be whatever you want it to be: This can be an engagement ring, a promise ring, a cocktail ring for all I care. I just want you to know how much I love you,’” she continued. “And there was no pressure ever put on me, ‘You have to accept this as this.’”

While Todd Chrisley and Julie Chrisley's daughter said yes, she and Jason decided to keep their engagement private—partly because she noted “there is a huge age gap” between them.

“It’s just such an amazing, fulfilling feeling to finally find your person, and I’m there,” the 29-year-old added. “So, yes, the age difference, so what? I don’t care.”

Knowing they wanted to have a family one day, Savannah and Jason began the IVF process.

"To the public, I stated that I was going to freeze my eggs," she said. "And it wasn’t necessarily a lie. I had just not been public about my relationship yet. There were some things that I just needed to keep safe to me and also Jason."

Specifically, that they were “actually creating embryos,” Savannah added, “which was why it was really hard for me to talk about things at times because I couldn’t be 100 percent transparent about it. But we ended up with two healthy embryos.”

About a month after the egg retrieval, she and Jason went on a vacation along the coast of Turkey and Greece with plans to tie the knot in January. But while they were there, they discovered that Savannah is pregnant.

“It was a major plot twist,” she said. "We were both so shocked, so thrilled. God’s hand was written all over this. I thought, 'There is no way.'"

Now with plans to wed in May, Savannah and Jason are looking forward to becoming a family of three. Although, the Chrisleys are a much larger crew. To learn more about Savannah’s parents and all her siblings, read on.

#3704. These TP-Link Travel Routers Are up to 25% Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

A travel router sounds unnecessary until you run into unreliable hotel wifi, device limits, or a public network you’d rather not use. The TP-Link Roam 6 is built for exactly those situations, and its AX3000 variant is currently down to $69.99 from $79.98 on Amazon, while the cheaper AX1500 is $44.99, down from $59.99, with price trackers showing both at their lowest prices so far.

So the choice mostly comes down to whether you want to save $25 or pay more for faster networking and extra capacity—both can take hotel, Airbnb, or other public wifi and turn it into one private network for your devices, which means you can connect the router once and then join your phone, laptop, tablet, and streaming stick through it instead of signing each one in separately. They also support OpenVPN and WireGuard, so you can route those connected devices through a VPN if you want an extra layer of privacy.

The AX3000 is the one I would look at if you travel with several devices, work remotely, or simply want more headroom. It can deliver up to 2,402Mbps on the 5GHz band and 574Mbps on 2.4GHz, compared with 1,201 Mbps and 300 Mbps, respectively, on the AX1500, and it can handle up to 80 connected devices instead of 60. You also get a 2.5Gbps WAN port for faster wired internet, plus USB 3.0 for things like phone tethering, a USB modem, or file sharing. None of those upgrades will fix bad hotel internet, but they make the AX3000 better suited to heavier use once you are on a decent connection.

The AX1500, meanwhile, still gives you Wi-Fi 6, Gigabit Ethernet, USB tethering, and the core travel-router features, making it the easier pick if you mostly travel solo or just want a simple way to get a laptop, phone, and a few other devices online.

#3703. Lincoln Electric (LECO) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Lincoln Electric Holdings, Inc. (LECO +9.17%) reported record performance for the third quarter, led by organic volume growth and sales leverage that improved operating margins. Management raised full-year sales and capital expenditure guidance, citing robust demand across all five end markets and record-level backlogs in automation and equipment. The company is managing inflationary pressures in energy, logistics, and electronic components by implementing price actions intended to reach price-cost neutrality in the fourth quarter. Strategic focus remains on the RISE strategy, including the launch of physical artificial intelligence solutions and capacity expansions to support long-term growth objectives.

Operator: Greetings, and welcome to the Lincoln Electric's 26 Third Quarter Financial Results Conference Call. This call is being recorded. It is my pleasure to introduce your host, Amanda H. Butler, vice president of investor relations and communications. Thank you. Amanda, you may begin.

Amanda H. Butler: Thank you, Melissa, and good morning, everyone. Welcome to Lincoln Electric's third quarter 2026 conference call. We released our financial results earlier today, and you can find our release and this call slide presentation at lincolnelectric.com in the Investor Relations section. Joining me on the call today is Steven Hedlund, our Chairman and Chief Executive Officer as well as Gabe Bruno, our Chief Financial Officer. Following our prepared remarks, we are happy to take your questions.

But before we start our discussion, please note that certain statements made during this call may be forward looking, and actual results may differ materially from our expectations due to a number of risk factors and uncertainties, which are provided in our press release as well as in our SEC filings on Forms 10-K and 10-Q. In addition, we also discuss financial measures that do not conform to U. S. GAAP. A reconciliation of non GAAP measures to the most comparable GAAP measure is found in the financial tables in our earnings release which again is available in the Investor Relations section of our website at lincolnelectric.com. And with that, I will turn the call over to Steven Hedlund. Steven?

Steven Hedlund: Thank you, Amanda. Good morning, everyone. Turning to slide 3, we reported record third quarter sales and earnings performance with adjusted EPS increasing 21% to $2.98. Our results highlight 2 important trends we navigated in the quarter. First, accelerating demand in core areas of the business and second, challenging operating conditions that we are diligently managing. Our strong earnings were driven primarily by operating leverage from mid teens percent organic sales growth led by 9% volume growth. This is the first quarter in over 2.5 years that volume growth has outpaced price and volume momentum remains robust through October.

However, energy, logistics, and electronic component costs continue to increase with unprecedented AI data center demand creating spot shortages for some raw materials. While our supply chain and operations teams great teamwork and agility in managing these supply challenges, the additional cost impacted our gross profit margin. We implemented price actions during the quarter in all 3 segments, which will enable us to achieve price cost neutrality in the fourth quarter. We improved our adjusted operating income margin by 60 basis points to 18% at a 22% incremental margin which was in line with our September update and delivered top quartile ROIC at 23.9% up a 170 basis points compared with the prior year.

We also focused on long term growth and returns as we executed our capital allocation strategy. We invested $41 million in growth and $143 million to shareholders in the quarter. We remain focused on serving our customers, advancing our RISE strategy, and positioning the company for long term success. Turning to slide 4 to cover growth drivers in the quarter. Organic sales growth was broad with growth across all 3 reportable segments, product areas, and in all 5 end markets. Organic growth was largely driven by higher volume demand in The Americas region, and Asia Pacific and in select product areas in the Harris product segment. Sales in the U.S. increased over 20%.

Asia Pacific continued to expand with growth in China, India, and North Asia. Core Europe was relatively steady versus prior year, and we are starting to see benefits from European import tariffs and some defense spending. The Middle East was more resilient than expected with a $1 million sales headwind in the quarter. Capital spending continued to improve with high teens percent organic sales growth on automation resulting in $232 million in automation sales in the quarter. Equipment organic sales growth also accelerated to a low teens percent rate in the quarter. Strong order rates in both automation and equipment in the third quarter have elevated our backlog to new record levels. Which positions us well for 2027.

Consumable volumes increased to the mid single digit percent rate on a consolidated basis performed higher in Americas and Asia Pacific where we are seeing the strongest level of industrial and infrastructure activity. Looking at end sector trends on a consolidated basis, all 5 end markets grew in the quarter. General fabrication organic sales grew mid 20% with the outperformance reflecting solid industrial production activity in The Americas, as well as data center related projects and rising HVAC demand in Harris. Energy and nonresidential structural steel organic sales increased mid-teens percent with energy outperforming in Americas Welding and oil and gas due to strong project activity given the region's optimal positioning for the energy market.

Heavy industries and transportation both grew at a low double digit percent rate. Heavy industries continue to see strengthening in construction and mining to support infrastructure, data center, and mineral investments, while agricultural demand appears to be stabilizing. Transportation automotive growth benefited from easier prior year comparisons in both production rates and in timing of automation shipments. We expect growth to continue in the fourth quarter as well. Separately, the October domestic auto industry survey indicated a pickup in 2029 and 2030 model launches following 2 years of unprecedented compression. That outlook is consistent with the increase in quoting activity we are seeing for long lead time automation systems for transportation customers. Moving to slide 5.

At the FabTech trade show last week, we launched over a dozen new solutions that address customer pain points and expand the addressable markets we serve. For example, ArcTracker AIR provides customers with an easy to deploy that enables them to track productivity across their welding operations regardless of the mix of brands or type of welding equipment in use. We also launched a hot wire submerged arc welding solution for our heavy industry sector customers that delivers up to 50% improvement in deposition rates using their existing installed base of Lincoln equipment with only modest incremental investments. Our new InstructArc extended reality welding trainer supports workforce development and training by providing a more realistic simulation of the welding experience.

And finally, I am most excited about the launch of ExpertLink, our Physical AI solution that will revolutionize welding automation. Featured initially on our Cooper Cobot, ExpertLink enables a welding robot to adapt on the fly to real world conditions like an experienced human welder would. This capability significantly reduces barriers to adoption and broadens the market for cutting and welding automation. We will be integrating ExpertLink technology into our automation portfolio over the next few years which will further differentiate the value of our innovation and support our RISE growth objectives. I will now pass the call to Gabe Bruno to cover third quarter financials in more detail.

Gabriel Bruno: Thank you, Steven. Moving to slide 6. Third quarter sales increased 15% to $1.221 billion. Sales growth reflected approximately 9% higher volumes and 6% higher price. We anniversaried the acquisition of Alloy-Steel in August and contributed 50 basis points to sales growth. Gross profit increased 9% to 4 hundred and 25 million dollars on higher sales. Our gross profit margin compressed 190 basis points to 34.8% primarily due to the timing of our new price increases which went into effect late in the quarter and unfavorable mix. On a year to date basis, we are lagging on neutral price cost target by 100 basis points and expect to achieve a neutral price cost position in the fourth quarter.

SG&A expense was relatively flat versus prior year at $206 million while SG&A as a percent of sales improved 260 basis points to 16.9% as a result of sales leverage. SG&A expense included an additional $5 million in corporate investments related to the rollout of our RISE strategy and related initiatives. Given current RISE investment activity and business performance, we are maintaining our quarterly SG&A estimate at $210 million to $215 million with corporate expenses likely in the $4 million to $5 million range in the fourth quarter. Reported operating income increased approximately 20% to $211 million primarily on higher sales leverage.

Adjusted operating income increased 19% to $220 million and our adjusted operating income margin improved 60 basis points to 18% with a 22% incremental margin. Third quarter diluted earnings per share increased 19% to $2.63. Adjusted earnings per share increased approximately 21% to a record $2.98. We recognized a $0.04 benefit from share repurchases and there was no impact from foreign exchange translation. Moving to our reportable segments on slide 7. America's welding sales increased approximately 16% to $801 million, primarily from 13% higher volume and 2.4% higher price. Americas volume growth was broad based across all product groups and end markets and in key market channels.

Equipment and automation each delivered high teens percent volume growth supported by strong capital spending. Consumable volume growth increased to a mid to high single digit percent rate as industrial activity accelerated. Price reflects the benefit of the May price action. We expect price in the Americas to be in the low to mid single digit percent range in the fourth quarter reflecting third quarter price actions. Americas adjusted EBIT increased approximately 27% to $167 million. Adjusted EBIT margin expanded 190 basis points to 20.1%. The increase was driven by higher organic sales and improved automation performance, which was partially offset by price cost headwinds.

Sequential performance versus the second quarter was better than seasonal trends due to a pickup in demand in our Alloy-Steel acquisition and incremental structural savings. In just a few years, The Traitors has become an Emmy-winning success that's wildly popular for those with a Peacock subscription. Luckily for fans, the producers behind that show have another campy reality competition with The Mob. The series recently had its two-episode premiere for those with a Hulu subscription, and it's another super fun and silly series to follow. The cast is stellar, but the premiere really highlighted that Willam Belli is a top tier reality star.

The Traitors cast list is like my Avengers, where favorites from the Real Housewives, Survivor, and RuPaul's Drag Race collide in the castle. The Mob also didn't disappoint in this regard, especially the inclusion of The Sopranos star Aida Turturro, DWTS judge Bruno Tonioli and RHONJ star Joe Gorga. But the real star of the show is Willam, who is one of the most iconic Drag Race stars of all time.

Willam was a soundbite machine on Season 4 of Drag Race, and to this day she's the only contestant to be disqualified from the Emmy-winning competition series. Despite the season finale airing back in 2012, Willam has remained one of the most popular alumni from the show, thanks to her online content, popular podcast Race Chaser, and major acting gigs like A Star Is Born.

I was instantly invested in The Mob when it was revealed that Willam would be one of the contestants, and she did not disappoint in the show's premiere episodes. While I was originally mad that she wasn't included in the cast, she and Debi Mazar had a surprise entrance halfway through the first mission. From the minute she appeared in the trunk of a car, Willam brought non-stop entertainment to the new competition series.

Whether she was showing off her jewelry as the Don ring was introduced, or accidentally losing a nail during the wine stomping mission, the Nip/Tuck actor brought countless hilarious moments to The Mob's first episodes. I also love her cutting up with Mazar, and getting involved in the drama of The Family after Don Joe didn't give her a cut of the second mission's money.

Given how wildly entertaining Willam has already been on The Mob (including some stunning fashions), it's wild to think that she hasn't been on RuPaul's Drag Race All Stars yet. She's been open about the beef between her and that series, but her one-liners, looks, and sheer entertainment value proves that she's reality TV gold. If she doesn't ever return to Drag Race perhaps we can see her in other shows like House of Villains or The Traitors.

For now I'm just going to enjoy the gift of Willam Belli on my TV. New episodes of The Mob air Mondays on Hulu as part of the 2026 TV schedule.

After working in administrative theater for a year in New York, he started as the Weekend Editor at CinemaBlend. He's since been able to work himself up to reviews, phoners, and press junkets-- and is now able to appear on camera with some of his favorite actors... just not as he would have predicted as a kid. He's particularly proud of covering horror franchises like Scream and Halloween, as well as movie musicals like West Side Story. Favorite interviews include Steven Spielberg, Spike Lee, Jamie Lee Curtis, and more.

#3701. Watch Jay-Z Bring Out Dr. Dre and Snoop Dogg at L.A. Tour Closer

Jay-Z closed out his Jay-Z 30 trek on Saturday at Los Angeles’ SoFi Stadium, bringing an end to a star-studded headlining run that broke Yankee Stadium’s ticket sales record and featured guest appearances from Nas, Rihanna, and Beyoncé. The tour celebrated the 30th anniversary of Jay’s debut album, Reasonable Doubt.

On Friday, October 23, Hov invited Scarface to the stage to rap “This Can’t Be Life” and “Mind Playing Tricks on Me.” Later, Blue Ivy Carter accompanied her dad on piano during “The Story of O.J.” and “Dead Presidents / Dead Presidents II.” After ending his main set with “Public Service Announcement,” Jay returned with two more guests: John Legend and DJ Khaled, for a performance of their 2022 track “God Did."

The second and final night of the tour saw Jay joined by Too $hort for a medley of the former’s 2008 “Blow the Whistle” freestyle along with $hort’s original version. But the marquee guest spot of the evening went to Dr. Dre and Snoop Dogg, who came out to perform “The Next Episode”; “Still D.R.E.,” which Hov famously ghost wrote for Dre; “Nuthin But a G Thang”; and, of course, “California Love.” Jay also took the opportunity to honor late Los Angeles rapper Nipsey Hussle, who was killed in a 2019 shooting. He projected a black-and-white photo of the artist while playing his song “Hussle & Motivate,“ then incorporated the rapper’s “Last Time That I Checc’d” into a performance of “On to the Next One.”

Jay-Z also took a moment to address criticism of the tour, specifically the alleged low-priced resale tickets circulating online. ”Y’all gotta be very aware of what’s going on in the world, right? People will tell you that nobody was in here tonight, but you got eyes, you can see. People will tell you that the music wasn’t great, but you got ears,” he said. “You gotta understand, you gotta question everything in this world of AI.”

#3700. James Gunn and Peter Safran’s DC Experiment: What’s Working, What Isn’t, and What’s Next

Four years ago this week, James Gunn and Peter Safran were handed one of Hollywood’s most coveted, and impossible, jobs. When they were named co-CEOs of Warner Bros.’ DC Studios label, they received complete oversight of comic book characters like Superman, Wonder Woman, Batman* (more on that later), Green Lantern, the Flash, and countless others. With a seemingly blank canvas to make as many movies and shows as their hearts desired, the implied mandate was clear: get DC Comics out of second place.

The studio’s DC division spent much of the 2010s as a laughingstock while its Marvel competitors seemed to never miss. Despite having a better roster of characters — 20 years ago, mentioning the Guardians of the Galaxy in the same breath as Batman would have been laughable even to the biggest comic book nerd — Warner Bros. couldn’t figure out how to turn its characters into must-see cinema. The incredible cultural moment of the Christopher Nolan Batman movies gave way to one misfire after another: Zack Snyder’s “Man of Steel” gave way to the “Batman v. Superman: Dawn of Justice” debacle, Patty Jenkins’ acclaimed “Wonder Woman” movie led to a critically panned sequel, and the studio’s attempt to launch its own “Avengers” with “Justice League” was bad enough to leave fans arguing about different cuts instead of wondering where the story went next. Related Stories ‘I Play Rocky’ Wins Virginia Film Fest Audience Award as Peter Farrelly’s Stallone Drama Readies for Theaters Gotham Film Awards 2026 Nominations Spread the Love from ‘All of a Sudden’ to ‘The Invite’

It was the kind of turnaround project that entitles its leaders to a long runway. After Gunn and Safran accepted the job in late 2022, they began announcing projects in early 2023. Then they had to shoot and edit them, meaning that we didn’t really see the vision take shape until this year. But now we’ve seen Gunn and Safran release three major theatrical films, two live-action shows, and multiple animated ones — more than enough of a sample size to start grading their creative direction. Even if we’re still in Phase One of their long-term plan, the pros and cons of their studio approach are becoming clear.

Gunn and Safran kicked off their DC tenure with the safest and smartest film they could have possibly made: a “Superman” movie written and directed by Gunn himself. Putting DC’s signature character in the hands of a filmmaker with an unimpeachable ability to deliver smart popcorn fare was a no-brainer. And in many ways, “Superman” delivered.

The film wisely skipped the obligatory origin story that everyone already knows, checking in with the man formerly known as Kal-El after he had already assumed his dual role of Superman and Clark Kent on Earth. Gunn brought his signature blend of heart, humor, and needle drops, delivering an entertaining blockbuster that stood in clear contrast to the excessively dark DC films of the 2010s. This movie was colorful and fun, even in its heaviest moments. Unsurprisingly, it grossed over $600 million worldwide — though it still didn’t tell us much about Gunn’s ability to run a studio. His bona fides as a comic book writer/director were never in doubt, but we still hadn’t seen him supervise other filmmakers.

Those opportunities came in 2026, and the results were more polarizing. The studio’s second film, “Supergirl,” was one of the year’s most public flops. Milly Alcock’s eponymous character appeared briefly in “Superman,” but Craig Gillespie’s film traded its predecessor’s grounded Earth drama for a space western set across a series of “Mad Max”-esque planets. The confusing mythology and grating visual effects felt like a departure, and the film received mixed reviews and only made $100 million worldwide despite costing nearly twice that. Equally concerning were reports about creative clashes between Gunn and Safran and Gillespie, with two competing cuts being screened against each other months before the movie opened.

Then came “Clayface.” James Watkins’ origin story for an obscure Batman villain was branded as a horror movie that just happened to exist within a comic book universe. The gory, goopy story of an actor who sacrifices everything to regain his skin-deep beauty wasn’t for everyone, but it represented a real creative swing. And it was made on a responsible $44 million budget, which removed the pressure to be universally appealing.

It’s easy to see a world where this DC universe thrives with a combination of major tentpoles directed by Gunn (who is already hard at work on a “Superman” follow-up dated for July 2027) and smaller, weirder films based on minor characters. But we still haven’t seen Gunn and Safran oversee a blockbuster that connects with mainstream audiences with another filmmaker in the director’s chair. “Supergirl” may end up being an anomaly, but right now it still represents one-third of their theatrical output. That sadly has to count for something.

Gunn and Safran’s overhaul of DC’s TV output technically began before they took over as CEOs, as “Peacemaker” premiered on HBO Max nine months earlier. Created (and largely directed) by Gunn, the raunchy action comedy saw John Cena reprising his role from Gunn’s “Suicide Squad” movie as an antihero willing to kill as many men, women, and children as he needs to achieve his goal of world peace. Gunn’s sensibilities were still there, but the HBO brand let the show include far more nudity and profanity than you’d ever find in a four-quadrant film. A hit with fans and critics, “Peacemaker” reminded audiences what was possible when you had access to a premium cable brand to complement your theatrical films.

Their first official project after taking the reins was also a TV show. The animated “Creature Commandos” debuted on HBO Max in December 2024 with an inoffensive six-episode Season 1 that showcased Gunn’s favorite themes about weirdos who assemble their own found families. The business stakes were low — fairly or not, the average consumer isn’t watching animated superhero shows, and the diehard fans who do tune in are unlikely to ever stop supporting DC’s other projects. But the series has one notable wrinkle: Gunn cast its voice actors with the intention of reprising their roles in live-action projects, a tactic he says he plans to use on all future animated projects.

“Peacemaker” also concluded with a well-received Season 2, but the real test of the DC Universe’s TV output came this fall with “Lanterns.” Chris Mundy, Damon Lindelof, and Tom King’s series about DC’s intergalactic police force more than delivered on its promise to be “True Detective” with Green Lanterns. The film uses classic police procedural tropes — the show centers on an older, set-in-his-ways Green Lantern who reluctantly partners with a talented young partner he won’t give the time of day to — in a dark, season-long murder saga set in middle America. The show itself received rave reviews, but its actual contents matter less than what it represents: a unique, auteur-driven project that fits into Gunn and Safran’s storyline while allowing other filmmakers to showcase their own voices. So far, it’s the ultimate example of what the CEOs have always said they wanted their DC Studios to do.

Perhaps the biggest variable facing Gunn and Safran’s DC Studios is largely outside their control. Matt Reeves’ “The Batman” had already opened in theaters a few months before they took over, and Warner Bros. announced that the Robert Pattinson-led series would continue separately from the new DC Universe. A second film is in production, with Steven Soderbergh helping to complete principal photography, and HBO shows like “The Penguin” and the recently announced “Poison Ivy” continue to expand the mythology.

Gunn and Safran have some involvement in these “Batman” projects and publicly support them, but the films and shows exist outside the overarching storyline they’re trying to build. And while it’s not an insurmountable problem — multiple artists working with the same characters in different timelines has been a trademark of comic books from the very beginning — it’s also not ideal to have one of your franchise’s two biggest characters tied up outside your purview.

Nothing’s stopping Gunn and Safran from bringing in their own Batman, and it seems inevitable that it will happen soon. “Clayface” takes place in Gotham City, and once you start introducing Batman villains, you need to give audiences something to fight them. Gunn and Safran previously tapped Andy Muschietti to direct a Batman movie called “The Brave and the Bold,” which was still in development as of 2025, but no cast or release date has been announced.

Many fans and Warner Bros. shareholders believe this DC Universe won’t reach its full potential until it introduces its own Batman. The kind of major team-up movies with billion-dollar box office potential, like a new “Justice League” or a “World’s Finest” team-up between Batman and Superman, all feature the Dark Knight. And assuming their Batman isn’t Robert Pattinson, they’ll inevitably be faced with audiences who wonder why or see theirs as a second-tier product.

It’s not fair to grade Gunn and Safran on this problem, since they didn’t create it, but they still have to navigate it. Again, it’s not insurmountable, but Kevin Feige never had to deal with this at Marvel.

Gunn and Safran’s overarching DC story is still in its infancy, but their development process has become clear. And it’s arguably their best asset. In many ways, their DC Studios can be described as the anti-MCU. Whereas their competitor (and Gunn’s former employer) became famous for seeing everything as part of a larger puzzle, DC Studios seems much more focused on treating each film as a standalone project. Films are greenlit based on the quality of their individual scripts, not the Easter eggs that connect them. Nobody would ever tell you that a brand-new DC cinematic universe should release “Clayface” as one of its first films, but Gunn has openly said Mike Flanagan’s script stood out among the many other projects they were developing. The script was ready; therefore, they made the movie.

Prioritizing scripts over corporate synergy should be music to the ears of cinephiles burned out on Marvel’s assembly-line approach to storytelling. That means continuity will never be as tight as what you find in the MCU, and plenty of projects will be announced without ever coming to fruition. (We’re still upset about James Mangold’s “Swamp Thing” and Luca Guadagnino’s “Sgt. Rock” falling through the cracks, but it’s better than rushing out movies that aren’t ready.) Comic book fans used to following these companies like sports teams might need to adjust their expectations, but movie lovers who care less about those details should get richer viewing experiences. It’s also better for the creatives who get a chance to pitch their own original ideas and put their own stamp on beloved characters.

The only thing holding this back from an “A” grade is the fact that ideals that sound good on paper still have to be executed. I deeply admire Gunn for publicly declaring that no DC Studios movie will receive a release date until everyone is happy with the script. But I also saw “Supergirl” and wondered what this mysterious great script could have possibly looked like, and why it apparently didn’t make it into the finished movie.

#3699. Escaped Inmate Jacob Allen Morley: Cops Find Car He Stole Abandoned in Illinois

Cops found a major clue in the hunt for escaped Tennessee inmate Jacob Allen Morley ... they just found the car he stole abandoned in Illinois.

The Tennessee Bureau of Investigation say they found the Lindamood Planting Company truck Morley stole after busting out of prison ... all the way in Marion, IL.

They say they still don't know where the convicted killer is, but "locating him continues to be an urgent priority for law enforcement."

The TBI also says public safety is their top concern, and they're working with local, state and federal organizations to find him "as quickly as possible."

As you know, Morley escaped from Northwest Correctional Complex in Tiptonville, TN Sunday night, after apparently slipping through multiple fences.

The Tennessee Bureau of Investigation issued a shocking update Monday evening, posting a video of Morley stealing the white 2008 Ford F-350 truck around 1 AM that day in Lake County, TN ... about 150 miles from where the truck was found.

He was convicted of killing Cody Birchfield last year -- and Cody's sister says the cops never called her to tell her Morley escaped.

Authorities are urging people to not approach Morley if they see him, but instead call 911. They say to contact 1-800-TBI-FIND with any helpful information.

#3698. The Queen That Never Was: The Twisted Tale of Lady Arbella Stuart

Early in her reign, Queen Elizabeth I—sensing her court was teeming with doubters and double-crossers—made it clear she’d cede her power to no husband. This also meant, of course, she would not produce an heir, which must have turned her bloodline-crazed dad, Henry VIII, in his tomb.

Elizabeth I, Queen of England and Ireland, 1588. Version of the Armada portrait attributed to George Gower.

What’s more, she refused to name a successor. “Assuredly, if my successor were known to the world, I would never esteem my state to be safe,” she told parliament in 1559. Into this vacuum stepped an array of noble sons and daughters with even the flimsiest claim to the throne. “In the 1590s, there were at times as many as a dozen viable contenders to succeed Elizabeth I,” said Elizabeth Goldring, art historian and author of Nicholas Hilliard: Life of an Artist.

In 2024 Goldring, along with Emma Rutherford, who runs The Limner Company, a portrait miniature consultancy, discovered a long lost miniature of one such claimant to the throne: 16-year-old Lady Arbella Stuart, great-great-granddaughter of Henry VII, the first Tudor king, and niece of the doomed Mary Queen of Scots. Described by her first cousin (twice removed) Elizabeth I as “an eaglet of her own kind,” and by the Venetian ambassador as having “very exalted ideas,” Arbella was at times favored above the Queen’s eventual Scottish successor, James I. “Arbella is one of the greatest ‘might haves’ of history,” said Rutherford.

The rediscovery of this long-forgotten portrait, which sold earlier this year, pushes Arbella’s almost-unbelievable story back to the forefront of Tudor and Elizabethan history, an age littered with tales of highborn daughters moving about like pawns on a chessboard, but few got as close to the throne as Arbella.

Elizabeth Hardwick, Countess of Shrewsbury, “Bess of Hardwick,” 1520–1608.

Raised in gilded captivity by her dynastically inclined grandmother, Bess of Hardwick, who described Arbella as her “jewel” (to be prized, polished and hidden away), Arbella grew up isolated and believing she’d be queen, much like Queen Victoria centuries later. Unlike Victoria, that never happened. Such exalted notions can’t be good for the teenage psyche (she would suffer from fragile, and at times dangerously unstable, mental health for the rest of her life.)

In the spring of 2023, Rutherford was contacted by the then-owner of the miniature to see if she could identify the sitter. She brought in Goldring, who had written a book on the miniature’s painter, Nicholas Hilliard. “Emma and I ended up researching the miniature together for the next twelve months and having a whale of a time on what turned out to be an incredible journey of discovery,” said Goldring.

There were hints. “After almost thirty years of looking at portrait miniatures it takes a lot to make my heart beat faster,” said Rutherford. “I had never seen anything like it…there were so many clues in the painting which could help us identify the sitter.”

Bess of Hardwick was a meticulous keeper of household expenses, so Rutherford and Goldring were able to trace a payment to England’s premier portrait miniaturist of the time, Hilliard, as well as tips paid to Elizabeth I’s gardeners at Greenwich Palace, where the portrait was painted. They were also aware Hilliard had painted Arbella in the early 1590s, “albeit the conventional wisdom was that the resulting portrait was no longer extant,” said Goldring. “It seemed almost too good to be true that we could have found Hilliard’s portrait of her...But the deeper we dug in the archives, the more evidence we found relating to this commission, all of which pointed to the sitter being Arbella.”

Forgotten for centuries and likely tucked away at the back of a drawer or cupboard, the watercolor portrait was “remarkably fresh and vibrant”, said Goldring. As Rutherford recalls, “it was unglazed and unframed and loosely held in a Victorian leather case. One sneeze could have obliterated something that had survived for over 400 years virtually untouched.”

Queen Elizabeth I, “The Ditchley portrait” by Marcus Gheeraerts, 1592.

Though small in size, the portrait tells a story of soaring ambitions. In the summer of 1592, Grandma Bess hired Hilliard to paint Arbella standing in the flowering gardens of Greenwich Palace dressed in the impossibly-high-ruffed dress of a queen, and making what Goldring realized was a preposterously bold allusion to the famous “Ditchley portrait” of Elizabeth I from the same year (now hanging in the National Portrait Gallery in London).

“This was a miniature designed to present Arbella as, among other things, a Queen-in-waiting, another Elizabeth I, but younger and still of child-bearing age,” said Goldring. “Note all the images of fertility and fruitfulness in that garden.”

Small and therefore easy to transport, portrait miniatures such as these were designed to be used in dynastic marriage negotiations between Europe’s royal courts and powerful families. But as Goldring and Rutherword would discover, this particular miniature was more than just a pretty picture of a would-be bride.

“One of the most extraordinary things Emma and I discovered was that Hilliard’s miniature of Arbella was, at one stage, intended for use by the Elizabethan spy services as a prop in a plot to wrong-foot Philip II of Spain,” said Goldring. By leading Philip into believing Arbella was marrying the son of his ally, the Duke of Parma, Protestant England could drive a wedge between two Continental Catholic powers.

“Arbella stands with the world at her feet: She is presented as royal, she is poised to marry into an alliance which will be for England’s good, but she is about to lose it all,” said Rutherford. In the end the marriage plot fell through and the portrait never made it to the continent. Tucked away and forgotten for centuries, it served as a woebegotten metaphor for Arbella’s life.

“When she failed to fulfill her one role in life to become queen, and then her grandmother died a few years later, she rebelled on a grand scale,” said Rutherford. In her 30s Arbella made a last ditch grab for power—or simply agency—when she married William Seymour (another former claimant to the throne) in secret. Word of the marriage reached the top, as it always does (Will these courtiers never learn?) and Arbella’s former rival for the crown, now King James I, had the couple locked up, separately.

Arbella escaped, disguised as a man in a wig, boots and sword, with plans to flee to France with her ill-begotten husband. Seymour made it to safety and prospered like a man. She was caught and sent to the Tower. In a tragically poetic twist of fate, she died in 1615 following a hunger strike in what were likely the former Tower lodgings of her forebear, Anne Boleyn, that other dynastic pawn-turned-power player of the Tudor age.

“I think that this portrait has gone a long way to reviving an interest in Arbella. It has humanized someone who was previously just a footnote in history and fleshed out her story in a way that perhaps no other artefact might have done,” said Rutherford. “And everyone loves a new discovery.”

#3697. Stephen King Thinks A Beloved Taylor Sheridan Drama Was Snubbed By The Emmys

If you've ever wondered what Stephen King does between writing I can tell you: watch TV and movies. The man's social media is basically a recommendation machine at this point, with the master of horror handing out suggestions for new shows and films to watch on an almost daily basis. King recently recommended the perfect horror miniseries on Netflix, and previously gave his fans pause when he suggested a forgotten action movie with a 28% Rotten Tomatoes score. Now, he's back to deliver his take on Taylor Sheridan's "Landman" being snubbed by the Emmys.

Sheridan has never been an awards darling, but his oil drama should have finally broken his Emmy curse. The Billy Bob Thornton-led series is easily one of the best shows the "Yellowstone" creator has made, and with its combination of soapy melodrama and relatable family dynamics, has delivered something so watchable it's honestly sort of concerning. Alas, the show — which debuted back in November 2024 — has been overlooked by the Television Academy throughout its three-season run.

King, who is evidently a fan, doesn't think that's right, and felt compelled to say as much on his Twitter/X account. "I don't understand why 'LANDMAN' didn't get nominated for Emmy awards," wrote the author. "Particularly Billy Bob Thornton for best actor and Stephen Kay for best director. Is it politics, or what?" King raises a good point. Regardless of how you feel about Sheridan's brand of soap-meets-realistic-drama entertainment, there is absolutely no doubting Thornton's performance as oil man Tommy Norris. Likewise, the overall quality of the production, including the direction, hasn't ever been in question. Like many, then, King is confused as to why the show hasn't received any recognition whatsoever from the Emmys.

"Landman" is probably the best Taylor Sheridan show. With this series, the prolific writer seems to have perfected his formula for over-the-top nonsense tempered by some of the most strikingly moving scenes you'll ever see. Meanwhile he has an absolute powerhouse in the lead role, with Billy Bob Thornton already having been recognized for his portrayal of Tommy Norris with Golden Globe, Satellite Award, and Critics Choice Award nominations. Thus far, however, neither he nor his oil drama have received any Emmy nods, despite a significant Emmys campaign for "Landman" Season 2 in 2026.

This issue is bigger than "Landman," however. Aside from the fact his shows have typically been associated with right-leaning politics, Sheridan hasn't minced words when it comes to critics and generally sees himself as somewhat anti-Hollywood (he previously described "Yellowstone" to Gold Derby as "a punk rock, rebellious teenage, both middle fingers at TV and at Hollywood" project). Throughout its five-season run, "Yellowstone" only received a single Emmy nomination for production design, and Sheridan's other shows have secured technical and stunt coordination nominations. Otherwise, the Television Academy has been reluctant to acknowledge any lead actors, Sheridan's writing, or directors of his shows.

Sheridan himself claims to be unbothered, telling The Bill Simmons Podcast (via The Hollywood Reporter) "You're not going to win no Emmys with me, but I'm not trying to win Emmys. That's not my goal. My goal is to sit somebody on their couch and move them." I think it's fair to say he's done that. Certain scenes in "Landman" are some of the most poignant of the last decade, and the show's absence from the Emmy nominations is starting to become extremely conspicuous to those paying attention.

It's interesting to note that Stephen King highlighted Stephen Kay in his social media post about "Landman." Most discussions about the show's lack of Emmy recognition focus on Taylor Sheridan or Billy Bob Thornton, but Kay has been a quiet force behind the scenes of not only "Landman" but a raft of Sheridan shows that have all been well-received.

Sure, the director is responsible for Sylvester Stallone's big crime flop with an 11% Rotten Tomatoes score "Get Carter." But he has more than proved himself on the small screen. After overseeing 15 episodes of "Yellowstone," Kay helmed the final four episodes of "Lioness" Season 4. The second season of the Zoe Saldaña-led spy thriller ended with a gripping shootout in the Iranian desert, and its final episode stands as one of the most memorable season finales of recent years. Yet neither Kay, nor anyone else involved in "Landman" has even been nominated for an Emmy.

Since then, Kay has directed the entirety of "Landman" Season 2 (as well as overseeing most of Season 1). Once again, however, he has yet to be recognized by the Television Academy. You can argue that Sheridan's ostensible politics being voiced by Thornton's character is the reason neither of them have been nominated. But as King highlights, Kay's absence points to something else going on behind the scenes, and it's starting to get weird. If nothing else, at least take King's recommendation and check out "Landman" for some absolutely ridiculous yet somehow deeply moving entertainment.

#3695. This Top-Rated Tapo Wireless Outdoor Camera Is 33% Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

There’s no shortage of outdoor security cameras, but finding one that balances video quality, battery life, and ease of use without pushing past $100 is rare. Right now, the Tapo MagCam 2K+ is on sale for $59.99 on Amazon, down from $89.99, which is its lowest price to date, according to price trackers. PCMag named it the best security camera of 2024, pointing out that while battery-powered cameras often trade convenience for frequent recharging, the MagCam 2K+ avoids that issue with a 10,000mAh battery rated to last up to 300 days per charge, depending on how often it records.

Charging happens over micro-USB, though there’s an optional Tapo A200 solar panel if you want to avoid manual recharging altogether. Video tops out at 2K resolution, with a wide 150-degree field of view that works well for driveways or side yards. During the day, footage looks sharp and detailed. At night, the camera can switch between infrared black-and-white or color video using its built-in spotlights and starlight sensor. Storage is flexible. You can record locally to a microSD card, up to 512GB (card not included), or you can go with cloud storage through a Tapo Care plan starting at $3.49/month.

Alerts can distinguish between people, pets, and vehicles, and those intelligent notifications are free, as highlighted by PCMag in its “outstanding” review of the MagCam 2K+. It also integrates smoothly with Alexa, Google Assistant, and IFTTT, though it doesn’t support Apple’s HomeKit. You also won’t find mechanical pan or tilt, but the fixed lens covers enough ground for most yards or porches. As for managing the camera, its companion app is intuitive, offering settings for motion sensitivity, siren activation, spotlight brightness, firmware updates, and more. Overall, the MagCam 2K+ handles the basics well and throws in a few smart features usually reserved for pricier models.

#3694. CrowdStrike vs. Fastly: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy in 2026?

The cybersecurity and edge computing sectors remain vital as digital transformation accelerates across global enterprises. Choosing between CrowdStrike (CRWD +2.36%) and Fastly (FSLY +6.87%) requires balancing high-octane growth against valuation and operational risks.

CrowdStrike focuses on cloud-native security, protecting endpoints and identities through its unified platform. Fastly operates in the edge cloud space, helping developers build and secure fast digital experiences closer to the end user. Both companies are prominent technology names that cater to the modern enterprise's need for speed and safety.

CrowdStrike sells its AI-native Falcon platform to secure endpoints, cloud workloads, and data for a diverse group of global organizations. It remains a prominent name among tech stocks and maintains a strategic partnership with Nvidia (NVDA +0.90%). Following a content configuration incident in mid-2024, the company implemented customer commitment packages and subscription extensions to mitigate contract terminations.

In the fiscal year ended Jan. 31, 2026, revenue reached nearly $4.8 billion, representing growth of approximately 21.7% compared with the prior fiscal year. Despite this top-line expansion, the company reported a net loss of roughly $162.5 million. This reflects a wider net margin of negative 3.4% compared to the negative 0.5% reported previously, as the business invested in customer remediation.

As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio was close to 1.8x, meaning the company's short-term assets cover its short-term liabilities. The debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.2x, indicating that total debt, which includes short-term and long-term obligations, is low relative to shareholder equity. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 68% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Fastly provides an edge cloud platform that enables businesses to deliver fast and secure digital experiences by processing data closer to users. Its customer base is concentrated, with the ten largest customers accounting for a significant portion of revenue, including TikTok and Jio Platforms. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business since it increases dependency on a small number of high-volume users.

In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, revenue reached approximately $624.0 million, a 14.8% increase over the previous year. The company recorded a net loss of nearly $121.7 million during this period. However, the net margin improved to negative 19.5% from the negative 29.1% reported in the prior fiscal year, showing progress toward narrowing losses as the platform scales.

According to its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio was roughly 2.6x, indicating a strong ability to meet short-term financial obligations with its current assets. The debt-to-equity ratio was approximately 0.5x, showing that total debt is equivalent to half of the company's shareholder equity. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 124.2% of operating cash flow, meaning reported cash generation is heavily inflated by this non-cash add-back.

CrowdStrike faces ongoing business and reputational damage following the technical incident on July 19, 2024. The company must navigate an intense competitive landscape from rivals like Microsoft (MSFT -0.24%) while managing reliance on Amazon (AMZN +1.61%) for infrastructure. Furthermore, it is managing ongoing securities litigation related to the incident and a complex global regulatory environment.

Fastly deals with significant revenue concentration among a small group of customers, which makes it vulnerable to shifts in usage or restructuring. The company faces stiff competition in the edge computing market from legacy providers and specialized rivals like Cloudflare (NET -1.22%). Operational risks include a dependency on a limited number of suppliers and ongoing securities class action litigation.

Fastly appears to be the more affordable option based on both sales and earnings multiples, while CrowdStrike commands a significant premium for its market leadership.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with CrowdStrike. Fastly has turned a corner and deserves more credit than it gets, but when I think about which business I want to own for the next decade, the answer is clear.

Every enterprise deploying AI creates new security vulnerabilities, and CrowdStrike keeps winning that business at scale. It delivered what its CEO called the best quarter in company history in Q2 fiscal 2027. Free cash flow hit a record, platform adoption accelerated sharply, and the company raised its full-year outlook substantially. The company has already signaled a record Q3 pipeline, which sets up the Dec. 1 report as one to watch.

Fastly posted record revenue and gross margins in Q2 2026 and raised its full-year guidance, with security revenue leading the way. Profitability is still a work in progress, but the trajectory is looking good.

But CrowdStrike is already the dominant platform in its category, profitable, and accelerating. I'd much rather own the established leader in such an important market than wait for a turnaround to fully play out.

#3686. Why the Holidays Are Actually the Best Time of Year to Start Working Out

If you have even the tiniest inkling that you might want to get in shape in the new year—or anytime really—don’t wait until January. This isn't a "winter arc" self-improvement pitch; it's just facts. The best time to start a gym habit is before the holiday season. Heck, in December, you’ll even have the place mostly to yourself.

Sure, technically “right now” is always the best time to start exercising, but the holiday season is special. If you wait until January, gyms will be packed. The first two weeks of the year, everybody thinks they’re at the start of an amazing new fitness habit...and by February, most of them have forgotten all about it. Don’t make life harder for yourself by starting during the big rush. Classes will fill up, you’ll have to fight people for the squat rack, and it will be hard to have any real fun. So start now.

In October and November, gyms and classes are at more or less their normal capacities. This is a good time to feel out the vibe of a new place. But it gets better: Once December rolls around, the fair-weather gymgoers will be off shopping for gifts after work and attending holiday parties on the weekends. They won’t feel like getting out of their warm beds in the morning, either. When you arrive, the squat rack will be free. You’ll have your choice of treadmills. The gym will be your oyster.

If you start now, you'll have about two months to get up to speed before the rush starts in January. And that is a perfect little time frame to set a small goal and keep it. Vow to hit the gym three times a week, and you’ll be able to get in 24 workouts before Christmas, even if you take a few days off for festivities. You can get a lot stronger in 24 workouts. Or run, if that's the fitness journey you're most excited about. If you start a couch-to-5k running plan now, you’ll be routinely running 30 minutes at a stretch by the time we get to January.

Yes, the holiday season can be busy and stressful, but remember that working out tends to help our brains manage stress. Mark workouts on your calendar, and keep those appointments with yourself.

Don’t worry about missing out on deals. Any gym or studio that offers a January special probably offers sales year-round anyway. And ultimately, if you get into the habit of actually using the gym, you're getting a lot more value for your money than somebody who joins in January and quickly stops showing up.

#3684. Throwing Muses Reissue Self-Titled Debut Album for 40th Anniversary

Rhode Island indie rock legends Throwing Muses will reissue their long-out-of-print (and off streaming) self-titled debut this December, in honor of its 40th anniversary. The new edition of Throwing Muses, due December 11 on 4AD, celebrates the first full-length album by the first American band ever signed by the label, a major inflection point in its history.

The quartet of of vocalist Kristin Hersh, guitarist-vocalist Tanya Donelly, bassist Leslie Langston, and drummer David Narcizo were still teenagers when they released Throwing Muses in 1986. The album largely drew inspiration from a car accident that left a 16-year-old Hersh, the band’s primary lyricist, with a rod in her skull. In the aftermath, she says she began hallucinating songs like the snarling tracks Throwing Muses would eventually release.

“I can’t even remember what it was like to hear a song that didn’t grab my face and shout at it,” Hersh wrote in her 2010 memoir Rat Girl. “Must’ve been soothing. But this is electrifying.”

After 4AD signed Throwing Muses, the label’s direction shifted towards American alternative rock, exemplified by their signing of the Pixies in 1987. Throwing Muses went on to release seven albums and two EPs on 4AD. Their most LP was 2025’s Moonlight Concessions. The group has also spawned multiple side projects, including 50FootWave and Lakuna.

#3683. 3 Types of Investors Who Should Avoid SpaceX and Tesla Stocks as the Companies Explore a Potential Merger

On Space Exploration Technologies' (SPCX +1.14%) third-quarter earnings call on Oct. 20, CEO Elon Musk revealed that the company and his other megacap, Tesla (TSLA +4.04%), are contemplating a merger.

Not surprisingly, many investors have taken a strong interest in a possible SpaceX-Tesla merger. The combined entity would be one of the world's largest AI companies.

Although many investors would likely own stakes in that company, such a stock is not suitable for everyone. In particular, these three types of investors should probably not rush to buy it.

Even for those who know nothing about investing, both SpaceX and Tesla might look like attractive choices on the surface. Both are growing their revenues thanks to technologies that increasingly interest the public, and Musk's past successes add to their appeal.

However, most investors do not own individual stocks. Instead, they tend to own long-term passive mutual funds and exchange-traded funds held in their retirement accounts. That is a sensible strategy, particularly for investors who don't have the time or willingness to dig into the details of individual businesses' competitive advantages, risks, and financial metrics. Many of these funds may include Tesla and SpaceX among their numerous other holdings.

Moreover, if one wants to learn how to invest in stocks, most experts recommend starting with companies that have well-known brands, solid balance sheets, and long track records. SpaceX and Tesla are both well-known, but SpaceX doesn't even have a full year under its belt as a public company, and Tesla only went public in 2010 -- that's not a terribly long track record either.

Moreover, objectively speaking, these companies' financials may not be the easiest to understand. Blue chips such as Home Depot or Chevron, which have existed for decades and operate in more straightforward businesses, may make better sense as one's first stock picks.

Another aspect of investing that both novices and veterans alike must account for in their choices is their risk tolerance.

Risk-averse investors would rather accept less growth potential in exchange for a lower chance that they'll actually lose money on a stock. As such, they are better advised to buy slower-growing stocks in stable businesses that are less likely to fall in the long run. Even in bear markets, they tend to decline less than cutting-edge tech companies that get valued heavily on the future potential, and those slow-and-steady stocks often pay dividends.

In contrast, risk-tolerant investors are willing to accept a markedly higher possibility of stock price declines if it increases the likelihood of rapid growth. Almost by definition, this requires them to be comfortable holding on through significant short-term ups and downs.  Not surprisingly, SpaceX and Tesla typically attract this type of investor despite being among the largest companies trading on the market.

Additionally, both are bets on the artificial intelligence megatrend. Assuming their AI technology thrives in niche markets, either of their stocks or the combined entity could earn considerable returns. However, failures in this area could mean investor losses. Moreover, since both trade at extremely high and speculative valuations, investors today face a greater risk of overpaying for these stocks.

Not all investors will feel comfortable with these risks. Those who don't should probably not own these companies directly.

Given the other two reasons to not own these stocks, telling some growth investors not to buy SpaceX or Tesla does not seem to make sense. Tesla has historically experienced impressive periods of rapid growth in its publicly traded history, and both appear well-positioned to outperform the S&P 500.

Unfortunately, investors also must temper their expectations given the companies' sizes. Growth investors have likely read stories about how those who invested a few thousand dollars in Home Depot or Amazon around their IPOs and held on now have positions worth millions.

The difference is that Home Depot or Amazon were small-cap companies at the time of their IPOs. At this point, Tesla and SpaceX are megacaps. Currently, Tesla's market cap is about $1.5 trillion, while SpaceX's is at $2 trillion -- just about where it was early on the day of its June IPO.

This means that the combined company's size would be at least $3.5 trillion. A 100-fold gain in such a stock would take it to $350 trillion. Considering that no company has yet reached $6 trillion, and the entire value of the U.S. stock market is around $75 trillion, even a 10-fold gain would seem hard to imagine.

Admittedly, AI development is driving rapid growth. If a combined SpaceX and Tesla succeeds, a $10 trillion or even $20 trillion market cap is possible in the foreseeable future. Still, a $20 trillion market cap would be close to a sixfold gain. That would be impressive, but it's still well short of the 100-fold or more return potential that extreme growth investors might seek.

Although a combined SpaceX and Tesla holding could be desirable, that prospective stock would not be suitable for everyone. Both companies are likely to be among the top AI companies. This could mean they will outperform the market regardless of whether the merger happens.

Nonetheless, before buying such a stock, one has to know what kind of investor they are. If they are not knowledgeable, cannot tolerate significant risk, or have extreme growth expectations, they may not want to buy shares of this prospective future company.

Will Healy has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Chevron, Home Depot, and Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3680. Matthew Perry Made His Movie Debut In A Forgotten River Phoenix Drama

Mary Robertson's documentary "The One About Matthew Perry" –- a play on the episode titles of "Friends," the sitcom that launched Perry into the television stratosphere -– is one of the most brutally valuable portraits of how fame and fortune solve nothing. It's the old "Richard Cory" story: a person who seems to have it all inexplicably ends their life due to hidden sadness and/or distress.

The narrative surrounding Perry, before he died, has long been one of an incurable addict. He was acutely aware of his sickness, but he couldn't maintain his sobriety. As he says, in his own words, in the documentary, he found booze and pills and whatever else to be a halcyon escape from the joylessness of his life.

As such, it's doubly tragic to realize that Perry's first big-screen role came opposite River Phoenix in William Richert's bawdy coming-of-age movie "A Night in the Life of Jimmy Reardon." Per Perry's autobiography, we know he had been drinking since the age of 14, and, according to Gus Van Sant's "Pink," Phoenix's major issue was not heroin but alcohol. They were two bruised peas in a pod, and while they were wildly different people (in the documentary, Perry is described as a cheeseburger dude whereas Phoenix was a practicing vegan), they were perfectly matched on screen.

If you or anyone you know needs help with addiction issues, help is available. Visit the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration website or contact SAMHSA's National Helpline at 1-800-662-HELP (4357).

"A Night in the Life of Jimmy Reardon" should've been a horned-up coming-of-age classic in the ribald mode of "Fast Times at Ridgemont High." You've got a never-sexier Phoenix romancing Meredith Salenger, Ione Skye, and Ann Magnuson. But it wasn't a low-aiming sex comedy like the "American Pie" films. William Richert was adapting his novel, "Aren't You Even Gonna Kiss Me Goodbye?," and he envisioned Phoenix's title character as a half-assed beatnik who wanted out of Chicago.

Perry's Fred Roberts was his moneyed, Harvard-bound, easily manipulated best friend who got nothing out of the relationship. It's one of the few instances in his career where Perry was cast as a dupe. Early in the film, Jimmy hits up Fred to cover half of his college tuition, which he coughed up in an abortion scam. Fred resists until Jimmy promises to get him a girl who'll relieve him of his virginity. They find the girl, but, predictably, she ends up with Jimmy.

Perry and Phoenix were good together, but as a charmless rich kid, the former was playing way against his strengths. I've never seen Perry more directed, which leaves me wishing he could've collaborated with a filmmaker who might've unlocked the actor we never got to see. He was so talented, and so willing to please, but sometimes tragedy is inescapable.

#3679. Will King Charles Be the Last Windsor to Lead the Commonwealth?

According to a report by The Times, King Charles is reportedly aware that he may be the last member of the British royal family to serve as head of the Commonwealth. The reason? His son William, the future monarch of England, reportedly has no intention of succeeding him in this role as well. A prospect that, if confirmed, would mark a significant change in relations between the British monarchy and the organization that brings together 56 countries. For while it is true that William is destined to inherit the throne, it is by no means certain that he wishes to retain every responsibility and tradition associated with the Crown. (Vanity Fair has reached out to Buckingham Palace for comment on the Times report.)

The issue, in reality, is more complex than it might seem. Unlike the British throne, in fact, the leadership of the Commonwealth is not automatically passed from a sovereign to their heir. It is a symbolic role, and succession is decided by the leaders of the member states. Charles assumed the role after the death of his mother, Elizabeth, but his appointment had been agreed upon by the Commonwealth heads of government in 2018. William, therefore, would not be obligated to accept it.

According to The Times’ report, the Prince of Wales has indicated that he does not wish to become head of the organization when he ascends to the throne. However, this is neither an official announcement from Kensington Palace nor a decision that has already been formalized. To understand the significance of this potential decision, we must look back to Elizabeth. During her 70-year reign, the monarch made the Commonwealth one of the pillars of her institutional work, guiding the transformation of an organization tied to the history of the British Empire into a voluntary association of independent states.

Charles has embraced that legacy with conviction. His commitment to the Commonwealth is an important part of his diplomatic vision and the relationship he intends to maintain with countries united by a shared—and often complex—history. And it is precisely this complexity that makes the current moment particularly delicate. The king is, in fact, preparing for a trip to the Caribbean, scheduled for October 27 through November 4, that will include stops in the Bahamas, Guyana, and Antigua and Barbuda.

This visit comes as the debate over Britain’s colonial past, slavery, and calls for economic reparations continues to take center stage in relations between London and several Caribbean nations.

For some time now, however, Prince William has been associated with the idea of a more streamlined monarchy, with a smaller number of members engaged in official duties and a greater focus on the causes he considers priorities, from the environment to mental health. In this context, a potential relinquishment of the leadership of the Commonwealth could be interpreted as a further sign of change. This does not necessarily mean distancing himself from member countries, but rather a different interpretation of the role the British monarch should play in relation to them.

After all, the Commonwealth does not consist solely of territories that recognize Charles as head of state. It also includes numerous republics, and his leadership does not entail governing powers over member countries. William’s potential selection would therefore raise a primarily symbolic question: Who could represent the organization after Charles, and what relationship would they wish to maintain with the monarchy?

For Charles, who is deeply attached to the Crown’s international role and to continuity with his mother’s reign, the prospect of being the last Windsor to lead the Commonwealth would hold historical significance. For William, on the other hand, it could represent a way to distinguish the constitutional responsibilities of the future sovereign from those belonging to a particular phase of British history.

For now, this is merely a scenario, not a succession that has already been decided. But the question raised by The Times goes beyond an institutional role: It concerns how the British monarchy intends to redefine its presence in the world. The future King William will certainly inherit a crown. But it may be precisely what he chooses not to inherit that best reflects the monarchy to come.
