#3677. I Tested ChatGPT's New 'Slides' Tool, and Here's How It Compares to Gemini

David Nield is a technology journalist from Manchester in the U.K. who has been writing about gadgets and apps for more than 20 years.

He has a bachelor's degree in English Literature from Durham University, where he also spent a term as editor of the award-winning student newspaper Palatinate. His journalism career started in print media, where he contributed to and edited several technology magazines and bookazines sold in the U.K. and internationally. More recently, he has worked as a freelancer for some of the biggest technology publications on the web, covering everything from on-the-ground reporting about product launches, to detailed explainers and how-to guides on apps, gadgets, and platforms. His expertise covers broad areas of consumer tech, including smartphones, laptops, wearables, and AI.

ChatGPT's capabilities continue to expand apace, and the latest feature is a fully capable slideshow creator. Announced a few weeks ago, ChatGPT Slides lets you create PowerPoint-style decks from inside your conversations, and it's now appearing for those on the Pro tier and above (meaning right now, you need to spend at least $100 a month to get it).

If you're on the free ChatGPT plan or the $20-a-month Plus tier, you can still get ChatGPT to generate downloadable PowerPoint files from the content of your conversations. However, they'll be more basic than those made in the full version of ChatGPT Slides, and there are no collaboration or native edit options.

ChatGPT Slides is part of OpenAI's push to give users a complete office suite inside the ChatGPT apps. Pages (the word processor) is already available for Pro tiers and above, while a spreadsheet app is on the way—the idea being that you will soon be able to get all your work done without any help from Google or Microsoft.

While Chatgpt Slides is only available on pricey plans right now, it's likely to come to lower subscription tiers in the future. But for now, how does the tool compare to what's available in the free and Plus tiers, not to mention what you can do with Gemini inside Google Slides? I've taken a detailed look.

When it comes to testing out AI tools, it helps to focus on something you're already knowledgeable about so you can spot any mistakes. To test this, I decided to see what ChatGPT could hoover up from the web about the new iPhone Duo. If you're on the free or Plus plans, you can start a conversation, then ask for a slideshow or PowerPoint about it.

Plus accounts also have access to a specific Presentations add-on, available via the + (plus) menu in the prompt box, though I get the impression this feature is in a state of flux since ChatGPT Slides arrived. In any case, you can begin asking about the iPhone Duo specs and early online reactions, and then say "build me a slideshow on the key features and reviews so far".

What I got back on the $20 Plus account was a fairly competent slideshow on Apple's foldable, with accurate information as far as I could see, and a simple series of layouts. It looked like something you might whip up from a template if you were in a rush and didn't have time to properly align all the bullet points or be very selective about which details you included and which you left out. One neat touch is that ChatGPT was able to drop in an official photo of the iPhone Duo at the start of the slideshow, but I had to specifically ask for one.

You can go back and make specific edits to the slideshow, but you have to describe them in the general prompt box—you can't select individual slide elements and make more precise changes. Still, if all you want to do is occasionally convert your ChatGPT conversations into a rough and ready slideshow, there's no real need to upgrade from the free or Plus accounts for now.

Switching to a Pro account, I gave a similar task to ChatGPT Slides hoping for something more comprehensive. To begin with, Slides gets its own dedicated area in the app, so you can start up a blank presentation from scratch—there are some basic image and text features, but the idea is to use prompts to build your slide deck. It's like a really basic Google Slides, with a prompt box.

Creating a slideshow this way feels rather tedious: Typing out what you want to say and then waiting for ChatGPT to do it isn't as intuitive as just throwing in some graphics and text yourself, as you might do in PowerPoint. It also seems to be a bit buggy, at least during the time I spent testing it, with some requests not properly completing.

Starting a standard conversation with ChatGPT and then turning all the information into a deck is much more useful from my perspective. My iPhone Duo slideshow was longer, laid out better, and more informative than the one I created on the Plus plan—there were even charts of battery life and official photos this time.

However, whether I used the free, Plus, or Pro tools, my presentations didn't come across as particularly well thought through. They don't really flow smoothly, either across individual slides or the slideshow as a whole—it feels very much like an algorithm, rather than a person, has decided what information to include and where on the slides to stick it.

Whether you create presentations in chats or through the ChatGPT Slides space, you're able to click on individual elements and do some basic manual resizing and repositioning. You can also request edits via prompts: "Drop in an image of a cat", "make this pop more", "center all of this information", or whatever it is.

ChatGPT Slides does offer collaborative tools, which I wasn't able to test, and I can see that being useful for teams heavily invested in ChatGPT's ecosystem. I can also imagine ChatGPT Slides coming in handy when you've got a lot of information you need to quickly dump into a slideshow that looks vaguely presentable.

In general though, I think I'd much rather stick to the traditional way of making slideshows for now. These old methods are more precise and more manageable, and give you full control over what's shown and where—even if they do take a bit longer. What's more, modern-day presentation tools come with so many templates and other layout aids that it's not difficult to put together something that's easy on the eyes while including all of the information you think is most critical.

Google is, of course, also all the way in on AI (and then some), and if you pay for Google AI Pro ($19.99 a month) or above, you can have Gemini pop up right inside Google Slides to give you some assistance with your presentation-making. To compare the tools, I gave it the same task as I did ChatGPT, asking for a slide deck on the new iPhone Duo.

Gemini actually asked some questions along the way: about wht visual style to use, the audience to aim the slideshow at, and the details to highlight. Though it's always possible to put this sort of information manually into a prompt, this would be a helpful additional to ChatGPT Slides.

What Gemini and Google Slides produced was impressive in terms of the layout and overall vibe of the presentation: It felt much more like a human being had put together the slide deck compared to what ChatGPT had produced, in terms of the information chosen, the way the slides were laid out, and the color picks.

However, Google's result had its faults too: The presentation got the facts on the maximum brightness of the iPhone Duo screen wrong, and produced an "iPhone Duo" graphic that looked like two iPhones from around 2021 stuck together. ChatGPT Slides at least managed to pull official imagery from the Apple website.

You can't attach individual edit prompts to specific parts of your presentation in Google Slides as you can in ChatGPT Slides—you have to mention them in the general prompt box. That said, Google Slides is obviously a much more mature and established slideshow tool compared to the OpenAI equivalent, in terms of its features and layout options (it has been around for a decade, after all).

Given that extra software structure and the lower cost of entry, Gemini and Google Slides is the better option for AI-assisted slideshow making at the moment—though I wouldn't bet against OpenAI catching up. For now, ChatGPT Slides is something that's good to have available if you're already paying a lot for ChatGPT every month, but not a feature that makes it worth your while to swap your AI allegiance.

#3674. How Two Rival Columnists Created Hollywood’s Gossip Machine — and Taught America to Worship Attention

In her new book, “Hollywood Whispers: The Untold Story of the Original Gossip Queens, Louella Parsons and Hedda Hopper,” Eileen Cope delves into a rivalry that dramatically shaped the entertainment industry and culture at large throughout the mid-20th century. After the birth of the star system in Hollywood, Parsons and Hopper rose to prominence as gossip columnists who could make and break careers, and choose to keep or expose celebrities’ secrets. With a combined readership of 75 million at their peak, Hopper and Parsons wielded extraordinary influence — and instilled paralyzing fear. As columnist Liz Smith has written, “The studios created both of them. And they thought they could control both of them. But they became Frankenstein monsters escaped from the labs.” In the excerpt below, Cope summarizes their stark differences — and even more striking similarities. Related Stories What the Success of ’28 Weeks Later’ Meant for an ‘Indiewood’ Still Finding Its Footing What’s It Like to Be a Hair and Fur Fabricator for Laika? A New Book Will Answer That (and More)

Hollywood has always been a hall of mirrors. What began as an industry of light and illusion had, by the 1940s, become a machinery of control—an empire built on fantasy, running on fear. Its dreammakers no longer needed scripts or cameras to keep order. They had Louella Parsons and Hedda Hopper.

By then, both women had transcended the ordinary limits of journalism. They were not simply reporters; they were gatekeepers of belief, the quiet regulators of a nation’s imagination. To cross them was to risk vanishing—not merely from the screen, but from the collective story America told about itself.

Hollywood insiders referred to them—half jokingly, half in dread—as “the High Priestesses.” Studio secretaries clipped their columns before the morning dailies reached the lot, marking any mention of the studio’s stars with red pencil. Publicists phoned assistants in panic if a name appeared in the wrong context. “They could wreck you before lunch,” remembered producer Pandro Berman. “And by dinner, they’d make you again—if it suited them.”

It was easy, at first, to mistake their power for personality. Louella, with her sanctimonious charm and the backing of William Randolph Hearst, ruled the studios from her desk at the Los Angeles Examiner. Her typewriter was a throne. Her column ran like clockwork through America’s breakfast tables, a sermon of what was right and who had sinned. Hedda, the actress-turned-crusader, cultivated a different tone—brassy, righteous, vengeful. Her voice carried across radio waves like a call to arms. She didn’t just write gossip; she announced it, her cadence turning every name into an accusation or an absolution.

Both women understood theater. Louella played the role of moral mother, her column written in the soothing cadences of propriety—rarely venomous on the surface, yet devastating in its implications. Hedda was the showwoman. “When she walked into Ciro’s,” recalled gossip photographer Frank Worth, “it was like someone had switched on the lights.” Even those who loathed her found themselves glancing up, afraid she might be looking at them.

Louella and Hedda were two sides of the same coin, minted in a culture that worshipped celebrity and feared moral chaos. Each understood what the studios feared most: scandal, exposure, the sudden rupture of the illusion. And they learned to trade in that fear, turning rumor into regulation.

Louella had built her empire first. By the early 1930s, her column appeared in hundreds of newspapers through the Hearst syndicate, reaching an estimated twenty million readers daily. Hollywood Hotel, her weekly radio show, brought the glamour of opening nights directly into American living rooms. Its mix of studio orchestras, star appearances, and previews of upcoming films created a powerful cycle of cross-promotion: her print column drove listeners to the broadcast, and the broadcast reinforced her authority in print.

She was feared as much as courted. Stars who crossed her found themselves frozen out. Studio executives who annoyed her might find their films receiving tepid coverage. A single line hinting that an actor appeared “unhappy with recent assignments” could trigger studio meetings and contract reviews. Louella’s prose was brief, polite, and devastatingly coded; her readers learned to interpret her tone as carefully as her text. The fear she inspired was not only of what she printed but of what she might choose not to.

The competition she could not have predicted came in the form of a woman she had once treated as a harmless curiosity. By 1935, Hedda Hopper’s film career was essentially over. She was fifty, though she claimed forty-five, watching her savings evaporate and terrified about her future. When a studio executive suggested she could “transition to extra work,” Hedda turned the humiliation into fuel. If she couldn’t star in movies, she would make herself the story. The weapon she would wield was not beauty or youth, but the pen.

When a friend suggested she try writing a gossip column, Hedda was insulted. Writing gossip seemed like a public acknowledgment that she couldn’t make it as a performer. But desperation has a way of making humiliation seem acceptable. She borrowed a typewriter, set up at her kitchen table, and began by writing what she knew best: the petty rivalries of actors who had underestimated her. “I never lied,” she said later, “but I didn’t always tell the truth either.” The early columns were messy but alive, full of energy and vengeance.

Then Hedda got angry. She stopped trying to write like a journalist and started writing like herself— sharp, critical, unsparing, willing to say things other columnists wouldn’t dare print. Twenty years as a working actress had also given her something valuable: relationships with the people Louella ignored. Character actors, crew members, hairdressers, chauffeurs, and wardrobe girls had no loyalty to Louella, and many were happy to talk to Hedda, who had once been one of them.

The Los Angeles Times noticed. Hearst’s primary competitor in Southern California saw Hedda as a weapon against his dominance through Louella. In 1938, the paper offered Hedda a contract with better pay, more resources, and a much larger platform. She would compete directly with the woman who had been Hollywood’s undisputed gossip queen for fifteen years.

Most people thought she would be crushed. Instead, Hedda transformed herself for the opportunity. She began wearing the enormous hats that became her trademark—creations decorated with feathers, flowers, fruit, and even stuffed animals. The hats ensured that everyone at a Hollywood event knew when Hedda Hopper had arrived. She also developed a confrontational style that contrasted with Louella’s cozy approach. Where Louella praised, Hedda criticized. Where Louella protected relationships, Hedda burned bridges. Where Louella sought to be loved, Hedda was content to be feared.

In the beginning, the difference between them seemed simple enough. Louella had the empire; Hedda had the instinct. Louella’s authority came from her link to Hearst—a patriarchal structure she both served and mastered. Hedda’s came from sheer velocity. She moved fast, wrote fast, spoke fast. She had learned that outrage was more contagious than affection.

At first, the two women coexisted uneasily, even politely. Louella sent Hedda a congratulatory telegram when her first radio broadcast went national. Hedda responded with a bouquet of orchids and a note that read, “To the Queen of the Kingdom I am only visiting.” But the civility evaporated as soon as Hedda’s column began pulling readers away from the Examiner. In 1940, when Hedda’s column syndication reached more than six million households, Louella persuaded Hearst’s editors to bar her rival from every Hearst-owned paper.

As the 1940s unfolded, their feud became public theater. Columns answered columns. Louella hinted that Hedda was “overly dramatic.” Hedda fired back, calling Louella “a relic of studio servitude.” Studios courted both women, sometimes feeding them different versions of the same story to see who would publish first. Readers began to choose sides.

In 1945, Photoplay published “Battle of the Typewriters,” complete with caricatures of the two women as dueling queens—Louella seated on a gilt throne, Hedda brandishing her hatpins like weapons. The story outsold every issue that year except the one featuring Clark Gable’s return from the war. The public couldn’t get enough. For a nation recovering from global conflict, the spectacle of two powerful women waging a cold war of words was irresistible.

Their methods echo through the media that followed. The tabloid boom of the 1980s, TMZ, Instagram, celebrity influencers, blind-item accounts, and the daily social-media outrage cycle all trace their lineage to the template Louella and Hedda built. They understood that access could be traded, intimacy manufactured, morality weaponized, and a public image constructed or destroyed through repetition. The machine they built continues to hum behind every red carpet and every scandal recycled for entertainment. What they truly enshrined was attention—and America never stopped worshiping it.

The women themselves could not stop Hollywood from moving on. As the studio system declined and stars emerged, the two gossip queens became relics of the world they had once governed. When the Los Angeles Examiner folded in 1962, Louella suffered a professional blow. Already showing signs of physical deterioration, she nevertheless continued going out, bejeweled and bewildered, like a dowager empress whose country had overthrown her rule. She finally retired in 1965, beset by further medical problems.

Hedda had always advised leaving a party before the glow faded. On February 1, 1966, she died in Los Angeles of double pneumonia at the age of eighty. Her cryptic parting words were entirely in character: “They should know what I haven’t written!”

Louella’s daughter, Harriet, visited her mother at the Santa Monica nursing home to deliver the news that Hedda Hopper was dead. Louella sat in profound silence, then appeared confused. She would live for another six years, largely withdrawn from the world she had once ruled, before dying of arteriosclerosis on December 9, 1972, at the age of ninety-one. But on hearing that her great rival had finally departed, Louella gathered herself long enough to offer one last, unmistakable verdict: “GOOD!”

Adapted from “Hollywood Whispers: The Untold Story of the Original Gossip Queens, Louella Parsons and Hedda Hopper” available November 3 from Lyons Press.

#3673. Hot Mom Bods -- Guess The A-List Star Bustin' Out of Her Orange One Piece

#3672. This '70s Phenomenon Led To The Cancellation Of Several Promising Western Shows

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

When "Gunsmoke" was abruptly canceled after a 20-year run in 1975, it was already somewhat of an anachronism. When the legendary Western series emerged in 1955, the small screen oater was entering a golden age built upon the success of shows such as the late-'40s TV Western featuring now forgotten actor William Boyd, "Hopalong Cassidy." By the mid-'70s, however, the TV landscape was almost unrecognizable. Much of that was due to a phenomenon known as the "rural purge" which saw networks pivoting away from rural-focused shows to deliver urban-based, socially aware entertainment. Viewed from that perspective, it was amazing "Gunsmoke" held on as long as it did. But that wasn't the only Western felled by the rise of urban-focused TV.

By the end of the 1960s the Western genre was already undergoing a major evolution. Gone were the days of John Wayne's simplistic Western heroes. In 1969, films such as Sam Peckinpah's "The Wild Bunch" and Sergio Leone's "Once Upon a Time in the West" were heralding the arrival of the revisionist era, and over the next decade Western films would present a much more irreverent and morally complex vision of the American West.

Meanwhile, TV was undergoing its own revolution. In 1970, there were still several Western series on the air. "Gunsmoke" was one example, but there was also the similarly long-running "Bonanza," as well as James Drury's "The Virginian" and "Death Valley Days." By the mid 1970s, however, they would all be canceled due to a series of factors entirely out of their control.

Leif Erickson's underrated Western series "The High Chaparral" began airing in 1967. Hailing from "Bonanza" creator David Dortort, the show was a hit but lasted nowhere near as long as "Bonanza," wrapping up in March 1971. It was far from the only TV show to get the axe that year.

As Pat Buttram of "Green Acres" famously put it, 1971 was "the year CBS canceled everything with a tree." The network had become well-known for capitalizing on the popularity of rural comedies throughout the 1960s, debuting such beloved shows as "The Beverly Hillbillies," "The Andy Griffith Show," "Petticoat Junction," "Hee-Haw," and Buttram's "Green Acres." CBS wasn't the only network to jump on the trend, either. It started with ABC's "The Real McCoys" which began airing in 1957 and functioned as an almost prototypical "Beverly Hillbillies," focusing on an Appalachian family who moves to the San Fernando Valley. By the end of the 1960s, however, all these shows were put on the chopping block. What happened? Well, the predilections of one CBS executive seem to be a big part of it.

Sara K. Eskridge, author of "Rube Tube: CBS and Rural Comedy in the Sixties" spoke to Woman's World about how much of the rural purge can be traced back to CBS programming chief Fred Silverman, who arrived in the late 1960s and started cleaning house. "He was looking at the data, which was not really something that had been done to any real extent before this," she explained. "He realizes only old people watch those [rural comedy-type] shows. They'd been on the air for more than 10 years, starting to lose favor with the younger crowd, so he cancels them."

Sara K. Eskridge noted to Woman's World how Fred Silverman seemed to have a personal animus toward rural shows, which he'd previously expressed in his masters thesis. When he came to cancel those series en masse, then, many of them were still garnering significant ratings. "These are shows that were in the top 10, most of them in the top 20 when they were canceled," Eskridge said. As far as Silverman was concerned, however, they brought in a "lower class of viewers" and had to go.

Silverman's approach caught on. Suddenly networks were cancelling everything "with a tree in it," to use Pat Buttram's phrase. The mass cull came to a head at the end of the 1970–71 television season, where rural series were suddenly replaced by a new raft shows epitomized by the likes of "All in the Family" and "The Mary Tyler Moore Show." Meanwhile, Westerns found themselves lumped in with the "rural" series, and suddenly "The Virginian," which had only just welcomed Lee Majors as a new addition to its cast, and "The High Chaparral" were gone. "Bonanza" lasted until 1973, before James Arness' Marshall Matt Dillon was told to hand in his badge in 1975 when "Gunsmoke" was canceled.

But TV Westerns didn't die altogether. The same year James Drury and Leif Erickson were told to pack it up, ABC greenlit "Alias Jones and Smith," a show about two outlaw cousins trying to live on the straight and narrow. "Little House on the Prairie" arrived in 1974, and "Maverick" star James Garner began fronting an offbeat Western about a pacifist sheriff in "Nichols." Otherwise, however, TV had purged rural entertainment from the airwaves, and the cowboys were, for the first time since the 1950s, well and truly outnumbered.

#3671. Photographer Gordon Parks’s Best Shots, From Ingrid Bergman to Muhammad Ali

Everything looks good in the art dealer Jack Shainman’s Tribeca gallery, located in the banking hall of the former New York Life Insurance Company building—complete with a freestanding marble bank vault, which has been sitting in the same landmarked space since the end of the 19th century. Each time Shainman conceives a show for the gallery—appearing around the Corinthian columns, beneath the marble wainscoting—he wants to make sure it’s something unprecedented.

Since 2018, Shainman has collaborated with the Gordon Parks Foundation, an organization dedicated to preserving the work of the New York legend who shot some of the most iconic images of the Civil Rights Era. The gallerist wanted to make his first Gordon Parks show in the marble former banking hall special—different from the last four solo Parks shows he’d put together in his Chelsea spaces.

“With Gordon, every time that I do it, I like to do it differently,” Shainman told me.

Ondria Tanner and Her Grandmother Window-shopping, Mobile, Alabama, 1956.

It was a brisk afternoon a few weeks after “Gordon Parks: Voices in the Mirror” opened, and he was walking me through the galleries, which will be full of iconic Parks photographs up through the first week of November.

“I did two shows back-to-back where we showed the fashion and all the unexpected things that people never knew that Gordon did,” Shainman said. “And then we flipped it to all the iconic images of the March on Washington. So it was really fun to show people that. I’m always combing through, looking for images that you didn’t know about. It’s harder to do than you think.”

For his fifth show of Parks images, Shainman asked a number of luminaries—those who worked with Parks, knew Parks, or posed for Parks—to contribute written texts on the great photographer. Ava DuVernay, who was a publicist for Parks’s 2000 documentary, Half Past Autumn, wrote one. So did two women close to his greatest muses: Lonnie Ali, Muhammad Ali’s widow, and Qubilah Shabazz, the daughter of Malcolm X.

Anderson Cooper also contributed a remembrance. I thought perhaps he participated due to his status as a well-known art collector, but he also has a more personal connection to Parks: His mother, the railroad heiress Gloria Vanderbilt, had a decades-long romantic relationship with the photographer. (It was nonmonogamous for at least some of the time, and they were open about being open.)

“He’s really cool with it, because he says how Gordon was such a ladies’ man,” said Shainman, speaking about Cooper. “I respect Anderson so much for just what he does, let alone that his mother was freaking Gloria Vanderbilt. We know him. He’s an amazing person.”

We walked by Parks’s images of the March on Washington, Malcolm X, Rosa Parks. There were incredible images of a small town, kids playing outside a small grocery store—images that have inspired younger artists such as Tyler Mitchell and Kendrick Lamar, who made an entire music video drawing from Gordon Parks photos.

Boy with June Bug, Fort Scott, Kansas, 1963.

There was also one of Parks’s iconic photos of Ingrid Bergman, shot on the island of Stromboli in 1949. At the time, Bergman had caused an international scandal by leaving her husband and small child for the director helming her upcoming picture, Roberto Rossellini. Rossellini was highly suspicious of all press and kicked most media members off the island while shooting his film, which was also called Stromboli. But at Bergman’s insistence, he let Parks stay, and the photographer captured images of the couple together.

As we walked past the images, Shainman mentioned to me that one of the three children Bergman had with Rossellini is the actor and filmmaker Isabella Rossellini.

“Gordon Parks’s photos of my parents were not affected by the gossip and scandal that swelled around them. My parents had fallen in love with each other before obtaining divorces. They were banned from the United States,” the younger Rossellini wrote in her exhibition contribution. “But Gordon Parks kept a firm eye on them as artists. With one photo alone he could capture the whole intent of their film Stromboli, that is, the cultural clashes that ravaged Italy after World War II. Parks had an incredible ability to synthesize complex realities in a single image.”

Whoopi Goldberg on the Set of The Color Purple, Anson County, North Carolina, 1985.

#3670. Pregnant Savannah Chrisley Defends "Huge" Age Gap With Fiancé

After Savannah Chrisley shared she is pregnant with her first baby, she detailed her and fiancé Jason's relationship, noting that despite their age gap, "His body is great. His mind is even sharper."

Savannah Chrisley knows best when it comes to her relationships.

As the Growing Up Chrisley alum announced she is pregnant with her first baby with her fiancé, whom she revealed is named Jason, she admitted they have a significant age gap.

"There is a huge age gap there," Savannah confirmed on the Oct. 27 episode of her Unlocked podcast. "That's probably part of the reason why I kept a lot of it quiet, because I know how brutal the public can be. But I genuinely do not care."

"I have always said, 'I would rather 20 phenomenal, unbelievable years with someone than 50 just meh years,'" she continued. "The amount of love and respect that we have—we have so many dreams, and we dream together, we work together."

The 29-year-old daughter of Todd Chrisley and Julie Chrisley—who split from Robert Shiver in 2025 after two years of dating—emphasized that age is just a number in their relationship.

"It's just such an amazing, fulfilling feeling to finally find your person," she reflected. "The age difference—so what? I don't care. He does not look his age, nor does he act his age. His body is great. His mind is even sharper."

"This person is the love of my life," she said. "It made all the heartbreak that I've gone through worth it. And all the heartbreak taught me something. It taught me what I want, what I don't want, what I should accept, what I shouldn't accept."

The Chrisley Knows Best star, who kept her partner's identity under wraps for the last year, also shared how peaceful her life has been with Jason by her side.

"Things have been really, really quiet," she explained. "I've really enjoyed it being that way. I feel like he and I have truly gotten to be just us and create our safe space and get to a really good place with each other."

"Our relationship as a whole has been absolutely amazing," she shared. "It has been the dream relationship that any woman could ever hope to be in. There hasn't been drama, and chaos and dysfunction. There has been absolutely none of that."

Instead, Savannah found a partner she could lean on through the highs and lows of this chapter.

"It feels so good to be able to be soft," she noted. "It feels so good to finally meet your partner to where you don't have to be that way anymore. You don't always have to be on guard. You don't always have to be the one fixing everything."

"When you find someone who's willing to do just as much as you will do in a relationship, it is the best feeling in the entire world," Savannah beamed. "We want to have life experiences with each other. That's when you know you have the right person, when you don't feel like you have to escape them at times. He is my best friend. We do everything together."

In Wicker, nothing about the Wicker Husband is left to the imagination. You can see everything, and let’s just say there’s a reason the poster for this movie was made to look like a Harlequin romance novel. The cast could also see everything, and one of Alexander Skarsgård’s co-stars explained the funny and relatable reaction she had when she saw him as the naked Wicker Husband while filming the project on the 2026 movie schedule.

To clarify, Skarsgård wasn’t naked as himself; he was naked as the Wicker Husband, which meant he was fully covered in wicker. However, he was still “stark naked as wicker,” as his co-star Elizabeth Debicki put it. So, how does one react to a sight like that? The Crown actress recalled her reaction during an interview with Variety:

I turned the corner, and I was like, ‘Hi, Alex!’ and I went bright red because my brain couldn’t compute what it was seeing. I was just like ‘Oh my God! Welcome to Budapest, I guess.’

Now, in the film, Wicker Husband goes full frontal in one scene, he’s in the water, and he’s pretty far away from the camera. However, you can see everything from his wicker abs to his wicker penis. Personally, I don’t know how I’d react to a sight like that if I were on this set. And I think Debicki, who played The Tailor’s Wife in the movie, had a very valid and relatable response to seeing her co-star in naked Wicker Husband mode.

I have to say, though, considering Wicker reviews highlight just how horny the movie is, I guess seeing all the wicker on set shouldn’t have come as a shock.

Anyway, this movie really is very romantic and spicy. Skarsgård’s Wicker Husband and Olivia Colman’s Fisherwoman have multiple sex scenes together. In fact, Colman explained that when they filmed those moments, it was actually helpful that the wicker suit was between them. She also said her trust in Skarsgård, their intimacy coordinator and the movie's directors, who are married, made the whole situation “so much fun.”

Overall, Wicker does not shy away from the intimate and sexual elements of the story. It also leans into the tender and sweet side of Wicker Husband and Fisherwoman’s relationship, and all around, this movie truly is a lovely romance.

Women are loving Wicker Husband too. Yes, he’s good-looking and fit. However, in a different interview, Colman and Debicki also noted that Skarsgård’s character is so attractive because he’s also a good listener. He’s very attentive, too, he cares deeply about the people he loves, and he won’t give you splinters. So, he’s the full package.

Well, with all that said, it was quite funny and relatable to hear how the cast reacted to seeing a fully naked Wicker Husband. It’s also been fun to see everyone else reacting to Wicker in all its horny and heartfelt glory. So, if you’d like to watch Wicker, you can catch the movie in theaters now.

#3668. This New Garmin Feature Is Draining Battery Life, but There's a Quick Fix

Garmin users are taking to the company's support forums with a common grievance: The newly introduced "OK Garmin" hands-free voice feature appears to be eating through battery life. The feature arrived through Garmin’s September software updates, bringing hands-free voice activation to the Fenix 8, Forerunner 570 and 970, Venu 4, and several other models. Here's how to make sure Garmin's new updates aren't slashing your watch's battery life.

Last month's updates for select watches also brought all-day fall detection, a set of new Quick Workouts and Garmin Run Coach plans, as well as that "OK Garmin" hands-free voice command feature. It's exactly what it sounds like: Instead of pressing a button, users can simply say “OK Garmin” to activate voice commands. However, it looks like "OK Garmin" in particular is what causes some watch's projected runtimes to drastically drop, allegedly by roughly 35% to 50% in some cases.

Garmin does acknowledge that enabling these settings shortens battery life, though it doesn't say by how much. The tradeoff makes sense, since the watch has to listen constantly for its wake phrase. Even so, giving up several days of runtime is a steep price for a feature that many people may seldom use. Luckily, the fix is simple: Turning off "OK Garmin" should restore your battery life estimates right away.

If your watch has been draining faster since the update, go to Settings > System > Voice and disable "OK Garmin." If needed, you can also switch off the Phone Assistant wake word from the same area. The good news is that turning this feature off won't even take voice commands away—you can still trigger them manually using the watch's physical controls.

Keep in mind that these numbers are battery estimates rather than independently tested, real-world runtimes. Some of the battery complaints that surfaced after the update may also stem from unrelated issues. Still, trading away as much as half your battery life just to avoid pressing a button is a difficult bargain to defend.

#3666. Luchino Visconti’s Tragic, Beautiful Final Film ‘L’Innocente’ Restored in 4K — Watch the Trailer

Luchino Visconti‘s lush, aristocratic worlds have seen a major revival at repertory movie houses over the past year. From the black-and-white “White Nights” with Marcello Mastroianni to the psychosexual queer drama “Conversation Piece” with Burt Lancaster, the Italian grandmaster’s vivid melodramas capture souls adrift, possessive, and tragic. While Visconti is probably best known for epics like “The Leopard” and “Rocco and His Brothers,” in his later career up until his death in 1976, the Palme d’Or-winning filmmaker turned his lens on more intimate dramas and marital discord.

A new restoration of Visconti’s final film, “L’Innocente,” premiered in Cannes Classics and at the New York Film Festival. Starring Giancarlo Giannini, Laura Antonelli, and Jennifer O’Neill, the film was lovingly restored by Film Movement Classics to preserve the film’s 50th anniversary. Below, IndieWire debuts the exclusive trailer for the new release. A theatrical rollout begins December 11. Related Stories Watch ‘My Undesirable Friends’ Director Julia Loktev Recall the Week Russia Invaded Ukraine ‘Wildwood’ Sparked a Much-Needed Comeback for Laika at the Box Office

Set in Rome at the turn of the 20th century, the film centers on “a wealthy and self-absorbed nobleman Tullio Hermil (Giancarlo Giannini) [who] carries on an affair with a sophisticated yet possessive widow, Countess Teresa Raffo (Jennifer O’Neill), while treating his devoted wife, Giuliana (Laura Antonelli), with cold condescension. Wounded by his years of neglect, Giuliana takes a lover of her own with young novelist, Filippo (Marc Porel). When she becomes pregnant with Filippo’s child, Tullio’s wounded pride cannot be contained, setting off a chain of events leading to ultimate tragedy.”

The film loosely adapts Gabriele D’Annunzio’s 1892 novel. At the time of production, Visconti had suffered a major stroke a few years prior that didn’t necessarily impact his ability to work, but it made him a liability in terms of securing casting. For “L’Innocente,” Visconti had wanted his “The Leopard” and “Rocco and His Brothers” star, the French actor Alain Delon, in the lead. He also wanted Delon’s ex and repeat Visconti star Romy Schneider in the film, too, as well as Charlotte Rampling (“The Damned”) at one point. Visconti died in March 1976, just two months ahead of the film’s Cannes premiere. Critic Jonathan Rosenbaum called the film “a withering autocritique of masculine vanity and self-delusion.”Cinelab Service in Italy created a new 4K digital transfer on an Arriscan film scanner, working from the original camera negative.

#3665. Cillian Murphy's Scarecrow Holds A Unique Record Among Batman Movie Villains

Which Batman villain has appeared in the most movies? Unsurprisingly, it's the Joker. As of 2026, the Clown Prince of Crime has appeared in nine theatrically-released films (live-action and animation), played by eight different actors. Close behind the Joker in frequency are Catwoman, the Riddler, and the Penguin. All four of these villains have a leg up: Together, they're the most famous villains from the Adam West "Batman" show, and they formed a quartet of evil in the 1966 "Batman" spin-off movie. But there's another villain who never made it to the Adam West show yet has a similar record.

That'd be Jonathan Crane/the Scarecrow. After Joel Schumacher's third Batman film (intended to feature Scarecrow) fell through, Crane finally made it to the silver screen as one of two main villains in Christopher Nolan's "Batman Begins." Crane escapes at the end of the movie, setting up a sequence early in the sequel, "The Dark Knight," when Batman (Christian Bale) busts Scarecrow during a drug deal with the mob. Come trilogy capper "The Dark Knight Rises," Bane (Tom Hardy) turns Gotham into a city ruled by the criminals — with the freed Crane serving as judge of any dissident.

Scarecrow is the only villain to appear in every chapter of the "Dark Knight" trilogy. That also gives the character a unique record among Batman movie villains: He's appeared not just in three films, but three films in a row, without a recast or a reboot. "Batman Begins" was the first time that Nolan and Murphy worked together, a partnership that eventually yielded multiple Oscar wins come 2023's "Oppenheimer."

But despite the recurring appearances, has Scarecrow gotten his laurels? Even with his supporting role across the Nolan films, there's never been a Batman movie with Scarecrow as the primary villain.

In "Batman Begins," Crane is subordinate to Ra's al Ghul (Liam Neeson). The League of Shadows provides Crane with the plants he needs to make his fear toxin, and it's the League's plan to destroy Gotham by spreading that toxin across the city. Scarecrow winds up as a glorified henchman, with the pathos of a villain as Batman's foil all saved for Ra's.

Frankly, this is a problem that Scarecrow has faced across "Batman" media. Even the superlative "Batman: The Animated Series" didn't use him that much. Scarecrow has a sadistic compulsion to scare people and an academic's interest in studying fear's effects, i.e. qualities that are hard to make sympathetic. The best Batman villains are usually tragic monsters, and Scarecrow doesn't quite fit —but some of the "Batman" stories that use Scarecrow the best suggest this monster doesn't need a tragedy.

In a red carpet interview with Deadline, "Clayface" star Tom Rhys Harries suggested that Scarecrow should be the next Batman villain to get his own movie: "You could do some kind of like 'Inception,' the audience [doesn't] know what's real kind of thing." But I'd push back a little. Scarecrow is best when he's a way to bring out Batman's fears, and there has to be someone to overcome the fear he inspires. A recent iteration of Scarecrow, and one that shows both that and Cillian Murphy's lasting legacy in shaping the villain, comes from Scott Snyder and Nick Dragotta's "Absolute Batman."

"Absolute" Scarecrow is the villain of the arc "The Straw Man," and his design somewhat resembles Murphy-as-Crane in the "Dark Knight" trilogy. He wears a jacket and tie, resembling a business suit instead of the farmer-like clothes of a typical Scarecrow.

What sets "Absolute" Scarecrow apart from Cillian Murphy's is his face. He has buttons stitched over his eyes, with similar stitching across his mouth. Crane even hints that his Scarecrow "mask" isn't really a mask.

Some Batman fans have noted the lanky "Absolute" Scarecrow looks even more like another one of Murphy's roles: J. Robert Oppenheimer. Scarecrow's suit and dress shirt outfit is a classical early 20th Century style, he wears a wide-brimmed pork pie hat, and he's rarely seen without a cigarette — as was Oppenheimer, in film and reality. If an "Oppenheimer plus Scarecrow" design is what Nick Dragotta was going for, that's a clever meta approach. But is that all there is to it?

"Absolute" Scarecrow is less about terrorizing individual people and more about spreading paranoia. In "Absolute Batman" #20, Scarecrow explains why he does what he does: It's because the modern day is "an age of dread," one where people have to compartmentalize slow-moving disasters and go about "fearing things too big to comprehend let alone affect." For a fear-fascinated sadist like Crane, that's heaven. What created the "age of dread"? I'd argue Oppenheimer's atomic bomb, for it birthed a world where one day, by accident or impulse, everyone might die in a nuclear fire.

"The Straw Man" casts Crane in a role similar to his part in "Batman Begins." This Scarecrow is still only a pawn of a greater villain (Jack "The Joker" Grimm), and the comic never invites sympathy for him. Batman ultimately shuts out the doubts and fears Crane planted in his head, then leaves him strung up on a cross like a real Scarecrow. It's as fitting a punishment as Murphy's Crane getting a taste of his own fear toxin in "Batman Begins."

#3664. Wave Life Sciences (WVE) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Wave Life Sciences, Inc. (WVE +7.48%) reported progress across its RNA medicine pipeline, specifically in its RNAi and RNA editing programs for obesity and alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency. The company reported a net loss of $61.9 million for the quarter while maintaining a cash runway through the third quarter of 2028. Management confirmed alignment with the FDA on a registrational pathway for WVE-006, which includes a single pivotal trial for potential accelerated and full approval. In its obesity program, the company initiated combination studies with tirzepatide and expects multiple clinical readouts, including 600 mg data for both WVE-006 and WVE-007, in the fourth quarter of 2026.

Operator: Hello, and welcome to Wave Life Sciences' third quarter 2026 earnings call. We ask that you please hold all questions until the completion of the formal remarks, at which time you'll be given instructions for the question-and-answer session. Also, as a reminder, this conference is being recorded today. I will now turn the call over to Kate Rausch, Vice President of Corporate Affairs and Investor Relations.

Kate Rausch: Thank you, operator, and good morning to everyone on the call. Earlier this morning, we issued a press release outlining our third quarter 2026 earnings update. Joining me today with prepared remarks are Paul Bolno, President and Chief Executive Officer, Chris Wright, Chief Medical Officer, and Kyle Moran, Chief Financial Officer. Also on the call are Chandra Vargeese, Chief Technology Officer, Erik Ingelsson, Chief Scientific Officer, as well as our new Chief Business Officer, Monika Vnuk, who joined Wave last month. The press release issued this morning is available on the investor section of our website, www.wavelifesciences.com. Before we begin, I'd like to remind you that discussions during this conference call will include forward-looking statements.

These statements are subject to several risks and uncertainties that could cause our actual results to differ materially from those described in these forward-looking statements. The factors that could cause actual results to differ are discussed in the press release issued today and in our SEC filings. We undertake no obligation to update or revise any forward-looking statement for any reason. I'd now like to turn the call over to Paul.

Paul Bolno: Thanks, Kate, and good morning to everyone joining us on today's call. At Wave, we are harnessing the convergence of deep genetic insights with our proprietary chemistry to rapidly advance a pipeline of transformational RNA medicines. Our broadly enabled oligonucleotide platform has leading capabilities in both siRNA and RNA editing that are now translating across both our wholly owned pipeline and in our collaboration with GSK. In siRNA, our best-in-class SpiNA design is demonstrating the potency and durability that we believe are needed to unlock important genetically validated targets. We've seen this translate clinically with WVE-007, and during the third quarter, GSK selected an siRNA development candidate to transition into its pipeline.

Next week, at Research Day, we'll take a deeper look at these capabilities and how we are continuing to push the boundaries of our proprietary chemistry across hepatic and extrahepatic siRNA, RNA editing, and our emerging bifunctional modalities. I'll begin today by discussing WVE-007, our INHBE siRNA for obesity, before moving to WVE-006, our RNA editing therapeutic for AATD, and other recent updates. The INHBE target is strongly supported by human genetics. Individuals who have a protective loss-of-function variant in one copy of the INHBE gene have a healthier cardiometabolic profile, including less abdominal fat, less liver disease, lower triglycerides, and lower risk of type 2 diabetes and cardiovascular disease.

With WVE-007, the goal is to replicate this healthy metabolic profile by silencing INHBE and lowering serum activin E, thereby taking the brakes off lipolysis or fat metabolism. WVE-007 offers a distinct therapeutic approach from incretins by driving fat loss without muscle loss. This makes it a differentiated alternative to GLP-1s or other incretins, a potentially powerful combination with incretins. Importantly, when we think about the challenges of sustaining chronic long-term incretin therapy, WVE-007 offers new potential to prevent weight regain with just a once or twice a year dose. There are several reasons we chose to target INHBE mRNA directly and turn off activin E production rather than the receptor of activin E, ALK7.

Turning off protein production in hepatocytes, the upstream source with a GalNAc siRNA, is the most efficient and durable way to impact this pathway. Suppressing activin E is a more selective approach with a lower risk of unintended consequences as compared to disabling a receptor that transduces signals via multiple ligands across different tissues. We believe this selectivity is especially important for long-term safety and clinical translation. WVE-007's unique ability to durably suppress INHBE is enabled by our proprietary chemistry and SpiNA siRNA design, which distinguishes us from others in the field and has been shown to enhance interactions with AGO2, stabilize the loaded RISC complex, and improve liver exposure.

Across INHBE and other targets, our proprietary chemistry has repeatedly delivered greater potency and more durable effects than industry-leading siRNA designs, setting us apart from others attempting similar silencing approaches. With WVE-007, we have now seen the compelling INHBE biology translate both pre-clinically and clinically, with differentiated data from other approaches in the field. Pre-clinically, our INHBE silencing drove substantial visceral and subcutaneous fat loss with muscle preservation and GLP-1-like weight loss in the DIO mouse model. Our pre-clinical data also demonstrate INHBE silencing increases adipocyte lipolysis, shrinks adipocytes, reduces pro-inflammatory macrophages and fibrosis, and improves insulin sensitivity, all key indicators of better cardiometabolic health, which further validate the human genetics.

In the clinic, we're seeing this profile translate in our phase I single-dose study, with clinically meaningful reductions in visceral and total fat and prevention of lean mass. We have also observed a favorable tolerability profile and durable dose-dependent activin E suppression, supporting once or twice yearly dosing, further highlighting the advantages of our SpiNA siRNA design. Our upcoming 600-mg data from the phase I portion of INLIGHT is expected this quarter and will help further characterize potency, duration, as well as safety and tolerability. We continue to make significant progress advancing WVE-007 across three distinct treatment settings: monotherapy, combination therapy, and as post-incretin maintenance therapy to prevent weight recurrence.

With our ongoing phase II-A monotherapy trial, we are now evaluating WVE-007 in our target patient population, individuals with higher BMI and comorbidities. In other words, a typical population observed in phase II and III obesity trials. These patients are expected to have greater excess fat at baseline and will receive multiple doses of WVE-007. With more excess fat to lose, we expect both visceral and total fat loss with WVE-007 to be substantially more pronounced in our phase II-A higher BMI participants. Clinical experience also highlights the importance of baseline adiposity. Leaner individuals without comorbidities typically show more modest fat reductions versus individuals with higher baseline adiposity.

Collectively, these factors support the potential of this phase II-A study to cross the one-year regulatory threshold of 5% weight loss. This monotherapy trial will also include multiple assessments of cardiometabolic biomarkers, including liver fat, which have the ability to de-risk development pathways in MASH and other cardiometabolic diseases. The second part of our strategy is to evaluate WVE-007 as a combination therapy with incretins, which has the potential to deepen weight loss and enhance metabolic benefits. During the quarter, we made strong progress on this initiative with the initiation of our phase II-A combination trial, which is evaluating WVE-007 in combination with weekly doses of tirzepatide in individuals with BMIs between 35 and 50 and type 2 diabetes.

We believe that WVE-007's orthogonal mechanism, ability to drive reductions in fat while preserving muscle, and favorable safety profile are well-suited to a combination approach. Despite the commercial success of incretins, there remains a clear opportunity for orthogonal approaches. Incretins reduce body weight through both fat loss and muscle loss, and they carry high discontinuation rates due to GI side effects, limiting the potential for long-term health benefits. Muscle loss associated with current standard of care therapies is substantial, representing upwards of 40% of the total weight loss. The consequence of muscle loss is particularly notable as skeletal muscle plays an important role in both metabolism and weight maintenance.

We believe WVE-007 could enable GLP-1 down titration and help patients achieve fat loss with a more favorable tolerability profile and less muscle loss. In addition to mitigating muscle loss and improving tolerability, WVE-007 also garners the potential to help patients achieve further fat loss on top of incretins, as up to 40% of patients don't reach treatment goals. Our preclinical data provide compelling support for WVE-007's ability to help these patients, as we've observed approximately two-fold greater weight loss as an add-on to incretins versus incretins alone. Lastly, and perhaps one of the most exciting and unique opportunities, we plan to evaluate WVE-007 in the maintenance setting to enable an off-ramp for GLP-1s.

We often hear from patients about the fear of weight regain post-cessation of incretins. Up to 70% of patients discontinue GLP-1 therapy within the first year of treatment, and for many, it's due to the challenges that WVE-007 may address, including treatment burden and tolerability issues. Notably, following discontinuation of GLP-1 treatment, upwards of 70% of lost weight is regained and driven by increase in total fat, not muscle regain. In this maintenance setting, WVE-007 would have the potential to enable individuals and payers to realize the sustained health benefits of fat loss and improve metabolic health for the long term. We remain on track to initiate a trial investigating WVE-007 as post-incretin maintenance in the fourth quarter.

Collectively, we believe WVE-007 development program can redefine the treatment landscape in obesity and are working expeditiously to examine its potential across treatment settings. We look forward to engaging with KOLs and the community to present and discuss WVE-007's differentiated approach at ObesityWeek. In RNA editing, we are advancing WVE-006, our GalNAc RNA editing candidate for AATD, towards a potentially registrational study. Our clinical data support WVE-006's potential to offer a new standard of care for individuals living with AATD by treating both lung and liver manifestations of the disease and restoring dynamic AAT response with convenient, infrequent subcutaneous dosing and would offer a potentially differentiated value proposition for both healthcare providers and payers.

As a reminder, current IV augmentation standard of care does not have any impact on liver manifestations of the disease. Importantly, those augmented protein levels are depleted during acute inflammatory events, leaving the lung unprotected and potentially prone to damage. Investigational DNA editing, a different therapeutic approach, carries with it both safety concerns and, just as notably, payer challenges for a one-time, very costly therapy with uncertain durability. This leaves RNA editing well-suited as a first-line therapy.

As Chris Wright will speak to in further detail momentarily, following our recent meeting with FDA, planning is now underway for a single registrational trial of WVE-006 for AATD, offering an efficient path to full as well as potentially accelerated approval without the need for a separate confirmatory trial. Building on our RNA editing success, we also continue to advance our second RNA editing candidate, WVE-008 for PNPLA3 liver disease towards the clinic this year. WVE-008 aims to address an area of high unmet need, with nine million individuals living with PNPLA3 I148M liver disease. At our Research Day next week, we look forward to having Dr.

Arun Sanyal, a leading hepatologist, join us to share additional perspectives on PNPLA3 I148M liver disease and the opportunity for an RNA-editing correction approach. We will also provide further updates on how we are continuing to push the boundaries of innovation with a proprietary chemistry across our hepatic and extrahepatic RNAi, RNA editing, and bifunctional modalities. Beyond our internal pipeline, our PRISM platform and proprietary chemistry continue to demonstrate their value, as evidenced by GSK's recent development candidate selection. The transition of this development candidate to GSK provides further validation of our best-in-class SpiNA designed siRNA. We have also welcomed Monika Vnuk to Wave as Chief Business Officer. Monika brings extensive corporate development experience and joins Wave from Sanofi.

Monika will leverage her background to further capitalize on and expand the potential of our platform and support Wave's next phase of growth. Now, I would like to turn the call over to Chris Wright, who will discuss our clinical development plans for WVE-007, WVE-006, and WVE-008. Chris?

Chris Wright: Thank you, Paul. We continue to make strong progress on our WVE-007 clinical development plan. Let's start with our phase II-A monotherapy trial. This is a global, placebo-controlled trial enrolling individuals living with obesity, with BMI of 35 to 50, and comorbidities. There is a total of four cohorts with up to 40 participants each across two doses, 240 mg and 400 mg, and two study populations, with and without type 2 diabetes. Participants receive two doses of WVE-007, one on day one and one on day 85, and are followed for up to 12 months.

To date, with dosing underway, we're observing that participants have baseline levels of adiposity that are substantially higher than the phase I portion, in line with phase II and III obesity trials. Given WVE-007's mechanism of action, we believe this population is well suited to demonstrate WVE-007's potential to deliver meaningful improvements in body composition and healthy weight loss. This trial also specifically includes measurements of liver fat content by MRI PDFF to inform WVE-007's potential in MASH. Our preclinical data and a growing body of clinical evidence support the potential for substantial reductions in liver fat. In parallel with this progress, we remain actively involved with the patient community, KOLs, and foundations.

Together, we're working to engage with regulators on the importance of improving body composition, including reducing excess fat and preserving muscle, consistent with recent guidance. Turning to combination therapy. In the third quarter, we initiated our phase II-A combination trial. This portion of INLIGHT has a randomized controlled design that will evaluate 240 mg and 400 mg doses of WVE-007 in combination with weekly tirzepatide versus tirzepatide alone in individuals living with obesity with a range of BMIs from 35 to 50 and type 2 diabetes. For our third treatment opportunity, we remain on track to initiate a clinical trial of WVE-007 in the fourth quarter as a maintenance therapy to preserve weight loss following discontinuation of treatment.

In addition, we look forward to sharing 600 mg data from the phase I single ascending dose portion of INLIGHT in the fourth quarter. This will help us further characterize potency, duration, as well as safety and tolerability. Turning to WVE-006 for AATD. WVE-006 is our investigational RNA editing therapy with the potential to address both lung and liver manifestations of AATD. We continue to make excellent progress advancing WVE-006 for patients in need and, following a productive meeting with the FDA, are now planning our registration trial. Our discussions with the agency, KOLs, and the Alpha-1 community underscore the high unmet need and limitations of current standard of care.

As a reminder, the only currently approved treatment for AATD is weekly IV plasma-derived augmentation therapy, which carries several limitations. With a fixed scheduled dose, there is no restoration of the dynamic response, leaving individuals with Alpha-1 at risk if AAT protein levels fall too low during an infectious or inflammatory event. IV therapy is time-consuming and often requires in-patient visits and does nothing to lower the Z-AAT to address the risk of liver disease. While DNA base-editing approaches target both lung and liver disease in AATD, they introduce permanent DNA modifications, carry bystander editing risk, and rely on LNP delivery, which is associated with liver enzyme elevations.

The risk of irreversible off-target effects is particularly notable as genomic DNA editing in cancer-associated genes has been reported in preclinical studies. In addition, treatment with DNA editing therapies may render patients ineligible for future base editing, RNA editing, or other new therapies. siRNA approaches reducing Z-AAT are also in development for AATD liver disease. However, this approach does not restore M-AAT, and it leaves the lung unprotected due to the chronic AAT knockdown. AATD is a uniquely compelling disease for RNA editing. It is a monogenic disorder caused by a single well-characterized genetic variant in the SERPINA1 gene.

This leads to misfolded Z-AAT protein and an absence of healthy circulating M-AAT protein, which normally protects the lung during inflammatory or infectious events. Without dynamic production of functional AAT protein, individuals with Alpha-1 are at risk for lung damage, ultimately developing emphysema and bronchiectasis, characterized by chronic cough, recurrent infections, and shortness of breath. In parallel, misfolded Z accumulates in hepatocytes and causes progressive liver injury and increased risk of liver disease. With WVE-006, which targets the underlying causes of AATD, we have generated a growing body of data supporting its potential as a new standard of care for individuals living with Alpha-1 by restoring an MZ-like phenotype, which has a low risk of lung and liver disease.

In our ongoing RestorAATion-2 clinical trial, WVE-006 delivered substantially reduced levels of Z-AAT, protective basal levels of total AAT with a percent of wild type M-AAT well into the MZ range, as well as a restored dynamic physiologic response to inflammatory events. Enrollment and dosing are complete in the 200 mg. 400 mg, and 600 mg cohorts of RestorAATion-2. We remain on track to share data from the 600 mg monthly dosing cohort in the fourth quarter, which will help inform an optimal dose and regimen.

With the compelling profile of WVE-006 we have observed to date, and following our FDA meeting, we are planning for a single two-year registrational trial with a one-year interim analysis for potential accelerated approval based on AATD biomarkers. We believe this single trial design, without the need for a separate confirmatory study, offers an efficient path for full approval, bringing WVE-006 to patients in need as quickly as possible. In addition, we are encouraged that the agency supported the use of our LC-MS assay to measure M-AAT and Z-AAT proteins. We also continue to make strong progress advancing our second RNA editing candidate, WVE-008, for homozygous PNPLA3 I148M liver disease.

Similar to WVE-006 and WVE-007, our approach for WVE-008 is strongly rooted in human genetics. There are an estimated nine million homozygous PNPLA3 I148M carriers with liver disease across the U.S. and Europe, who are at a nine-fold higher risk of dying from liver disease as compared with non-carriers. The PNPLA3 variant is a well-established driver of MASH and liver diseases more generally, yet there are no approved medicines that directly address this underlying disease mechanism. Currently, the only treatment options are non-precision medicines aimed at reducing liver steatosis in early fibrosis, which have limited efficacy for these I148M carriers.

Silencing PNPLA3 can only partially address this disease biology and is likely to leave residual pathology as it knocks down all PNPLA3 protein without restoring healthy wild type protein, which has several important physiologic functions in the liver. As a result, silencing may partially address steatosis, but inflammation and fibrosis may remain unaddressed or worse. Recent clinical trials of PNPLA3 silencing support this notion. A dose-dependent increases in liver enzymes were observed. By contrast, with WVE-008, we aim to correct the I148M variant using our leading RNA editing platform with the goal of restoring normal PNPLA3 function. This is expected to enhance lipid mobilization from the liver, reverse steatosis, reduce inflammation and fibrosis, and ultimately improve overall liver health.

In our upcoming first-in-human study of WVE-008, we plan to leverage previously genotyped populations to efficiently identify homozygous I148M carriers and accelerate enrollment. We will evaluate target engagement with circulating biomarkers and assess early signs of efficacy using non-invasive liver imaging. We remain on track for a CTA submission in 2026. With that, I'll turn the call over to Kyle to provide an update on our financials. Kyle?

Kyle Moran: Thanks, Chris. Our revenue for the third quarter of 2026 was $13.2 million, compared to $7.6 million in the prior year quarter, and this relates to our ongoing collaboration agreement with GSK. Research and development expenses were $53.8 million in the third quarter of 2026 as compared to $45.9 million in the same period in 2025. The increase primarily reflects continued investment in our advancing clinical programs, including the phase II portion of INLIGHT and progress across our RNA editing pipeline. Our G&A expenses were $25.4 million in the third quarter of 2026 as compared to $18.1 million in the prior year quarter.

The increase primarily reflects costs associated with supporting our advancing pipeline and preparing for the next stages of development. Our net loss was $61.9 million for the third quarter of 2026 as compared to a net loss of $53.9 million in the prior year quarter. We ended the third quarter with $431 million in cash equivalents, and marketable securities, which we expect to be sufficient to fund operations into the third quarter of 2028. We are also due to receive an incremental $12 million milestone payment from GSK in the fourth quarter of this year following their candidate selection. As a reminder, GSK has three additional programs advancing towards development candidate.

Any potential future milestone and other payments to us under our collaboration are not included in our cash runway. I'll now turn the call back over to Paul for closing remarks.

Paul Bolno: Thank you, Kyle Moran. Our strong execution and progress throughout the year leave us well-positioned to unlock value across our pipeline. With WVE-007, we are actively advancing our phase II-A monotherapy and combination trials towards key data in our target patient population and remain on track to initiate our post-incretin maintenance trial later this year. We are advancing WVE-006 towards a potentially registrational trial and building on our success in RNA editing with WVE-008, which remains on track for CTA this year. We look forward to connecting with many of you next week for our annual Research Day.

Before turning to questions, I want to thank our team for the progress we have made and for their continued commitment to reimagining what is possible for patients. I will now turn the call to the operator for questions. Operator?

Operator: Thank you. We will now move into our Q&A session. For those of you who are joining us via Zoom, if you would like to ask a question at this time, please raise your hand by clicking the raise hand at the bottom of your Zoom window. Once called upon, please unmute your audio to ask your question. We will take our first question from Rick Bienkowski with Ladenburg. Please unmute your line and ask your question.

Rick Bienkowski: Hey, good morning, and thanks for taking the question. Now that you have clarity from the FDA on the accelerated approval pathway for WVE-006, do you have any insight on how large of a trial you might need to run for the pivotal study? Also, I know you have shared in the press release that you have aligned on the accelerated approval based on biomarkers, but I was curious if the full approval would also be based on these biomarkers, or has the FDA asked for evidence of improved functional outcome for the full approval?

Paul Bolno: Yeah, I will start the question, and then I will let Chris Wright join in. I think it is important as you laid out, so this is kind of the first into your second, the second part of that first question that went into what is required for the full approval. I think that was, stepping back, I think what was impressive for the team. I was excited that the team came back with a really innovative design that was efficient for patients, efficient from a capital standpoint, and could get us to a potential full approval in a two-year time point with the interim analysis being available for the accelerated approval.

So the predicated design is for that single study without the need for a separate confirmatory study. As it relates to the individual biomarkers that we're going to use, I think we've been pretty clear that we're not going to competitively disadvantage ourselves as others are still going through processes of laying out what's required for those analysis for both full approval and for accelerated approval. I think as Chris alluded to, it's nice to see that we've got validated LC-MS assays that we can use to quantify post. Yes. Chris?

Chris Wright: Sure. I would say we had a really excellent meeting with the FDA. The Pulmonary Division in CDER doesn't always have an opportunity to review genetic medicines, so they were particularly, I think, excited about our approach and really saw that we were hitting our target as intended, in terms of hitting the underlying cause of the disease with increasing M, decreasing Z. Also, they actually picked up quite quickly on the acute phase response as being one of the more relevant things in terms of treating the disease, so that you are increasing the level into the event to protect the tissue when it needs it the most.

They also appreciated that from the protein replacement perspective, you're kind of highest at this when you start, and that kind of goes down over time. If you get an event as that's going down, you may end up being unprotected. So they were, I think, quite understanding of that and getting it quickly and were highly supportive of us moving forward for patients.

Operator: Our next question comes from Joon Lee with UBS. Please unmute your line and ask your question.

Joon Lee: Great. Thanks for taking our questions, and great to be following you guys again. This year's Nobel Prize in Chemistry, how validating is that to your stereochemistry platform, and how do you think it helps you differentiate from your competitors, and are you able to provide some examples? Just a quick follow-up question for Dr. Monika Vnuk, if she's on. What is your first order of business at Wave, and what is your midterm and long-term goals at Wave? Thank you.

Paul Bolno: Thanks, Joon. It's great to have you back. I'll take the first part, and then I think Monika will join. Obviously, she's sitting next to me for the second part, and we're excited to have her. To your first piece, I think it's nice to see the recognition on chirality. As you point out, it's not just the award for a prize, but it's the recognition of just how important chirality and stereochemistry is to fundamental biology. We've often talked about it in the context of other therapeutic examples like thalidomide and others, and that specificity matters and stereochemistry matters.

If we think about what happens on every enzymatic structure we work with, whether that it's an AGO2, whether we see it with ADAR, whether we saw it with RNase H in antisense, whether we see it with the innate immune system recognizing various patterns, biology recognizes single structures. I think the most important piece in drug development is being able to optimize from a mixture. I think if we look back over a decade now, we're able to do this in a highly consistent, programmable way. Meaning, if you think about, again, INHBE, it was 18 months from mouse data to human data, so it's efficient. It doesn't cost any additional features, so this isn't separation.

We've been able to build the platform capability to drive that to manufacturing, where our costs are not different to unlock that potential. I think having that clarity of understanding of medicines lets us understand in preclinical studies why something's working or not, and frankly, in clinical studies, lets us understand what needs to continue to be optimized as we watch the evolution. So I think it's fundamental, and it's nice to see it widely recognized. I'll turn to Monika to answer, as she's dove in with both feet. Or I guess, yeah, that's jumping. But yeah, go for it.

Monika Vnuk: Great. Thank you, Paul. Joon, thank you so much for the question. I am very excited to be joining Wave and this highly accomplished team. On one hand, you could be a bodybuilder with elevated BMI or you could be overweight. I think the notion of really getting into body composition as a function of subq fat, visceral fat, and lean mass is important. I know we've said this before, but I do think it's wonderful to start seeing the fact that scales that people can have in their homes are now almost inexpensive to be able to measure and characterize that.

The only tool people were able to use before was a scale where they could look at pounds, they could measure height, and they could try to calculate what is happening in terms of BMI and say, "Okay, I need to lose weight." I think the discipline that we are able to see now where people are able to really quantify the characterization of what weight is being lost, the quality of that weight loss, as a function of health is important, and is very much tied to what we hear from the patients that we are speaking to and their advocates, as well as the clinicians we are speaking to.

I think it all goes net favorably with the direction of travel that this program is taking and hopefully the conversations that continue to emerge within what is healthy weight loss. As it relates to WVE-006, we do not comment on individual regulatory interactions, but it is safe to assume that we will be well prepared when we go into that trial to deliver.

Operator: Our last question comes from Cha Yang with Jefferies. Please unmute your line and ask your question.

Cha Cha Yang: Hey, team. This is Cha on for Roger. Thanks for taking our question. I wanted to drill down on some of the expectations for WVE-006 for your post-incretin maintenance trial.

Cha Cha Yang: Can you speak to the magnitude of weight regain prevention that you'd consider clinically meaningful in the maintenance setting? Thanks.

Paul Bolno: Yeah, thank you. Just to be clear, because I know it's WVE-007, just for others dialing in just to make sure. For INHBE WVE-007, the maintenance study and the prevention of weight gain, we'll share more details when that study initiates on the design and how we think about the characterization.

What I thought was, if we go back to the DIO data where we demonstrated the ability to drive that prevention of weight regain, I think it's important as we do look at that, to quantify it, we actually saw essentially what we saw when you use this mechanism, which is you get a slight bump, which comes from lean mass increases, and then the fat stays solid, hence why it stays flat. What you would expect to see when we provide that update is a requisite update on not just prevention of weight gain as a total poundage number, but also both as a function of how weight is being regained, right?

Because the prevention will be of fat coming back, which is the principal driver if you think about what happens when people come off their incretins, is what we saw in the DIO. The hedonic eating comes back, you take that signaling off, and when the weight regains, it comes back predominantly as fat, which is a less healthy state than when you started your therapy. The ability to curtail that fat regain, but to potentially regain some muscle, we see as advantageous, and we'll give the specifics when the study initiates.

Operator: Thank you. There are no further questions at this time. I will now turn the call back over to Paul Bolno for closing remarks.

Paul Bolno: Thank you for joining our call this morning, and we appreciate your continued support. Have a great day.

Operator: This concludes the call for today. Thank you for joining. You may now disconnect.
