#3663. Florida Cop Resigns From Police Force After Arrest For Assuming Dead Man's Identity

The Florida cop arrested for assuming a dead man's identity and taking over his home and his bills for years is now out of a job, TMZ has learned.

Former Police Officer Brian Sguros just resigned from the Lauderhill Police Department before he could be terminated following an Internal Affairs probe, according to local officials.

Prior to his resignation, Sguros was put on paid administrative leave pending the outcome of a criminal investigation into his alleged criminal behavior.

Last Tuesday, Sguros was taken into custody and charged with fraud, official misconduct, falsifying official documents, computer crimes to fraudulently obtain property, and using two-way communications to facilitate a felony.

Cops say Sguros assumed the identity of 71-year-old Dennis West, who was found dead at his Lauderhill home on July 11, 2022. Police say West died from natural causes, namely cardiovascular disease.

Sguros was allegedly a backup officer at the scene during the welfare check when he executed his plan to take over West's property and assume the man's identity.

According to cops, Sguros told the medical examiner he was West's close friend, and that West had no living relatives ... which was total BS, according to police.

After moving into West's house, cops say Sguros began paying property taxes and utility bills, all under West's name ... as if the dead man was still alive. Cops also say Sguros even used West's ID to obtain a permit to renovate the pad.

Sguros finally messed up when he allegedly used his personal access code to log in to law enforcement databases to collect information about West.

One more misstep ... filing paperwork with Broward County in February 2025 stating he'd been in possession of West's home since August 2022, weeks after West had died.

After that, Sguros was toast ... law enforcement had him in their sights.

#3662. How Kevin Jonas Accidentally Sent a Nude Photo to Group Chat

Kevin Jonas, along with his brothers Joe Jonas and Nick Jonas, detailed how he recently sent their group chat a nude photo unintentionally: “This is my trauma.”

Kevin Jonas’ cheeks are burnin’ up over this flub.

“There was a big announcement that J.J. McCarthy is going to be our quarterback,” Joe continued, with Nick noting, “Kevin sends through a picture to the group chat. It’s a TV and it’s got the announcement on it—and no one thinks much of it.”

Nick finished, “Sent it to the group chat with all the boys.”

For his part, though, Kevin just felt like a sucker.

#3660. Did Shia LaBeouf Have Beef With Hilary Duff And Lizzie McGuire? Co-Star Spilled Tea Decades Later

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Disney's after-school lineup came with some after-school drama.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With plenty of new shows arriving on the 2026 TV premiere schedule, I've still got a soft spot for the Disney Channel shows that dominated my afternoons in the early 2000s. Even Stevens and Lizzie McGuire were two of the network's biggest hits, and I always assumed they coexisted pretty peacefully. Apparently, somebody forgot to tell Shia LaBeouf. According to his former co-star Christy Carlson Romano, he had a one-sided rivalry with Hilary Duff, and I genuinely had no idea.

Romano, who played LaBeouf's older sister Ren on Even Stevens, recently appeared on The Joe Vulpis Podcast, where the conversation turned to whether Disney Channel stars had beef with their Nickelodeon counterparts. She said they rarely saw those actors and that the real competition was happening within Disney, especially when it came to ratings. That's when Romano brought up LaBeouf's feelings about Duff's hugely popular show:

Shia was definitely beefing with Hilary. Hilary had no clue about that. I'm sure she was doing her own thing, but he would always be kind of like, trash-talking Lizzie McGuire. And I was always like, ‘We don't know anything about them.’ Like, why?

The fact that Duff apparently didn't even know there was a rivalry is what gets me. Imagine minding your own business, playing a teenager whose animated alter ego occasionally narrates her problems, and learning decades later that another Disney Channel star was apparently trash-talking your show. That's some remarkably specific after-school drama.

There was a real ratings competition behind it, though. Even Stevens arrived in 2000 and turned LaBeouf's troublemaking Louis Stevens into a breakout character. Lizzie McGuire debuted the following year and quickly became one of the channel's defining hits. Interestingly, Duff's journey to landing the role was pretty wild, though she probably had no idea she'd eventually become part of an alleged rivalry with another Disney star.

Romano suggested the competition mattered especially deeply to LaBeouf, whose childhood and family circumstances had been difficult. She wasn't excusing the comments, exactly, but she offered some perspective on why he might have taken the rivalry so seriously:

He was very competitive. He wanted to be the number one show on the network. He had to be the number one show because I think his life depended on that show doing well. He had a rough upbringing and I think he needed that show to do well for him.

That puts a different spin on Romano's story for me. We're talking about kids carrying two of Disney Channel's biggest shows, and she believes LaBeouf saw Even Stevens as something he couldn't afford to lose. He's spoken about his complicated childhood before, even exploring parts of it in Honey Boy, although he later acknowledged that elements of the film's depiction of his father were fictionalized. Of course, this is Romano's recollection of his behavior more than two decades later, and from the sound of it, Duff didn't even know there was a rivalry.

Romano hasn't exactly sugarcoated her own experience working with LaBeouf, either. In a previous interview, she described their Even Stevens relationship as competitive and sometimes difficult, particularly as the show began focusing more on his character. The two aren't in contact today, although she's said she still wishes him well.

Here's another funny little Disney Channel connection: Romano and Duff actually starred together in the 2002 movie Cadet Kelly, which Duff recently discussed revisiting for a potential sequel. So while LaBeouf was apparently keeping score, his co-star was making movies with the very person he considered competition.

As for Duff, her relationship with Lizzie McGuire has continued long after the original series ended. She's even spoken candidly about Disney's decision to scrap the planned revival, making it clear that she still cares about the character and what the show meant to fans.

Neither Duff nor LaBeouf had publicly responded to Romano's latest claims when the initial reports broke. Still, I know I'll be thinking about this the next time I revisit either show. It's pretty funny to imagine that while the rest of us were flipping between Even Stevens and Lizzie McGuire after school, one of their stars was apparently keeping score the whole time.

Ryan graduated from Missouri State University with a BA in English/Creative Writing. An expert in all things horror, Ryan enjoys covering a wide variety of topics. He's also a lifelong comic book fan and an avid watcher of Game of Thrones and House of the Dragon.

#3659. This Is the Robot Vacuum I Use Every Day, and It's 50% Off Right Now

I live in a 1,600-square-foot home, and vacuuming and mopping it by hand takes a considerable chunk of time. Passing that labor off to a robot vacuum was a great decision. I decided to go with the Roborock Qrevo CurvX since it seemed like a great fit for my circumstances. If you also have a dog, a mix of different floor types, and a gym with a workout mat, this robot vacuum might work for you, too. Amazon currently has it for $749.99 (originally $1,499.99), a 50% discount, and the lowest price it has ever been, according to price-tracking tools.

One of the main things I was looking for in a robot vacuum was suction power. The Qrevo CurvX has 22,000 Pa, which is stronger than most options at its price and is powerful enough to pick up anything that needs vacuuming in my house. I like that it has a self-lifting chassis so it can climb over my workout mat, rugs, obstacles like cables, or anything else that might be in the way, so I don't have to babysit it while it's doing its job. I also like the extendable mop pads and side brushes that allow it to reach corners and crevices it might not be slim enough to fit into. (Speaking of which, it's only 3.14 inches tall, so it can fit under most couches and beds without issue.)

My experience with the Roborock app has been great. It's easy to use and gives me enough control to clean my house how I want without a hassle. "No Go Zones" are easy to set up, as is the schedule. The main issue I've had is the size of the internal dust bin: It's small, so it needs to be cleaned after basically every scheduled use (though that shouldn't be as bad if you don't have a pet that sheds a lot).

Take it from me: If you're looking for a reliable hybrid robot vacuum that can clean multiple types of floors, mop, and comes with a great app, the Qrevo CurvX makes a compelling case at the current $750 discount.

#3657. Gotham Film Awards 2026 Nominations Spread the Love from ‘All of a Sudden’ to ‘The Invite’

The 2026 Gotham Film Awards nominations are in, setting the stage for the next phase of the movie awards season. While last year’s nominations and wins were topped by “One Battle After Another,” which went on to win the Best Picture Oscar, the organization decided to show some “Tender Loving Care” to more films this time around. The new Mike Leigh film is among five nomination leaders this year, along with “All of a Sudden,” “Clarissa,” “La Gradiva,” and “The Invite.” Each scored four nominations.

The 2026 nominations also mark the return of the Gotham Audience Award after a six-year hiatus. The honor celebrates films that transcend the screen to become defining cultural moments, capturing audiences’ attention through their impact, reach, and resonance. Gotham members will vote to determine the winner, and the award will be presented to the film’s credited director(s) and producer(s). (In short, the prize indexes a film’s popularity in culture through audience response and box office.) Related Stories Watch ‘My Undesirable Friends’ Director Julia Loktev Recall the Week Russia Invaded Ukraine ‘Wildwood’ Sparked a Much-Needed Comeback for Laika at the Box Office

This year, the Gotham Audience Award nominees were curated from films submitted to The Gothams for consideration, measured by opening-weekend per-theater average against the highest-rated and most-watched films of 2026, as reported in box office tracking and on Letterboxd. Notable films not submitted for any Gotham Awards consideration include “The Odyssey” and “The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth.

“It’s a pleasure to announce this year’s nominees. It has been a remarkable year for film, with audiences turning out in theaters for bold, distinctive work from first-time filmmakers and established artists alike,” said Jeffrey Sharp, Gotham Film & Media Institute executive director. “This year’s nominations, alongside our Tributes and the return of the Audience Award, reflect that abundance and celebrate the creative community that has been at the heart of The Gothams for more than three decades.”

While the Gotham nominations committees are made up of clusters of critics, filmmakers, and tastemakers (including IndieWire Editorial Director Kate Erbland and Chief Film Critic David Ehrlich), rather than an 11,000-member Academy, these awards often help put less publicized films onto Oscar voters’ radars. Example: Kelly O’Sullivan and Alex Thompson’s extraordinary “Mouse” in the Best Feature category, which premiered way back at the Berlin Film Festival and is primed for an awards-season relaunch.

See the full list of Gotham Film Awards 2026 nominations below. The ceremony takes place on November 30 in New York City.

#3656. Netflix's Matthew Perry Documentary Is The Tribute The Friends Star Deserves

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Matthew Perry's death at the age of 54 in 2023 came as a shock to everyone, including fans who had only recently witnessed the actor reveal the depths of his struggles with addiction. But the new Netflix documentary "The One About Matthew Perry" is here to pay tribute to the beloved "Friends" star in a way that not only does justice to his legacy, but reminds us all that his life was not entirely defined by his personal struggles.

In 2021, we saw a version of Perry we didn't quite recognize appear on the "Friends" reunion special. The year after, he released his candid memoir, "Friends, Lovers, and the Big Terrible Thing," in which he revealed the extent of his battles with addiction in a book that was, at times, a harrowing read. The following year, he was gone.

Perry's death was a deeply sad end to a life that had brought so much joy to generations of TV audiences. For those who grew up with "Friends," and therefore had an element of Perry's inimitable comedic sensibility permanently ingrained in their psyches, all of this felt like a kind of grim alternate reality — as if we were living in the wrong timeline. Not only was Perry gone, but our collective memory of his lovable comedic persona had been darkened by his bravely honest account of having been living in a personal hell for much of his life. To be sure, it was crucial that Perry spoke out about that personal hell. It's just that it was inescapably sad to hear. But while "The One About Matthew Perry" is also a hard watch that doesn't shy away from its subject's hardships, it's also a beautiful reminder of his impact on his friends, family, and fans.

Despite being world famous and beloved by an entire generation who grew up on "Friends," Matthew Perry still doesn't get the credit he deserves. His comedic style is often boiled down to his signature cadence, which has taken on a catchphrase-like aura in the years since "Friends" went off the air. But as Chandler Bing, Perry was as influential to a generation's developing comedic sensibility as any other comedy icon of the era.

When he passed away, it wasn't just sad. There was a vague sense of foreboding about the whole thing, but it was due to more than just the criminal circumstances surrounding his death. Perhaps it was the fact it came at a time when so much of the culture in which Perry came to prominence was rapidly fading. It's no secret that the world in which millennials grew up simply doesn't exist in the 2020s. Modern day culture is more fractured than it's ever been, and many institutions of our youth have been undermined, as have many figureheads of the era. The worst revelations of the documentary "Quiet on Set: The Dark Side of Kids TV," for example, were emblematic of this erosion of a time that, viewed from the 2020s, seems like a dream — or perhaps, given the aforementioned revelations, a nightmare hidden beneath a veneer of contentment.

Perry's passing almost seemed like a symbol of all this. Not only was he gone, it turns out he was dealing with "the big terrible thing" the whole time. But with "The One About Matthew Perry," director Mary Robertson, who co-directed "Quiet on Set" alongside Emma Schwartz, reminds us that Perry's legacy can never truly be defined by his struggles.

Matthew Perry always projected a gregarious and outgoing energy, which somehow made it sadder to discover he was struggling so much. As much as Perry infused "Friends, Lovers, and the Big Terrible Thing" with his sense of humor, and as much as you had to admire his bravery and forthrightness in revealing the details of his journey, the overwhelming sense you got from the book was that Perry had truly been through hell. But "The One About Matthew Perry" not only acts a valuable lesson for other streaming documentaries, it serves as an important addendum to the actor's memoir, reminding us that however bleak things were for Perry, he did ultimately make so many people happy.

The most powerful moments of the doc are his family and friends' recollections. Perry's memoir was, of course, all in his voice. But hearing his story told by those who loved him lends a more uplifting aspect to the narrative. That's something his memoir simply couldn't provide, and "The One About Matthew Perry" acts as a fitting tribute to the man by allowing those who truly cared for him to tell us just how happy he made them.

That's not to say the documentary isn't hard to watch at times. But it is a celebration of Perry's life, and in some ways acts as an antidote to the darkness that surrounded his final years. For fans, it's a reassurance that the warm spirit we felt emanating from our TVs every time he was on screen was real, and something that can't ever truly be eroded by time. Perry meant a lot to so many, and after a few years of bleak revelations about his life, it's nice to be reminded of that.

#3654. Cincinnati Financial (CINF) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Cincinnati Financial Corporation (CINF +2.31%) reported third-quarter 2026 results that demonstrated consistent underwriting profitability despite a significant increase in catastrophe losses compared with the prior year. Management reported that the consolidated property casualty combined ratio remained healthy at 90.9%, supported by favorable prior-year reserve development and disciplined pricing across commercial, personal, and specialty lines. The company stated that it continues to balance new business opportunities with strict underwriting standards, particularly in a competitive market environment where renewal price increases have begun to moderate. Furthermore, the company reported a strategic emphasis on leveraging data technology and artificial intelligence to enhance operational efficiency and maintain strong relationships with its independent agency partners.

Operator: Good morning, and welcome to the Cincinnati Financial Corporation third quarter 2026 earnings conference call. All participants are in a listen-only mode. After the speaker's remarks, we will conduct a question-and-answer session. To ask a question at this time, you will need to press star followed by the number one on your telephone keypad. As a reminder, this conference call is being recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to Andy Gossard, Investor Relations Officer. Thank you. Please go ahead.

Andy Gossard: Good morning, and thank you for joining us for our third quarter 2026 earnings conference call. Late yesterday, we issued the news release on our results, along with our supplemental financial package, including our quarter-end investment portfolio. To find copies of any of these documents, please visit our investor website, investors.cinfin.com. The shortest route to the information is the Quarterly Results section near the middle of the investor overview page. On this call, you'll first hear from President and Chief Executive Officer Steve Spray, and then from Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer Mike Sewell. After their prepared remarks, investors participating on the call may ask questions.

At that time, some responses may be made by others in the room with us, including Executive Chairman Steve Johnston, Chief Investment Officer Steve Soloria, and Cincinnati Insurance's Chief Claims Officer Marc Schambow, and Senior Vice President of Corporate Finance Andy Schnell. Please note that some of the matters to be discussed today are forward-looking. These forward-looking statements involve certain risks and uncertainties. With respect to these risks and uncertainties, we direct your attention to our news release and to our various filings with the SEC. Also, a reconciliation of non-GAAP measures was provided with the news release. Statutory accounting data is prepared in accordance with statutory accounting rules and therefore is not reconciled to GAAP.

Steve Spray: Good morning, and thank you for joining us today to hear more about our results. We had another quarter of strong operating performance that reinforces our confidence in the long-term direction of our business. Our insurance operations produced excellent underwriting results, even as the property-casualty combined ratio increased from last year's exceptional level. Net income of $459 million for the third quarter of 2026 included recognition of less than $1 million on an after-tax basis for the increase in fair value of equity securities still held. Non-GAAP operating income was $443 million for the quarter, down only $6 million from a year ago, despite catastrophe losses that were $146 million higher on an after-tax basis.

The 90.9% third quarter 2026 property-casualty combined ratio increased by 2.7 percentage points compared with third quarter last year, including an increase of 7.0 points for catastrophe losses. Our current accident year combined ratio before catastrophe losses for the first nine months of 2026 was 86.5%, improving slightly compared with the 86.7% reported through the first nine months of 2025. Moving to premium growth, our consolidated property-casualty net written premiums grew 2% for the quarter. Premium growth moderated as expected, reflecting our commitment to pricing discipline, careful risk selection, and segmentation on a policy-by-policy basis.

Estimated average renewal price increases for most lines of business during the third quarter were lower than the second quarter of 2026, but still at levels we believe were healthy. Commercial lines and excess and surplus lines averaged increases in the low single-digit percentage range. Our personal line segment included homeowner increases in the high single-digit percentage range and personal auto increases in the mid-single-digit range. Although market conditions continue to be challenging, we remain confident that our diverse product portfolio, exceptional service, and strong relationships with independent insurance agents provide a solid foundation for long-term success. Next, I will highlight third quarter performance by insurance segment compared with a year ago.

The commercial lines combined ratio was 92.8%, an increase of 1.7 percentage points, including 4.3 points from higher catastrophe losses. Net written premiums grew less than 1%. Personal lines grew net written premiums 1%, impacted by lower new business premiums that reflect continued softening market conditions. The combined ratio for personal lines was 90.3%, just 2.1 percentage points higher than last year, despite an increase of 12.1 points from higher catastrophe losses. Excess and surplus lines grew net written premiums 5% and produced another excellent combined ratio of 89.4%. Cincinnati Re and Cincinnati Global both contributed meaningfully to premium growth and continue to provide diversification benefits for our insurance operations.

Cincinnati Re's third quarter 2026 net written premiums increased by 34%. Its combined ratio was 94.8%. Cincinnati Global grew 13% while maintaining an impressive 70.7% combined ratio. Our life insurance subsidiary had another solid quarter, including 11% net income growth. In addition, term life insurance earned premiums grew 2%. Now, I'll give a summary of our primary measure of long-term financial performance, the value creation ratio. Our VCR was -2.1% for the third quarter of 2026. Net income before investment gains or losses for the quarter contributed 2.7%, lower overall valuation of our investment portfolio and other items contributed -4.8%.

Before I hand the call to Mike, I wanted to give you an update on our ongoing investment in data technology and artificial intelligence. We continue to prioritize applications of generative and agentic AI that can improve the efficiency and effectiveness of our associates. We want to optimize their efforts, allowing them to add more value to our business, deepening relationships, sharing expertise, and focusing their energy on the most complex insurance and technology decisions. I'll give you two examples of AI applications that are in production today that we believe have already and will continue to produce meaningful benefits across the organization. Many other use cases exist in varying stages of development, from design all the way through production.

First, our AI Center of Excellence is harnessing cloud provider large language models to create reusable internal capabilities that can be adapted for additional business areas where the use case and supporting evidence are strong. So far, we've developed seven proprietary intelligent assistants for associates to use in areas such as underwriting, claims, and loss control. These assistants, along with other tools we've deployed, reduce the time spent on information-intensive tasks such as summarizing underwriting and claims files and understanding internal appetite and risk guidance. Second, we are applying automation and AI-enabled capabilities within selected new business and commercial service workflows.

More specifically, we are using these capabilities to process new business submissions received by email, enabling us to accelerate quote delivery by more than 30% across our commercial lines and excess and surplus lines businesses. Our approach is disciplined. We establish a baseline, evaluate the operating results, ensure we have strong governance and controls, and then expand the capability only where the evidence supports it. Because our agents tell us that speed to quote is a key variable as we compete for business, we believe reducing the average time to quote will positively support our continued profitable growth. Now, I'll turn it over to Chief Financial Officer Mike Sewell for additional insights regarding our financial performance.

Mike Sewell: Thank you, Steve, and thanks to all of you for joining us today. Investment income growth of 10% in the third quarter of 2026 contributed nicely to our operating results, driven by strong cash flow from insurance operations. Bond interest income grew 11%, and the net purchases of fixed maturity securities totaled $1.1 billion for the quarter and $2 billion for the first nine months of the year. The third quarter pre-tax average yield of 5.04% for the fixed maturity portfolio was down 6 basis points compared with last year. The average pre-tax yield for the total purchased taxable and tax-exempt bonds during the third quarter of this year was 5.93%. Dividend income was down 4% for the quarter.

Net sales of equity securities totaled $52 million for the quarter and $784 million on a year-to-date basis. Valuation changes in aggregate for the third quarter were slightly favorable for our equity portfolio and unfavorable due to rising interest rates for our bond portfolio. Before tax effects, the net gain was $6 million for the equity portfolio, while the net loss was approximately $1.2 billion for the bond portfolio. At the end of the third quarter, the total investment portfolio net appreciated value was approximately $7.4 billion. The equity portfolio was in a net gain position of $8.9 billion, while the fixed maturity portfolio was in a net loss position of $1.5 billion.

Cash flow continued to benefit investment income growth. Cash flow from operating activities for the first nine months of 2026 was $2.1 billion, a decrease of less than 1% from a year ago. Moving to expense management, our goal is to balance making strategic investments in our business while also controlling expenses. The third quarter 2026 property casualty underwriting expense ratio increased by 0.4 percentage points due primarily to timing of recognition of certain expenses. Regarding loss reserves, our approach remains consistent and aims in net amounts in the upper half of the actuarially estimated range of net loss and loss expense reserves. As we do each quarter, we consider new information such as paid losses and case reserves.

Then we updated estimated ultimate losses and loss expenses by accident year and line of business. For the first nine months of 2026, our net addition to property casualty loss expense reserves was $1.26 billion, including $997 million for the IBNR portion. During the third quarter, we experienced $94 million of property casualty net favorable reserve development on prior accident years that benefited the combined ratio by 3.6 percentage points. For the commercial casualty line of business, there was no material reserve development for any prior accident year during the quarter.

On an all-lines basis by accident year, net favorable reserve development for the first nine months of 2026 included favorable $203 million for 2025, favorable $70 million for 2024, and an unfavorable $56 million in aggregate for accident years prior to 2024. I'll conclude my comments with third quarter capital management highlights. We paid $142 million in dividends to shareholders. During the quarter, we repurchased an immaterial number of shares. We believe our strong balance sheet continues to provide substantial flexibility and supports our financial strength. Parent company cash and marketable securities at quarter end was $6.1 billion. Debt to total capital remained under 10%.

Year-to-date VCR was 5.8%, and our quarter-end book value was $105.42 per share, with $16 billion of GAAP consolidated shareholders' equity, providing plenty of capacity for the profitability growth of our insurance operations. Now, I'll turn the call back over to Steve.

Steve Spray: Thanks, Mike. Before we open the call for questions, I'd like to take a moment to thank and recognize Chief Claims Officer Marc Schambow for his nearly 40 years of dedication to Cincinnati Insurance, our agents, and our policyholders. As most of you know, this is Marc's final earnings call before his January retirement. Since Marc took the reins of our claims operations, we faced some of the worst catastrophe events in our company's history, including Winter Storm Elliott, Hurricane Helene, and the California wildfires. He has continued to lead with empathy, nurturing our belief in responding person to person as we've made bad days better for countless families and businesses in their time of crisis. Steve Spray: Yeah, I think we've had the three-year policy since I've been with the company for 35 years. I'm a huge fan of it. If you look at our underwriting results, our underwriting performance, our three-year policies outperform the one-year from an underwriting. So our underwriters are the terms, conditions, the pricing, the intuition that they place on that three-year policy, our results are better. And what we do is we're guaranteeing that the rate won't change on the property, the GL, the crime, and the inland marine. The auto, the work comp, the umbrella, those are all subject to anniversary rate change. But we're giving a three-year commitment to that policyholder. Right now, it's over 50%, or it's a majority of our policies in commercial lines, package policies are on a three-year basis, and we're committed to it. It will go up and down, I guess, as in policy count or package account based on market conditions.

Steve Spray: Hey, you know what, Greg? I might add one more thing that came into my mind. That three-year policy, it protects us too in a down market. When we've got those rates locked in a down market, it can protect us on the downside too.

Steve Spray: Yeah. Thanks, Josh. That's something we monitor very closely. #3653. The Garmin Cirqa Finally Got a Much-Needed Feature

This week Garmin's screenless Cirqa smart band received a firmware update that fixes one of its most obvious gaps: the lack of alerts from your phone. With Version 4.20, you can now set up your smart band to buzz your wrist for texts, calls, and other smart notifications from your phone. The addition is listed on Garmin's forums as "Support vibration for smart notifications/incoming calls." Reaction has been upbeat, too—users on the Garmin subreddit have been glad to see the feature arrive.

Amusingly, Garmin's own FAQ still states that the Cirqa does not vibrate for text messages, phone calls, or other smartphone notifications. Clearly that page is now out of date, so Garmin will want to correct it. Since Garmin doesn't yet have step-by-step instructions, I tested the text-message vibrations myself. Here's how you can set up your Cirqa smart band to vibrate for calls and texts, in addition to a number of other promising updates to check out.

To receive these alerts, your band must be running firmware 4.20 or later. Here is how to get everything configured.

Update the firmware. Open your Garmin Connect app and sync your Cirqa, so the app can check for and install version 4.20 or newer.

Grant the necessary phone permissions. In your phone's settings, make sure Garmin Connect has permission to access SMS, Contacts, and Phone.

Configure notifications in Garmin Connect. Back in Garmin Connect, tap the Menu (or More) tab. Select Garmin Devices, then choose your Cirqa Smart Band. Go to Notifications & Alerts (or Smart Notifications) and turn on text and call alerts.

Troubleshooting tip: If alerts aren't coming through, check that Do Not Disturb, Sleep Mode, and any aggressive Battery Saver settings are turned off on both your phone and the band.

For most a lot of Cirqa fans, the phone alerts are the headline, but the 4.20 update carries a solid list of other improvements and bug fixes:

Trail Run: Run/Walk detection is now available for Trail Run activities.

Timestamp fix: Resolved a problem where an activity's recorded time didn't match the time on your phone.

Distance calibration: You can now calibrate distance for Treadmill Run, Indoor Rowing, and Indoor Cycling before saving the activity on the device.

Duration alerts: Duration time alerts for speaking out now accumulate.

Stress alerts: Stress alerts are now blocked while Auto Activity Detection is recording.

Bedtime reminders: Fixed an issue where the bedtime approaching notification sometimes failed to trigger.

Live Activity: Heart rate now appears on the Live Activity screen before recording begins.

Pinned activity bug: Fixed an issue where the pinned activity reverted to Timed Activity after changing the phone's language.

Workout editing: Fixed a problem that prevented editing reps during a workout.

Stability: General system stability improvements.

Paramount+ features more than 2,000 live matches each year. In addition to Serie A, the extensive portfolio of soccer properties also includes Coppa Italia, UEFA Champions League, UEFA Europa League and UEFA Europa Conference League; English Football League; Carabao Cup; NWSL; and more.

#3645. Public Storage (PSA) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Public Storage (PSA +1.79%) reported third-quarter results that exceeded management's prior expectations, leading to a second upward revision of its full-year 2026 financial guidance. Management reported that the company completed the major acquisitions of National Storage Affiliates and Public Storage Canada during the quarter, expanding its portfolio to over 4,500 locations and 327 million square feet. The company stated that operating momentum is shifting from a period of stabilization toward a gradual recovery, supported by improved customer retention and stronger move-in pricing. Management indicated it is leveraging its PS Next operating platform and new AI capabilities to drive efficiency and enhance the customer experience across its North American footprint.

Operator: Greetings, and welcome to Public Storage Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. At this time, all participants are in a listen-only mode. A question-and-answer session will follow the formal presentation. As a reminder, this conference is being recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to your host, Mr. Brandon Reagan, VP of Investor Relations and Strategic Partnership.

Before we begin, we want to remind you that certain matters discussed during this call may constitute forward-looking statements within the meaning of the federal securities laws. These forward-looking statements are subject to certain economic risks and uncertainties. All forward-looking statements speak only as of today, 10/27/2026, we assume no obligation to update, revise, or supplement statements that become untrue because of subsequent events.

H. Thomas Boyle: Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us. I will frame my comments today around 4 points. First, operating fundamentals exceeded our expectations in the quarter, giving us confidence to raise our outlook for the year. Second, PS Next continues to strengthen customer experience and improve how we run the business, including continued investment in digital and AI capabilities. Third, the closings of NSA and Public Storage Canada were important milestones for our value creation engine. Our focus has turned to integration, execution, and positioning these assets for stronger contribution in 2027 and beyond.

Fourth, in a higher rate environment, our balance sheet capital allocation capability and data science tools are strategic advantages that allow us to invest with discipline and speed. Let me begin with operating fundamentals. The third quarter results were better than expected. Supporting our increased outlook and strengthening our confidence in the trajectory of the business as we move towards 2027. That confidence does not reflect a major change in the macro environment. Demand remains uneven. But we are seeing better customer retention, stronger new customer pricing, and a more favorable supply backdrop as new construction slows. Our retention progress reflects a stronger culture of customer obsession.

During my property visit this quarter, I saw a cultural change moving. it is simple things: At 1 visit, a property manager ran to a customer to help them download our app. So that they could open the gate from their phone. But those simple things are showing up in our improving survey results and operating metrics. The combination of improved retention and moderating supply is creating a better operating setup. New customer pricing improved year over year for the second consecutive quarter, Occupancy remained ahead of the prior year and same store revenue accelerated sequentially from the second quarter to the third quarter by 110 basis points into positive territory.

As we exit 2026, confidence is building that we are moving from stabilization to gradual recovery. Now let me turn to PS Next. PS Next remains central to how we intend to win. It brings together customer experience, pricing, operating efficiency, and digital capability in 1 integrated platform. Nearly 90% of customer interactions are digital, and roughly 3/4 of customers complete their lease digitally today. that is an important foundation as AI changes how customers discover businesses compare options, and engage with brands. Last quarter, I spoke about Elli, our AI powered virtual agent. Ellie has now handled more than 300 thousand customer interactions. And that capability continues to expand.

It can improve the customer experience while allowing our teams to focus on the interactions where personal assistance makes the greatest difference. We also expect customers will increasingly interact with public storage not only directly, but through AI agents as well. Whether that is Meta Muse, Grok bots, Dots, or other emerging interfaces we are tuning our digital and AI platforms to make sure the experience is seamless either way. The key point here is that AI does not change the fundamental demand for storage. Storage remains need based, local, and physical. What AI changes is how customers discover, evaluate, and interact with operators.

In that environment, Public Storage is well positioned because of our scale, our digital foundation, our brand, and our customer experience. Turning to NSA and Public Storage Canada. The closing of NSA was a major milestone in the quarter. We moved the portfolio onto Public Storage Systems immediately after closing in July. Allowing us to move quickly from transaction execution to integration. During 1 visit at a transitioning store in the quarter, I saw the look on both the teammate and the customer's face when the teammate was able to immediately address a rent concern with our Web Champ system in place. A simple and quick resolution of a customer concern.

And the resolution was seamlessly real time supported by our data science engines. Rebranding is underway. With approximately 1/3 of the portfolio expected to be fully rebranded by year-end. We are encouraged by the early results in the wholly owned portfolio. Including strong move in volumes, and incremental capital opportunities including potential expansions. Tenant and property cleanup work is underway at the stores, creating some near term noise, including elevated bad debt and repair and maintenance activity, but this work is critical to position the portfolio for improved revenue, margins, and operating performance over time.

As the asset season on our PSNEXT platform, we see meaningful opportunities to improve the customer experience increase utilization, address offline units, and deploy high return capital. Our conviction in the long term opportunity has increased as we operate the portfolio. We were also excited to reunite the Public Storage Canada team and portfolio with Public Storage following the September closing of that transaction. This is a high quality Public Storage branded portfolio in an underpenetrated market with meaningful long-term potential. Systems integration is expected next month ahead of the original schedule. We are setting both portfolios up for 2027 and beyond, for revenue upside expense synergies, margin expansion and a strong long-term value creation.

My fourth point is capital allocation and external growth. Interest rates have moved higher. Higher rates create challenges, but they also create opportunity. When financing costs remain elevated, asset values and seller expectations adjust over time. Our balance sheet and financial flexibility are meaningful advantages in that environment. We are also becoming faster and more precise across our capital allocation tools, Our work in sourcing, AI informed underwriting, and approval processes is cutting deal timelines in half and allowing teams to act more effectively at the micro market level.

Capabilities we are building internally as well as through our partnership with Welltower, are also helping us identify and prioritize attractive markets by harnessing the deep consumer databases that Wellpower has built over a decade. And combining it with the power of Public Storage's vast operating data. The largest transaction in the quarter was an off market $150 million portfolio acquisition in desirable coastal locations from a family exiting the business. The deal has a mid-6% going-in cap rate and illustrates the opportunity created by generational transitions, the value of off market sourcing and the importance of speed and certainty of execution. Our development pipeline has grown year to date to nearly $800 million at quarter end.

Higher construction and also financing costs have made development more difficult across the industry. But we continue to leverage our platform advantages to find attractive micro markets in a less competitive environment. Across acquisitions, development, expansions, lending, and third party management, we continue to see opportunity in all elements of our value creation engine. As we enter the fourth quarter, our focus is increasingly shifting towards 2027. The building blocks for 2027 and beyond are increasingly clear. We are seeing better operating momentum in the core business. We are strengthening customer experience and retention. We are advancing PSNext in building new digital and AI capabilities. We have closed 2 major transactions and are now focused on integration.

We are preparing to deploy capital into 2027 with more precision faster timelines, and better data. And we are doing all of this with a strong balance sheet, an aligned leadership team and an Own It culture that is gaining momentum across the organization. These blocks are coming together to position Public Storage for stronger per share earnings growth in future years. With that, let me turn it over to Joe.

Joseph D. Fisher: Thank you, Tom, and good morning, everyone. As Tom mentioned, we are raising guidance across all key metrics for the second time this year. Reflecting continued robust operating momentum. In addition, we completed NSA and Public Storage Canada and continue to find accretive deals across all our value creation avenues. Funding over $12 billion year to date in activity at attractive locked in cost. Now digging into our third quarter results. Core FFO for the quarter was $4.34 per share, up 0.7% year over year. Growth in same store NOI and accretive external growth activity more than offset higher financing costs and G&A. Same store revenue and NOI each grew 0.5% in the quarter. More importantly, forward looking indicators strengthened.

Average move in rents improved a positive 9% our strongest level since the second quarter of 2022, and move in rates are up 28% from the fourth quarter of 2025, well ahead of historical trends and a clear indication that the business is moving beyond the last few years of stabilization. Existing customers also continue to perform well and respond to our improving customer experience, as reflected in lower churn, and higher occupancy. Market conditions remain mixed by geography. Although utilization across the industry remains strong as storage continues to provide an affordable space alternative to housing. New supply continues to pressure certain Sunbelt markets. But that supply is declining and should support a recovery there over time.

In many coastal and Midwest markets, where supply levels are lower, we are seeing more normalized revenue growth. In the 2% to 4% range with further improvement likely as supply declines. Los Angeles is improving following the expiration of state of emergency pricing restrictions earlier this year returning to positive revenue growth. Importantly, improvement extended beyond Los Angeles, and was broad based including across parts of the Sunbelt. Within our top 25 markets, 60% are generating positive year over year revenue growth and over 70% are showing sequential improvement. Our focus is on carrying this momentum into 2027 when supply conditions should become more favorable.

Expense growth was roughly flat in the quarter, supported by favorable performance in property taxes, payroll, utilities, and indirect cost. Property tax results included better than expected assessments in Illinois, and successful appeals in Texas. Outside of the same store pool, and excluding strategic acquisitions, non same store NOI grew 22%, while ancillary NOI grew 21%. Both were ahead of expectations and remain meaningful contributors to shareholder value. Turning to 2026 guidance. We are raising full year guidance for the second time this year. Supported by improving leading indicators and stronger than expected core operating performance.

We are raising core FFO guidance by $0.10 or 0.6% to 16.90 to $17.10 per share with a midpoint of $17 This increase reflects improved same store performance and continued strong contributions from non same store and ancillary NOI. At the midpoint, same store revenue and NOI growth are now expected to be positive 0.25% and negative 0.3%, respectively. Those represent improvements of 45 and 85 basis points from our prior outlook. The revised full year guidance continues to assume low single digit move in rate growth and modestly positive year over year occupancy, continued churn improvement is expected to drive higher ECRI contributions. Turning to transactions. Value creation.

Year to date, we have deployed or committed over $12.5 billion across acquisitions, development and lending. With a weighted average going-in yield in the low-5s and a stabilized yield in the high-6s. PS Next is a key driver of those returns, as our best in class operating platform helps us generate more NOI and cash flow in a storage property than the competition. Importantly, this activity has been funded on a leverage neutral basis Higher interest rates have increased hurdle rates and moderated transaction activity However, our strong balance sheet and operating platform position us well to capitalize on a potential reset in asset values. Lastly, balance sheet and capital markets.

Our balance sheet remains exceptionally strong in terms of leverage, liquidity, and capacity. Year to date, we have completed over $12 billion of capital markets activity, fully funding our external growth initiatives In the third quarter, we issued $1.2 billion of unsecured debt at a weighted average rate of 4.85%, That activity included our first Canadian bond issuance, and continued use of our hedge program, including a 10-year treasury hedge at 4.32% of which we still have $500 million remaining for future use. $900 million of this issuance year to date was subsequently swapped to Canadian rates to hedge our PS Canada equity exposure. Further reducing those rates by approximately 1%.

We have also entered into forward sale agreements under our ATM program year to date, that are expected to generate nearly $400 million of future net proceeds at approximately $326 per share. At quarter end, available liquidity was approximately $4.1 billion between cash and our line of credit supplemented by approximately $600 million of expected annual free cash flow. Our balance sheet remains 1 of the best in the REIT sector. With net debt to EBITDA of 3.4 times debt plus preferred equity to EBITDA of 4.7 times and debt plus preferred equity to enterprise value remaining in the low 20% range.

While debt metrics were modestly elevated quarter-over-quarter by 0.4 turns, due to the transaction timing we expect the 2 major transactions to be slightly deleveraging over time. In summary, operating momentum improved. Guidance increased, The value creation engine remained active. And the balance sheet was further strengthened. We are executing today with an eye to the future and building multiple drivers of long term per share earnings growth. With that, I will turn the call back to the operator for Q and A.

#3644. Why Sophia Bush Really Left Chicago P.D. After 4 Seasons

Sophia Bush shared her shocking exit from Chicago P.D. in 2017 was the result of alleged abuse she experienced from a colleague while working on the show.

Sophia Bush is shedding light on a challenging moment in her career.

Nearly a decade after her shocking exit from Chicago P.D. in 2017, Bush—who played Detective Erin Lindsay on the NBC police procedural drama for four seasons—alleged in her new memoir And I Will Tell You Mine that she left the show after experiencing ongoing abuse from one of her colleagues.

“An inappropriate comment. A raised voice. A hair pull,” she wrote in the book. “A held breath. A clenched jaw. A clenched fist. Fist connecting with flesh. And then, worse. Much worse.”

According to Bush, while the alleged abuse began during a New York City press run ahead of the show’s premiere, it quickly became clear the behavior was becoming a troubling new normal.

“Things done when there were fewer people in the room, but the rooms were never empty. Averted eyes, silenced protests, and whispers when something went wrong,” she wrote. “Knowing our set was the one talked about on all the others. Bracing for conflict, for screaming, for humiliation when something, anything, triggered outsized rage.”

But for four seasons, the 44-year-old shared she tried to prioritize the good of the show and ignore the mistreatment, a lesson she says she learned while filming One Tree Hill. (Bush was one of multiple women who in 2017 accused the show’s creator Mark Schwahn of sexual harassment. Though he never issued a public statement on the matter, he was subsequently fired from his post on The Royals that year.)

“At the time, nobody knew. Not really. Not enough. That’s what I told myself, anyway. I’ve been told I am alarmingly high functioning, but up until then, I had just called it ‘being a professional,’” she wrote. “I’d learned it on my first show: Put the job, the family, above all else, and never let a guy who can’t control himself ding your work ethic or your integrity. You can take it.”

“I’d had a few years of lovely sets in between, but a decade of training never leaves you,” she added, “and in this heightened version of having my ability to perform tested? I snapped right back into that rhythm.”

But after experiencing physical symptoms from the stress, including hives, weight fluctuations and hair loss, Bush made the decision to leave the show during the summer of 2016. As she put it, “I was done holding my breath.”

E! News has reached out to a representative for NBC for comment but has not yet heard back.

For more stars whose exits from hit TV series shocked fans, read on.

Gregg Sulkin, Wizards Beyond Waverly Place

In the Wizards of Waverly Place spinoff's shocking series finale, Selena Gomez's Alex Russo reunited with teen love Mason Greyback (Sulkin)—only to reveal a devastating twist.

Greyback revealed his character died during the August 2026 episode, then his ghost filled in the gaps on what Alex has been up to in the years since his passing.

"You have a whole other life, Alex," he told her. "You got married. You have a beautiful daughter. And she needs you. That’s the life that you’re meant to live. You have to go back."

The couple announced that the third season of The Valley will be their last following a tumultuous season during which Danny’s drinking habits were questioned and he was accused of acting inappropriately while drinking.

We are fully committed to each other and our beautiful children.”

#3643. Verity: How To Watch Anne Hathaway's Colleen Hoover Movie At Home

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Curious about the Colleen Hoover adaptation "Verity," but didn't go see it in theaters? I've got great news: You can now rent or buy it on demand on major streaming platforms, including Amazon Prime Video.

To say Colleen Hoover adaptations are uneven at best is an understatement, and though I reviewed "Verity" right here at /Film and called it "dead on arrival," I also think it's among the best, if not the best, adaptation of Hoover's work. So what's going on with this movie at a glance? Directed by Michael Showalter and adapted by Nick Antosca from Hoover's novel of the same name, "Verity" opens as middlingly successful fiction writer Lowen Ashleigh (Dakota Johnson) heads to an important meeting at a publishing house before she ends up getting splattered with blood after a cyclist gets hit by a cab. (Yes, it's weird that the inciting incident of this movie is this insane and is never mentioned again, but it's a Hoover movie. Go with it.) After Lowen is comforted by a handsome stranger who also lends her his shirt for her meeting, she reunites with that same guy at the meeting ... and as it turns out he's Jeremy Crawford (Josh Hartnett), husband of the best-selling thriller writer Verity Crawford (Anne Hathaway).

As Jeremy explains, Verity is unable to complete her best-selling series, but she's a fan of Lowen's work, and she wants to hire Lowen as a ghostwriter. Before long, Lowen is moving into the Crawfords' absurdly beautiful and massive Vermont home, scouring a draft of Verity's apparently secret autobiography, and finding herself in compromising positions with Jeremy. Also, is Verity actually in a catatonic state? There's a lot going on here and almost all of it is categorically nuts, but Hathaway makes it (slightly) fun.

Whether or not you think "Verity" is any good, filmgoers certainly showed their support for the Colleen Hoover cinematic universe by seeing this movie in droves when it came out in the US on October 2, 2026. "Verity" was an indisputable box office success when it hit theaters, and I don't think there's any doubt that we'll be getting way more Hoover movies as a result; unfortunately for anyone curious about this aforementioned cinematic universe, "Verity" is probably Hoover's best book, which isn't saying much.

Still, I'll admit that "Verity" has its moments, and every single one of those moments is thanks to the enduring talent and power of Anne Hathaway. Hathaway has had a pretty great 2026 thus far, and even though "Verity" isn't her best performance of the year, she still gives 150% in every single scene. Because of the way the film and story are structured, Hathaway's Verity primarily only shares scenes in flashback with Josh Hartnett's Jeremy — sadly, she and Dakota Johnson's Lowen only get one steamy, sapphic moment before we learn the whole thing was nothing more than a dream — but Hathaway is absolutely electric as the devious, smirking, sexy Verity, who finds herself incandescently envious of her own children after having to share Jeremy's attention and love.

"Verity" was a relatively recent release, so the fact that it's already available to buy or rent on demand might feel surprising until you recall that it's an Amazon MGM production, which also likely means it'll be free for Prime members eventually. For now, you can rent or buy "Verity" on Amazon and other platforms.

#3642. Sufjan Stevens Makes First Live Appearance in Over Eight Years

Sufjan Stevens made a surprise appearance during Meg Lui’s show at Tempo in Kingston, New York on September 25, accompanying the singer-songwriter on piano and backing vocals for her Instant Validation track “Elvis Queen.” It marked his first public musical performance in over eight years. In 2023, Stevens revealed he was diagnosed with Guillain-Barré syndrome—a rare disorder in which the body’s immune system attacks the nerves—and was relearning how to walk and use his hands. Watch a clip from the performance here. Two weeks later, Stevens joined Lui on stage again, at Kingston’s O+ Festival.

The September 25 concert was Lui’s record release event for the Stevens-produced Instant Validation. The Hudson Valley-based artist had worked with Stevens prior to her debut, contributing background vocals to his most recent album, 2023’s Javelin. The two met through mutual friends in the Catskills and recorded Validation at Stevens’ home studio. “With Sufjan there was so much more out-of-the-box thinking, and we ended up adding layers upon layers that created an unrestrained soundscape for the record,” Lui previously said of the experience.

In a 2025 interview with Vulture’s Craig Jenkins, Stevens updated fans on how he was doing: “I’ve had some pretty difficult things happen to me, so I’m in a state of repair and survival,” he said. “I’m not really in any state of mind or any position to go on tour yet. But I’m starting to see the light. I’m starting to feel a sense of direction toward something meaningful and substantial. I’ve been focusing on the moment and on things that feel very silly and Zen: serenity and acceptance and duty and stewardship.”

In the years following his diagnosis, Stevens reissued a 20th anniversary edition of Seven Swans and a 10th anniversary version of Carrie & Lowell, produced a children’s album for John Legend, and hopped on new songs by Mina Tindle and Mouse on Mars’ Jan St. Werner.

Read about Javelin at No. 6 in The 50 Best Albums of 2023.

#3641. I’m a Deals Writer, and These Are My Top 10 Tech Deals This Week

As Lifehacker's deals writer, I'm always looking for the best bargains on TVs, speakers, laptops, and other tech. Now that it's Friday, I've gathered together this week's sales highlights, all of which I've vetted using my favorite price-tracking tools to make sure they are actually good deals.

This week, you can find bargains on the Apple Watch 10, Samsung soundbars, my favorite robot vacuum, QLED TVs, and more.

I got the Roborock Qrevo CurvX since it seemed like a great fit for the fact that I have a dog, a mix of different floor types, and a gym with a workout mat. Amazon currently has it for $749.99 (originally $1,499.99), a 50% discount, and the lowest price it has ever been.

Shokz is one of the best brands making bone conduction headphones right now. Right now, the older but still capable OpenRun Pros are discounted to $109.95 (originally $179.99), the second-lowest price they've reached. As the name implies, these are meant to be used on outdoor runs. They have an IP55 rating for dust- and water-resistance, are lightweight and comfortable, and can be worn with bicycle helmets and sunglasses.

The 57-inch Samsung Odyssey Neo G9, one of the best ultra-wide gaming monitors on the market, is currently on sale for $1,499.99 (originally $2,499.99 at launch)—that's its second-best discount. It has impressive specs, but you'll need a top-notch gaming computer with specific outputs to take full advantage of its 240Hz refresh rate potential.

The Sennheiser Momentum 4 has been around since 2022, and they were only succeeded this summer by the Sennheiser Momentum 5. However, they are still great headphones, and with their $202.56 price (originally $299.99) after a 32% discount, they're a great opportunity to get top-quality headphones at a more than reasonable price.

Amazon has dropped the 46mm Apple Watch Series 10 (GPS + Cellular) to $679 from $799, taking $120 off this titanium model. Although it's no longer Apple's newest smartwatch, the Series 10 still has plenty going for it, with PCMag giving it an "outstanding" rating. Much of its appeal comes down to the design, which is thinner and more comfortable than the Series 9, with a larger always-on OLED display that gives you more room to read notifications and messages without making the watch feel particularly bulky. The titanium case also gives it a more polished appearance than most smartwatches, especially if you plan to wear it beyond workouts.

Samsung’s HW-Q600F soundbar is down to $379.97 at Amazon from $499.99, a $110 discount that brings it to its lowest price ever. This 2025 model sits in the middle of Samsung’s Q-Series lineup and pairs a sleek, angular bar with a subwoofer in a 3.1.2 setup. Dolby Atmos also brings a modest sense of height, and effects track cleanly from left to right. You get a graphic equalizer and sound presets for adjusting the audio to your preferences through the included remote.

Right now, the 65-inch QM8L is $1,197.99 (originally $2,499.99), its lowest price since it launched in April. The QM8L has a “Zero Border” bezel design, which gives it a modern, minimalistic look. When you turn it on, you'll likely note how bright this TV gets. TCL claims it reaches 6,000 nits; no OLED can come close to this unit's brightness. Another important spec to note here is the local dimming zones: TCL claims this TV has up to 4,000, which means it gets top-tier backlight control, limited light blooming, and incredible contrast levels that some might not be able to distinguish from an OLED.

The Ultimate Ears Miniroll is down to $49.99 (originally $79.99), the second-lowest it has sold for so far. It’s a small, flat, puck-shaped Bluetooth speaker that weighs just over half a pound, with a removable rubber strap you can attach to a backpack, bike handlebar, or shower caddy. Despite its size, the Miniroll can get surprisingly loud—which PCMag confirms in its review—and at moderate volume, it has enough sound for a kitchen or backyard without sounding thin. As for its battery life, you get about 12 hours of playback before it needs a charge over USB-C, while its IP67 rating protects it from dust and a brief dunk in water.

TCL's NXTPAPER 14 Android tablet, which has a massive matte screen with different viewing modes for reading, streaming video, and writing, is $349.97 (originally $469.99) after a 19% discount. With a 14.3-inch display, the NXTPAPER 14 might not be as portable as other tablets on the market, but the screen itself is the biggest perk. The matte display has a paper-like feel and three different viewing modes: You can switch from black-and-white Ink Paper Mode to Color Paper Mode (a must for comics) to full color (for streaming video or using other full-motion apps) with the push of a button.

If you're looking for a premium model, Samsung’s 65-inch S90H OLED TV is down to $1,497.99 from $2,697.99 at launch, saving you over $1,000. The sale price is easier to justify when you consider that this is a new 2026 model. If the 65-inch model does not suit your space, Samsung also sells the S90H in 42-, 48-, 55-, 77-, and 83-inch versions, and those sizes are discounted as well.

#3640. ‘The Gilded Age’ Review: Season 4 Is Team Bertha’s Time to Shine

Early in “The Gilded Age” Season 4, during a moment of great tension, a hoity-toity British doctor in a cravat loses his senses and snaps at Bertha Russell (Carrie Coon): “Let me be clear,” the doctor hisses. “You do not understand the situation. Please leave me.”

Bertha, with little more than a flick of her bulging eyes (which could mean anything from “Oh shit” to “If this goes wrong, I will roast your heart over the raging pyre of your dismembered corpse”), does as she’s urged and retreats. The precarious circumstances don’t allow for pushback — time is of the essence — and Bertha, in her boundless wisdom, knows she has no choice but to trust this man and his alleged expertise. Related Stories James Gunn and Peter Safran’s DC Experiment: What’s Working, What Isn’t, and What’s Next Why Emmy Wins and Hollywood Recognition Don’t Guarantee Jobs for BIPOC Artists: ‘The Needle Has Not F***ing Moved’

But know this, dear readers: Bertha Russell will not be dismissed again.

Not in Season 4, anyway. Much to the relief of Team Bertha acolytes everywhere (there are dozens of us!), the foreboding events at the end of Season 3 do not elicit further defamation for our masterful social climber. That’s not to say her life is suddenly all sunshine and rainbows. (Although let’s be honest: “The Gilded Age” lives — and thrives — in a world of sunshine and rainbows, where a patient who dies because of segregated hospitals is treated with equal consequence to tea that’s served with too much milk.) (Plus, Bertha’s life in particular is pretty darn close to bliss: hot husband, infinite gowns, gobs of money, a dud of a son, sure, but a boring kid isn’t the worst fate — and Gladys married a duke!)

Nevertheless, amid the relative challenges Bertha faces in Season 4, it’s not a spoiler to report that “The Gilded Age” creator Julian Fellowes and his co-writing executive producer Sonja Warfield wisely refrain from punishing their lovingly nicknamed Opera Mommy for the imaginary crimes cooked up by another man who’s taken leave of his senses: Train Daddy (aka George Russell, aka Morgan Spector, aka the future Robert Langdon). In fact, ol’ George has a pretty rough year—a fitting comeuppance for a once-proud titan of industry who needs to be humbled to remember all he has and who’s to thank for it. (Not to mention a fun season-long arc for Spector, whose growling baritone was begging to break bad anyway.)

Season 3 encouraged a thankless take on Bertha, repeatedly trotting out the idea that her pursuit of power blinded her to the needs of her family: a son who (wrongly) blamed her for his breakup with his vanilla crush, Marian (Louisa Jacobson); a daughter who (briefly) blamed her for an arranged marriage that she soon realized was awesome; and a husband who routinely brings his business to the edge of bankruptcy only to be saved by the brilliant ideas and shrewd scheming of his wife — yet who still somehow thought Bertha was the one playing it too fast and loose with their family’s love.

Bertha, after all, is only guilty of doing what’s best for her daughter, raising her family’s clout, and preserving the Russell name as essential benefactors of the arts (that new opera house didn’t open itself) rather than greedy railroad tycoons with terminal severance packages. Suggesting anything else was, to be frank, imbecilic, but “The Gilded Age” played it out through the final shot, where a distraught Bertha watched her jealous husband ride off in a huff after informing her he wouldn’t be coming home until he “knows what he wants.”

That’s where Season 4 picks up: months later, but the mood unchanged. Bertha is on the other side of the ocean, caring for a very pregnant Gladys (Taissa Farmiga), while George is in the States, struggling to keep his business in the black. Elsewhere in New York, Peggy (Denée Benton) savors the success of her first novel while her fiancé, the nice doctor William (Jordan Donica), moves up in the medical ranks. Jack (Ben Ahlers) is getting comfortable in his new house, courtesy of his innovative alarm clocks, but he’s less comfortable with his new status (which requires etiquette classes from his servant friends) and his next invention (which isn’t as easy to sell as the first).

Everyone is moving up! Building careers! Making names for themselves! And then there’s Larry (Harry Richardson) and Marian, who bounce between tedious flirtations and equally tedious frustrations, almost like they’re two attractive twenty-somethings who desperately want to bone but can only get there by boring the pants off each other. Admittedly, Marian gestures toward finding a purpose beyond playing house by volunteering to help the less fortunate, but even Elizabeth Marvel, the program’s guiding hand, struggles to keep our aspiring housewife from dreaming of her sole, snoozy passion.

Luckily, Agnes (Christine Baranski) and Ada (Cynthia Nixon) are more than happy to spice things up: The latter falls for forbidden fruit, and the former hosts the President of the United States.

You read that right: the President of America agrees to have dinner at Agnes’ cozy Manhattan mansion, which provides the seasonal centerpiece “Gilded Age” fans are sure to be sick over: Who’s invited? What will be served? What will they wear? All these gossipy questions and more help structure the better part of Season 4, building ample anticipation for the historic evening by pitting as many of its invited guests against each other as possible in a steel-cage death match of affronted expressions and polite self-satisfaction.

The stakes are set as finely as the silverware, with bragging rights — for socialites and their servers — taking dramatic precedence over the fate of American rail travel, as it should be: In “The Gilded Age,” who saves face is always more substantial than whatever unnamed sums are secured or squandered, and the presence of POTUS does little to upend those pleasingly backward priorities.

Speaking of presence, Season 4 does feel the squeeze of its sprawling cast. It’s a little too light on downstairs character development, even if the dinner involves a few favorites and implements one outstanding plot device I shan’t spoil here. Some supporting players go underutilized (Jack as well as the scheming, wannabe power couple, Oscar and Enid), while unworthy regulars become story anchors entrusted with too much emotional weight. (By now, you surely know to whom I’m referring.)

Ah, so it goes in a series with an insatiable appetite for perpetual opulence and little tolerance for legitimate loss. Far be it from me to say what influences Fellowes, but he seems unwilling to repeat past mistakes or kill off a beloved character — any beloved character — too soon, if at all.

And that’s just fine. Really! We need more cheery, soapy TV filled with simple delights. “The Gilded Age” is the rare HBO Sunday-night staple with even-keeled ambitions and no extra-dour drama. It’s a frothy, let’s-leave-’em-smiling show that’s open to acknowledging serious issues — misogyny, bigotry, and oppression all inform the cast’s choices, to one extent or another — so long as they don’t linger long enough to spoil the vibe.

That’s why Bertha’s ascendance comes as such a relief. Nothing ruins a good time like misplaced sympathy, and siding with the man of the house in a series that’s more rooted in women’s perspectives with each passing season just feels wrong. What little deep thinking “The Gilded Age” invites, there’s something to be said for how it depicts women shaping a country that actively tries to operate outside of their reach. Bertha, with her new money and never-say-die demeanor, isn’t afraid to roll up her sleeves and do whatever it takes. Seeing her shine isn’t just good and right given her specific circumstances (re: George); it’s good and right because it bucks historical precedent (rah-rah, feminism!).

She grasps each situation perfectly — and she’s not going anywhere. Thank goodness.

“The Gilded Age” Season 4 premieres Sunday, November 1 at 9 p.m. ET on HBO. New episodes will be released weekly through the finale on December 20.

#3639. Show HN: Specification First Agentic Development Plugin and Prove

Describe the feature in your own words. Four commands write the spec, check it against your code, build it in its own worktree and deliver it through your checks. Run several side by side and get back verified pull requests.

The rows with a dot are what the skills do on their own: reading, writing, checking, building. You type the lines in boxes.

This is how Agent Smith is built every day, across more than a thousand specs.

A fresh reviewer checks the spec against the code before the build. A gate runs your checks before the commit.

One branch and worktree per spec. Ids come from the clock, so two agents never take the same one.

A reviewed spec has already answered the open questions, so the agent builds to its done criteria instead of coming back to ask.

One spec, one branch, one commit. A pull request carries one spec or a whole series, and every change has the same shape.

State lives in context.yaml. Restart the machine or the agent and the work picks up where it stopped.

Each choice is logged with what it beat and why, so the reasoning stays next to the code.

Cut a bigger change into a series of specs. /spec-first:deliver-series reviews all of them, builds the groups that share no file at the same time, verifies the merged tree and opens one pull request.

3 worktrees building in parallel · every spec commit through the gate

An example run of /spec-first:deliver-series. Specs that share no file are built at the same time; specs that depend on each other share a group.

Four skills take a piece of work through its own branch and worktree. Each one leaves a file behind for the next step and for whoever reads the history later.

Goal, steps, facts with file and line, what done means. No code yet.

Every citation resolved, then a reviewer who never saw the chat attacks it.

Contracts, code, wiring, tests, following the project's own rules.

One commit through the gate, pushed, pull request opened.

Every spec, from planned to done, with its facts and done criteria.

What each spec chose, what it chose over, and why.

What you told the agent once, so you don't say it twice.

/spec-first:bootstrap-project writes the contexts once from your code: stack, rules and the checks a commit must pass. After that, each spec adds one line of state.

A REFUSE costs you one spec. Without the review, the same mistake would have cost you the branch.

Ticket providers so pipelines can pull work from GitHub and Jira.

Adapter over repository: read-mostly data, no unit of work needed.

Step jira needs a second auth mode the config can't hold yet. AppConfig.cs:15 Move it to its own spec.

Paging is missing: GitHub returns 30 issues per page. GitHubClient.cs:88 Add a done criterion.

A script opens each file and line the spec cites. A claim nobody checked in the code has to be listed as an assumption.

A new agent with no conversation history reads the code as it is, not as the spec says it is.

Nobody has to write documentation afterwards. While it builds, the agent logs each choice that wasn't obvious: what it chose, what it chose over, and why. One file per spec, next to the code.

cat decisions/*.yaml is the project's reasoning, spec by spec, in the same history as the code it explains.

How the operator wants things done, a constraint outside the repo, a pointer elsewhere. One fact per file, one line in an index.

Restart the machine, restart the agent, open context.yaml. It knows what is done, what is active and what comes next.

The plugin's commit gate runs every verify stage your contexts declare when a commit names its spec. All green, it commits. The first red stage blocks it, and its output goes back to the agent.

The id in parentheses triggers it. -F , -C and --amend --no-edit are gated too.

A spec commit with no line in the ledger never met the gate. A project with its own gate says gate: project, and the plugin steps aside.

The open source agentic software factory: a ticket goes in, a verified pull request comes out. Agent Smith is entirely built with this method, and the method grew out of building it. Everything in the plugin ran there first.

In Claude Code the plugin does the bookkeeping and runs the gate. Other agents follow the same files through AGENTS.md, and a git hook runs the same gate.

Codex, Cursor and Copilot read AGENTS.md when a session starts. It holds the read order and the workflow, and every step says how to do it by hand, the review brief for a fresh session included.

The commit-msg hook runs your verify: stages on every commit that names a spec, from any agent or the terminal. git commit --no-verify skips it. The evidence check is skills/review-spec/scripts/check-evidence.py in the same clone and needs PyYAML.

Specification-driven agentic development, in short answers.

A way of working with AI coding agents in which every change starts as a written spec in your repository. A fresh reviewer checks the spec against your code before anything is built, the agent builds it on its own branch and worktree, and the commit lands only when your checks pass.

The spec drives the work from start to finish. It names the goal, the facts it rests on with file and line, the steps and the done criteria, and the agent builds to exactly that. Spec-first agentic development is specification-driven agentic development in which the spec is reviewed against the code before the first line is written.

No. The Claude Code plugin runs the steps as commands. Codex, Cursor, Copilot or a terminal follow the same files through AGENTS.md, and a git commit-msg hook runs the same commit gate in every clone where it is installed.

You still describe the feature in your own words and talk it through. After that, the agent works from a reviewed spec instead of from the last prompt, logs each decision with its reason, and a commit lands only when your build and tests pass.

A one-sentence goal, the facts it rests on with file and line, the steps, what an upgrade costs an operator, and the done criteria. The decisions made while building go into a separate YAML file next to it.
