#3636. Nvidia Plays Kingmaker Again. Why the Meta Deal Is a Winning Move.

Just days after Meta Platforms (META -1.65%) announced plans to raise $97.5 billion to fund its AI infrastructure build-out, the company is signing on a new partner: Nvidia (NVDA +0.90%).

Nvidia and Meta announced on Tuesday morning that they would expand their partnership around Muse and the Muse family of models, and Nvidia will invest $3 billion in Meta's new equity offering.

As part of the collaboration, Meta will expand Vera Rubin and Vera CPU deployment to boost performance and scale workloads.

Nvidia said that the surging growth of the new Meta Muse personal AI agent led to the expanded partnership.

For Meta, the company receives a $3 billion investment from Nvidia and helps ensure it has access to the chips and hardware it will need as it aggressively expands its AI capabilities and invests in new data centers and infrastructure.

Nvidia continues to dominate the market for AI chips and has much of the cutting-edge technology that hyperscalers need, so it's important for companies like Meta to establish a close relationship with Nvidia.

For Nvidia, it's the latest example of the company shoring up its AI empire by bringing customers, suppliers, and other stakeholders into its fold through a series of investments, partnerships, and contractual commitments.

These moves give Nvidia both preferred treatment among the partners that it needs most, putting it on the inside track in AI, and the investment it's made also gives it exposure to stocks like Meta, meaning their success is Nvidia's success.

At a time when companies like Meta, Anthropic, Space Exploration Technology, and others are raising massive amounts of capital, Nvidia is a reliable investor with deep pockets and a desire to invest across the AI spectrum, meaning we're likely to see more deals like this one.

As of the end of the second quarter, Nvidia already owned stakes in Intel, an integrated chipmaker; SpaceX, which has become fast-growing AI infrastructure company; CoreWeave and Nebius, two neocloud companies; Coherent, a provider of optics solutions for AI infrastructure; Synopsys, a specialist in electronic design automation (EDA); Nokia, a developer of AI-Radio Access Network technology, and Generate Biomedics, which uses machine learning and generative AI to design custom therapeutics.

The company has also taken stakes in OpenAI and Anthropic, ensuring it has close partnerships with the two leading AI labs and that they continue to buy its chips. It forged an agreement with OpenAI to invest up to $100 billion in the company as it deploys at least 10 gigawatts of AI data centers using Nvidia systems.

Nvidia faces competition in the market for AI chips, most notably from Advanced Micro Devices. AMD has followed a similar playbook to Nvidia to a smaller degree and even announced a $1 billion investment in Meta shortly after Nvidia said it would invest $3 billion in Meta. However, Nvidia's leadership is going to be difficult to unseat, given its network of partnerships, deep relationships, and cutting-edge technology.

Nvidia will have to maintain its pace of innovation to ensure it keeps the technological lead, and it has done that since the launch of ChatGPT. In fact, SpaceX CEO Elon Musk recently declared that his Nvidia's Vera Rubin architecture was superior and said that SpaceX would exclusively build on Nvidia hardware.

Expect Nvidia to continue to forge more deals like the one it just did with Meta, especially as the AI infrastructure spenders look for new sources of capital. Nvidia is becoming a kingmaker, and that will only widen its economic moat as the AI boom continues.

#3633. ‘The Mosquito Bowl’ Review: ‘Friday Night Lights’ Meets ‘Five Came Back’ in Peter Berg’s Formulaic Netflix Movie About a World War II Football Game

There have been any number of movies about the hell of fighting in World War II, the most visceral of which have been made in the years since “Saving Private Ryan” provided a very enduring template for how modern filmmakers could render that carnage with both the spectacle of a blockbuster and the horror of a memory. Peter Berg’s brutal and concussive “The Mosquito Bowl” is definitely one of them! No one could ever say that it’s not.

What differentiates this unsparing epic from the scores of other stories about the Greatest Generation, however, is that some of its characters are good at football. Indeed, these All-American college boys are so good at football that, on Christmas Eve 1944, the soldiers are ordered to play a morale-boosting game of two-hand touch on the beach where their units are preparing for the Battle of Okinawa. Related Stories ‘The Gilded Age’ Review: Season 4 Is Team Bertha’s Time to Shine ‘I Play Rocky’ Wins Virginia Film Fest Audience Award as Peter Farrelly’s Stallone Drama Readies for Theaters

And for the span of the eight-minute montage during which “The Mosquito Bowl” actually makes good on its title, it’s easy to picture how the famous scrimmage — fought between a roster of impossibly beautiful athletes in their primes — might have been used to backstop a story that accentuated the senseless waste of sending thousands of young men to their deaths. Alas, for the rest of this 126-minute film, that waste is better expressed through Berg’s failure to make anything more of their lives, as the “Spenser Confidential” auteur’s latest Netflix project does almost nothing else to distinguish itself from a genre so well-worn that it’s seemingly run out of ways to blow up a human body.

Why “almost”? I was hoping you wouldn’t ask. For starters, there’s the decision to cast thin-skinned ESPN commentator Pat McAfee as an R. Lee Ermey-inspired drill sergeant. “The Mosquito Bowl” also stands out for how confusingly it conflates the thrill and teamwork of combat sport with the violence and camaraderie of armed conflict; war is presented as both a natural extension of what these boys have been trained to do on the gridiron (to the point that the soldiers briefly diagram their plan of attack as if it were a football play), and also the catastrophe that robbed them of a more innocent purpose. (Berg loves the troops as much as he hates the fact that generals and presidents so wantonly sacrifice their lives.) Someone declares that “The Marine Corps is the greatest offensive line in history,” and yet the film’s characters don’t seem to have any special advantages on the field of battle, as it would be glib to suggest that even the country’s best college running back might be able to slip free of a mortar blast. It’s one thing to argue that football is war, and another, much more asinine thing to argue that war is football.

Perhaps that’s why the Mosquito Bowl itself only took up a single page of the Buzz Bissinger book on which Berg’s film is based, and why that source material used the game as a lens through which a light could be shined on the semi-forgotten men who found their way into the lineup that day. It’s been suggested that Bissinger played up the sports angle in order to capitalize on the success of his “Friday Night Lights,” and given that Berg adapted that book into a film as well, it stands to reason that his “Mosquito Bowl” should do the same.

The problem is that, in stark contrast to Bissinger, Berg and co-writer Mark L. Smith only seem to care about their characters as a means to an end. The project’s anti-war ethos is solely conveyed by reducing collegiate legends into cannon fodder, as the movie is considerably less interested in what bonds these boys together than it is in the shells that blast them apart.

Perhaps that explains why I’ve been able to write almost 650 words without bothering to mention any of their names. To the extent that it matters to a film so blandly de-saturated that everyone in it looks half-dead from the start, Nicholas Galitzine stars as Brown University legend John McLaughry — the only character we know for certain will survive the assault on Japan. (The movie opens with a sustained flash-forward of John staring longingly at an empty football field as “Friday Night Lights” veterans Explosions in the Sky layer their post-rock shimmer over the soundtrack). He’s got sad eyes, a square jaw, and a brunette girlfriend, which is more than you can say for Bob Baumann (Bill Skarsgård), who has a devil-may-care attitude that disappears real quick once his unit makes land on Tawara. The only real source of contextual friction is owed to the rivalry between Purdue fullback Tony Butkovich (a glinty and magnetic Ray Nicholson) and the straight-laced mama’s boy he beat in a big game before the attack on Pearl Harbor (Tom Francis as the stoic and decent Dave Schreiner).

Individually, Butkovich is the only one of these characters who has the chutzpah to carry the movie on his back. Incidentally, he’s the only one of these men who was heading to the pros before the war got in the way. Still, it’s engaging to see them all blitz through life on their home turf, and “The Mosquito Bowl” benefits from Berg’s handheld and ruggedly naturalistic approach to how the players comport themselves off the field (read: they say “fuck” a lot and generally aspire to feel more like real people than they do patronizing impressions of what men were like in the ’40s).

But then the war comes to Hawaii, a match-cut turns a flock of birds into some fighter jets, and these beloved football stars all find themselves rendered nearly anonymous by the sea of shaved heads that are waiting for them at book. Little is made of their civilian celebrity, and even less of their individual decisions to enlist. “This is my rifle — there are many others like it, but this one is mine.”

Berg takes militaristic uniformity as all the excuse he needs to avoid deviating from expectation, and nearly everything that happens between basic training and the big game on the beach near Okinawa feels like it could have happened in several dozen other World War II movies, and almost certainly did. Defiant warriors curdle into terrified kids, bullets whizz through ocean waves, snipers pick off predictable characters at unsuspecting — or very suspecting — moments, and even the most hardened of marines find themselves staring blankly into the middle distance as their friends are decimated on all sides. Berg has always had a flair for the frenetic, and while there’s a perversely fetishistic veneer to the attention he devotes to entrails and enemy screams, it serves the film’s purposes that war has seldom seemed less survivable than it does here. (The film has no interest in humanizing the Japanese, but tries to compensate for that by presenting a few of the Americans as unrepentant monsters).

Still, for all of its supposed verisimilitude, “The Mosquito Bowl” seems less indebted to the reality of war than it does to Hollywood’s idea of it, and its abject refusal to flesh out its characters — a dereliction of duty, if ever there was one — makes it feel more contrived rather than less. I’m happy for Berg that he found a way to combine all of his favorite things into a single project (football, the military, and massive fireballs are all well-accounted for, even if Mark Wahlberg’s absence makes the whole movie feel like it’s forgotten something important), but “The Mosquito Bowl” accomplishes very little beyond violently illustrating that war is worse than football. It does that with such fervor that it loses all sight of what those things might really have in common.

Netflix will release “The Mosquito Bowl” in theaters on Friday, October 30. It will be available to stream on Netflix starting Wednesday, November 25.

#3632. The Mythical Man-Month in the Age of AI

Last weekend I watched four people desperately trying to get a laptop to connect to a screen. I was happily observing their efforts, with zero intent to join in.

But someone nudged me: can't you have a look at it?

My reply: "Haha sorry no! My experience is that adding more people will not solve this problem any quicker".

I was of course -- indirectly -- referring to Brooks's Law:

"Adding manpower to a late software project makes it later."

It was formulated by computer scientist Frederick P. Brooks Jr. in his 1975 book The Mythical Man-Month, based on his experience managing the IBM OS/360 project.

However I got to thinking. Does this still hold up in the age of AI agents and LLMs?

At first glance, you’d think no. The traditional bottlenecks -- onboarding time, endless stand-ups, and office politics -- disappear overnight. An AI agent is up to speed in milliseconds and doesn't demand a coffee break.

But when you look closer, and the law hasn't vanished. It just mutated.

The famous "Baby Problem" remains: nine agents still can’t deliver a baby in one month. Sequential tasks are still sequential.

Overhead hasn't disappeared; it shifted: instead of managing people, you are now managing agents. Someone still needs to define "done", review the code, catch hallucinations, and stitch the pieces together.

Throw nine autonomous agents at a single codebase without crystal-clear orchestration, and they will happily rewrite each other's work in a high-speed loop.

Maybe the 2026 update to Brooks’s Law should be:

"Adding agents to a project with unclear requirements gets you the wrong answer faster."

#3629. Oct 28 Could Be A Huge Day For The Stock Market

Wednesday, Oct. 28, 2026 could bring some big swings for the stock market. That's because the Federal Reserve's Open Market Committee (FOMC) announces whether interest rates will move higher, lower, or be held steady.

Rate announcements tend to spark a burst of trading activity across the market regardless of the circumstances, but with bond yields on the rise and a significant portion of the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.60%) concentrated in artificial intelligence (AI) -- an industry that is now, more than ever, leaning on debt markets, the Fed's decision carries even more weight than usual.

Here's what investors should be watching.

The Fed has two main jobs: keep prices stable (ie, fight inflation) and drive maximum employment. This is its "dual mandate." The two goals generally pull in different directions: higher interest rates slow inflation by making borrowing and spending more expensive, but that tends to curb growth and keep businesses from hiring. Lower rates tend to make borrowing cheaper, leading to expansion, more employment, and higher wages. But all that money flowing through the system leads to inflation.

Here's what the Fed has to go on at this point.

While unemployment is low (4.2%), the latest jobs report gives officials a reason to be somewhat cautious about hiking rates. The economy added just 29,000 jobs in September. Hiring has generally been sluggish for some time, averaging 45,000 jobs a month over the last year.

At the same time, the other side of the equation is moving in the wrong direction: the Consumer Price Index (CPI) rose 3.7% in September from a year earlier. That was up from 3.4% in August.

The Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE) -- generally the inflation metric the Fed leans on most -- showed prices rising 3.4% annually in August. The September reading actually comes out on Thursday, a day after the rate decision. Keep in mind, the Fed's "target" for inflation is 2%.

Combined, it presents a somewhat mixed picture, with rising inflation and a less-than-stellar job market. That puts the Fed in a tough spot.

The biggest single factor driving inflation is energy. Gasoline prices jumped more than 9% in September and were responsible for about half of the monthly PCI increase.

The big question for the Fed is whether to "look through" the current inflation. If you remove the spike in gas prices, the recent readings look much tamer, and because the current spike in gas prices is largely being driven by the War in Iran, it's possible that the inflation could be "transitory" -- that is, it could cool down without the Fed's help if the war is brought to a close.

At the same time, the Fed may believe hiking rates would have little effect on gas prices given the reason they are elevated.

But if the current dynamic holds, those elevated gas prices could bleed into the rest of the economy and turn transitory inflation into something more systemic. That's basically the argument New York Fed President John Williams made in September.

The Fed is expected to leave rates unchanged tomorrow, choosing another month of wait-and-see. I don't expect a surprise here. But what investors should pay attention to is any signaling as to where rates might move in the future. If it signals more hikes are coming -- I think they will -- it could have a real impact on the AI trade.

Building data centers and filling them with chips is expensive, and companies -- even the massive hyperscalers like Meta and Amazon -- are increasingly borrowing to pay for it. Higher financing costs make those projects harder to justify and could lead to a pullback in spending if rates move high enough.

The rate announcement is set for Oct. 28 at 2 p.m. ET and will be followed by a press conference at 2:30 p.m.

Johnny Rice has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3628. Cristiano Ronaldo and Lionel Messi headline new Ballon d’Or Of All Time award: Full nominee list, eligibility rules, voting deadline, and prize explained

Cristiano Ronaldo and Lionel Messi are once again at the center of soccer’s greatest debate as France Football introduces a special award celebrating the most iconic Ballon d’Or-winning performances in history. The new initiative brings together legends from different generations, allowing supporters to revisit some of the sport’s most memorable individual achievements and decide which deserves the ultimate recognition.

The announcement comes as the prestigious award celebrates its 70th anniversary in 2026. However, the format introduces an unusual twist that could make the competition far more unpredictable than a conventional vote for the greatest player of all time.

France Soccer, the magazine behind the Ballon d’Or since 1956, has unveiled the Ballon d’Or Of All Time (B.O.A.T.), a public vote designed to identify the greatest individual Ballon d’Or-winning year in soccer history. Rather than simply selecting a player, voters must decide which specific winning campaign deserves to stand above the rest.

The distinction matters because a player with multiple Ballon d’Or trophies has several eligible seasons competing for votes. Messi, for example, has eight winning years represented, while Ronaldo has five, potentially dividing support between their individual achievements.

FIFA Ballon d’Or nominees Lionel Messi of Argentina and FC Barcelona (L) and Cristiano Ronaldo of Portugal and Real Madrid (R)

The shortlist features 10 legendary players who have won the Ballon d’Or at least twice during their careers.

Messi leads the historical standings with eight Ballon d’Or victories, followed by Ronaldo with five. Platini, Cruyff and Van Basten each collected three trophies, while the remaining five nominees won twice.

The organizers restricted eligibility to players who have won the Ballon d’Or at least twice, immediately excluding famous one-time winners such as Zinedine Zidane, Ronaldinho and Eusebio. Pele and Diego Maradona are also absent, but for a different historical reason.

During their playing careers, the Ballon d’Or was restricted to European players, meaning the two South American icons were not eligible under the award’s original rules. The shortlist therefore reflects the history and eligibility criteria of the traditional prize rather than a comprehensive ranking of every player who has ever played the game.

Fans can vote through the official Ballon d’Or website until October 27, 2026, at 11:59 p.m. Paris time. Each vote costs €1, and supporters can submit multiple votes rather than being restricted to a single choice.

According to the published details, participants receive access to an iconic Ballon d’Or photograph and an opportunity to win two tickets to the 2027 Ballon d’Or ceremony. The B.O.A.T. results are scheduled to be announced on October 28, two days after the main 2026 ceremony at London’s Palladium on October 26.

Cristiano Ronaldo’s 2008 season leads Lionel Messi by a huge margin in the Ballon d’Or of All Time fan vote.

Lionel Messi’s attendance at the Ballon d’Or gala in London remains unconfirmed less than two weeks before the October 26 ceremony.

The 39-year-old remains among the leading names in the 2026 Ballon d’Or conversation after another productive season for club and country, but his relationship with the individual prize appears to have changed considerably over the years.

Lamine Yamal says the Ballon d'Or should reward style over goals, pointing to Messi and Ronaldinho as his model.

#3627. Dead Man’s Bones Unveil First New Song in 17 Years

After a short teaser clip lit up the internet with news of their return, Dead Man’s Bones—the duo of Ryan Gosling and Zach Shields—are officially back with their first new music in 17 years. “Lost in the Night” was produced with longtime collaborator Tim Anderson, and it gets a black-and-white music video starring Hamnet’s Noah Jupe and The Summer I Turned Pretty’s Rain Spencer that draws on its lovestruck lyrics.

Dead Man’s Bones set out to write new music in recent years, but couldn‘t settle on where to make it. The duo tried a number of Los Angeles studios before driving to a farm outside of the city with some gear in tow—two guitars, two drum machines, and two synthesizers—where they proceeded to record “Lost in the Night” in one day.

The first and only Dead Man’s Bones album came out in 2009. Gosling and Shields worked closely with the Silverlake Conservatory Children’s Choir, a California education organization founded by Flea, on the self-titled debut to achieve its cross of indie rock, gothic folk, and doo-wop. The duo retreated from the public eye after promoting their first album. However, they found renewed interest over the years through covers (like Cat Power tackling “Pa Pa Power” for her 2022 album Covers), various film and TV syncs, and a seasonal bump courtesy of social media users playing their music around Halloween.

#3626. Elizabeth Hurley Reveals Major Milestone in Billy Ray Cyrus Romance

Elizabeth Hurley and Billy Ray Cyrus are taking a major step, with her sharing that she and the country singer are planning to live together in Los Angeles while she works on her next project.

Elizabeth Hurley is strictly confident about her relationship with Billy Ray Cyrus.

In fact, the Strictly Confidential actress is so certain about their romance that she’s ready for an extended stay together while she works on her next project.

As Hurley explained on the Oct. 23 episode of the Off Air with Jane & Fi podcast, as she films the upcoming season of The ‘Burbs in Los Angeles, Cyrus is going to join her, noting, “Yeah, we’re going to live together out there.”

Since going public with their romance in April 2025, the pair have lived separately, with the Austin Powers alum staying full-time in the English countryside and the Hannah Montana star calling his Nashville-area farm his home base.

That said, the “Achy Breaky Heart” singer has prioritized spending time across the pond with his love, with Hurley detailing how much Cyrus “really loves” his U.K. visits.

“He never spent any time here, just briefly on tour and stuff,” she explained. “He spent most of the summer here last year, and on and off we were here this summer—we had to go to America quite a bit—and he loves it. Absolutely adores it.”

As for why Hurley believes the dad of six’s achy breaky heart fell in love with the English countryside?

“He grew up in Kentucky but lives in Tennessee, and their summers are pretty brutal compared to ours,” she shared. “They’re very hot and very humid and it rains a lot—so, for him to have our beautiful air and just the beauty of our countryside, I think is beautiful for him.”

Plus, Hurley—mom to son Damian Hurley, 24, with late ex Steve Bing—has previously detailed how their compatible living styles is part of what makes their relationship work so well.

“We're really enjoying trying to get this balance between America and the UK,” she told E! News in July. “The good thing is that he's very country where he is in Tennessee. I’m very country where I am. And that's a really important thing in our relationship that neither of us want to be out and about.”

Instead, they would rather be “at home in the country and snuggling up and doing a lot of things with nature.”

“Nobody would look at either of us and think that's who we were, but we are,” she continued. “And I love it. I love gardening. He loves being outside in nature. We both love watching birds of prey. It's a really lovely life.”

For a look back through Hurley’s dating history before she fell for Cyrus, keep reading…

Elizabeth Hurley and Hugh Grant were both up-and-comers when they met making the 1988 Spanish film Rowing With the Wind, in which Grant portrayed Romantic icon Lord Byron.

"Hugh did look particularly fine in those cream britches," Hurley jested in an interview. "But I think I might have liked him anyway. There was an instant rapport. He made me laugh."

The longtime couple seemingly shot to global stardom simultaneously, Grant for his breakout performance in 1994's Four Weddings and a Funeral and Hurley, by then a major model, for the still-iconic Versace safety-pin gown she wore to the film's London premiere.

So wasn't the globe shocked when Grant was arrested in June 1995 for lewd conduct with a prostitute (and later was sentenced to two years' unsupervised probation after pleading no contest), and his publicity tour for the warm 'n' fuzzy rom-com Nine Months turned into an apology odyssey?

But Hurley stood by him, including at the film's premiere—"She has been real supportive and we're going to try and work it out," Grant said on The Tonight Show With Jay Leno—and they ultimately stayed together until 2000.

"I loved everything I was doing, and I think I just sort of, you know—you know, I've got great friends, great family," Hurley said on Larry King Live in 2000, reflecting on how she fluourished post-scandal. "And so, you know, everything turned out OK in the end."

They remained super-close, Hurley calling Grant "my best friend for life" during an appearance on E!'s Daily Pop in 2018.

"The thing is, if someone’s lovely, how could you not be on good terms with them?" she told the Daily Mail's You Magazine in 2017, explaining how she stayed good friends with most of her exes. 'None of us has ever done anything bad to one another."

In December 2024, Hurely conceded they weren't as close anymore, but not because they had soured on each other.

"I mean, he's married with five children," she noted during the Bazaar At Work summit in December 2024. "I hardly ever see him, but he's still very close to my heart." (And she is godmother to one of his kids, as well.)

Hurley gave birth to son Damian Hurley in April 2002, after her romantic relationship with his dad, financier and producer Steve Bing, had ended.

Bing wouldn't initially admit to being Damian's father, but a DNA test proved it that June.

"I have always made it perfectly clear that I don’t want any financial help from him whatsoever," Hurley said in a statement at the time, per the Daily Mail. "The money is not wanted or welcome." (Bing had said, per the BBC, that once paternity was confirmed he would "generously support" his son.)

When Bing died by suicide in June 2020 at the age of 55. Hurley wrote on Instagram that she was "saddened beyond belief" by his death.

"It is a terrible end," she continued. "Our time together was very happy and I’m posting these pictures because although we went through some tough times, it’s the good, wonderful memories of a sweet, kind man that matter. In the past year we had become close again. We last spoke on our son’s 18th birthday."

Hurley briefly teamed up with NBA point guard Steve Nash in 2001.

"They just vibed," Nash's agent told Sports Illustrated at the time. "They ended up hanging out, and there was some chemistry. The relationship is authentic."

Hurley wed businessman Arun Nayar in 2007 after four years of dating.

She announced their split on X (formerly Twitter) in December 2010: "For the record, my husband Arun & I separated a few months ago. Our close family & friends were aware of this."

Their divorce was finalized the following year. But true to form, the exes remained friends, Nayar attending several public events with Hurley and Damian post-split.

"Hugh and Arun are very important in our lives," Hurley told You in 2017.

Moreover, she shared, "I absolutely adore his mother; she’s German so we call her Mutti. In India I stay with her and when she’s here she lives around the corner, so I see her nearly every day. Twice a year I go to Mumbai, where Arun grew up and his brother still lives. I have caftans made there for my beachwear company, so I go to supervise production."

Hurley got engaged to Australian cricket star Shane Warne in 2011 after a whirlwind courtship, but they split up two years later.

"It was a very sad time for both of us," Hurley told Hello! in 2014. "Love isn't a switch that you can turn on or off at will and of course I still love him—we are part of each other's history."

So they remained, the Austin Powers star telling You three years later, "Shane is still a good friend; we were texting each other last night. Damian loves Shane’s three kids, so we’ll always be connected to each other in one way or another."

And Warne only had kind words about his ex-fiancée during a 2018 appearance on the U.K.'s Lorraine.

"When we were together we both had a great time and unfortunately it didn’t work out," he said. "It wasn’t something that she did wrong or I did wrong. It was just one of those things that fizzled out. We’re still great friends now and we still keep in touch. You know, it was the happiest time in my life."

Sadly, Warne died in March 2022 of heart disease. He was 52.

"I feel like the sun has gone behind a cloud forever," Hurley wrote on Instagram at the time. "RIP my beloved Lionheart."

Talking to Red magazine in the spring of 2019, Hurley acknowledged it would be "fantastic to fall in love with someone wonderful."

Five years later, she hard-launched her relationship with Billy Ray Cyrus in a joint Instagram post on Easter Sunday.

They met making the 2022 movie Christmas in Paradise and, according to Cyrus, definitely felt a spark before going their separate ways for two years.

Once his eight-month marriage to Australian singer Firerose ended in 2024, the Bedazzled actress reached out over text, Cyrus shared on The Ty Bentli Show April 24, 2025. And, while he didn't know where they were headed yet, "It’s been a long time since I’ve been this happy."

#3625. ‘Nation’s Dumbest’ Renewed For Season 2 At Fox

Fox will put more of the nation’s dumbest on television.

The broadcaster has renewed Nation’s Dumbest for a second season. It comes after the first season debuted on July 15 with 2.3M total viewers over its first seven days, per Nielsen, which was the network’s biggest summer launch in four years.

Jack Whitehall hosts the series, which comes from BBC Studios.

Nation’s Dumbest is the first television show where the loser is the winner. Celebrities fight it out to be eliminated from the competition as quickly as possible to avoid being crowned the nation’s dumbest. From influencers to wrestlers, comedians to politicians, the contestants are put to the test in a series of physical and mental rounds about all the things you really should know. Watch on Deadline

The first season starred the likes of Hilaria Baldwin, Dr. Drew Pinsky, Steve-O and JoJo Siwa.

“The smartest thing about Nation’s Dumbest is that it never forgot the assignment: to have universally relatable fun,” said Michael Thorn, President of Fox Entertainment Network. “Blending Jack Whitehall’s wit with a class of celebrities fully committed to the joke made for a standout summer hit. Our partners at BBC have constructed an original format that’s entertaining, accessible and distinctively Fox, so a sophomore season was a no-brainer.”

#3624. Will ‘You Can See Everything’ Catch ‘Fahrenheit 9/11’ to Be the Highest-Grossing Documentary Ever?

It’s too early to say if Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s “You Can See Everything” will win Oscars or have a direct impact on history as it pertains to Elizabeth Holmes and Theranos, but it does feel like a movie that journalists are going to be referencing for a long time as a significant benchmark for documentary films at the box office.

Currently sitting at $45.1 million domestic, with no international release to speak of yet, A24’s film is already one of the highest-grossing documentaries ever. Domestically (not factoring inflation) it has already placed firmly within the top 10 and has obliterated films like “Free Solo” ($17.5 million), “Won’t You Be My Neighbor” ($22.8 million), and “An Inconvenient Truth” ($24.1 million). It has surpassed some big nature docs like “Earth,” “Chimpanzee,” and lags only “March of the Penguins” ($77.4 million), and it has surpassed concert docs like “Katy Perry: Part of Me” ($25.3 million) and “One Direction: This Is Us” ($28.8 million) but lags the Michael Jackson doc “This Is It” ($72 million) and “Justin Bieber: Never Say Never” ($73 million). Related Stories ‘The Mosquito Bowl’ Review: ‘Friday Night Lights’ Meets ‘Five Came Back’ in Peter Berg’s Formulaic Netflix Movie About a World War II Football Game ‘I Play Rocky’ Wins Virginia Film Fest Audience Award as Peter Farrelly’s Stallone Drama Readies for Theaters

If we want to put a bunch of really dumb caveats and qualifiers on it, “You Can See Everything” is already domestically the highest-grossing non-concert, non-political, non-nature docs ever made, a made-up distinction previously held, by my count, by “Won’t You Be My Neighbor?” (sorry Mister Rogers).

But even if it passes all those other movies, it might still fall short of the documentary that remains far and away the biggest box office hit for a doc ever, Michael Moore‘s 2004 film “Fahrenheit 9/11,” Moore’s scathing takedown of President Bush and the Iraq War as released in the heat of a massive election year.

Moore’s film did not win the Oscar (though it did win the Palme D’Or at Cannes) because it was ineligible after airing on TV ahead of the election, and it did not prevent George W. Bush from being re-elected to a second term, and it probably isn’t even Moore’s best film. But it’s worth remembering just how seismic an event the film was upon its release, not unlike the stir that initial cryptic, meme-worthy teaser of Fielder and Holmes was.

Moore was already a firebrand with instant name recognition thanks to “Bowling for Columbine,” which was previously among the highest-grossing documentaries ever. But “Fahrenheit 9/11” carried with it even more publicity and attention, including because of the fight to get it to screens in the first place. Disney famously blocked Harvey and Bob Weinstein’s Miramax from releasing “Fahrenheit 9/11,” leading the Weinsteins to buy the film back and then partner with Lions Gate and IFC Films to get the movie into theaters. It even had to do so within an incredibly quick turnaround time of 24 days to get prints made and get the film delivered to theaters. Even then, Lions Gate co-president Tom Ortenberg was predicting that the film was going to disrupt distribution and set the record as the biggest documentary ever.

He was right. “Fahrenheit 9/11” opened on just 868 screens on June 25, but it was the No. 1 movie in America with $23.9 million, beating out other new releases like “White Chicks” and “The Notebook.” “You Can See Everything” opened to a similar number of theaters but made only $15.6 million in that first weekend. By July 4 weekend it had expanded to nearly double the theaters and made another $22 million, and by its fifth weekend was over $100 million. The film ultimately played in theaters for 15 weeks into October ahead of the election. Domestically, it made $119.1 million and even made an additional $103 million abroad to bring its worldwide total to $222.4 million. “This Is It” actually has the edge worldwide with $267.9 million, but the bulk of that came from overseas audiences.

The film sparked serious headlines about whether it would damage Bush in the election, and it was a big enough media sensation that “Fahrenheit 451” author Ray Bradbury lashed out at Moore as a “jerk.” Other filmmakers had released parody documentaries against Moore, and it earned sharp criticisms from political pundits disputing its accuracy. Moore’s follow-up movie “Sicko” received an even larger budget of $9 million from the Weinsteins, and it too grossed above “Bowling for Columbine” with $24.5 million domestic.

Clearly the conditions under which documentaries are released in theaters have changed drastically since “Fahrenheit 9/11.” Such a film would most likely today open on streaming, and in today’s hyper-polarized political climate, might struggle to be platformed by gun-shy distributors and streamers. And the market for all documentaries in theaters has fallen drastically in the post-Covid era. “Melania” was previously the highest-grossing doc of 2026 with $16.6 million domestic, but that one is an outlier for a number of reasons.

For “You Can See Everything” to catch “Fahrenheit 9/11,” it would have to put up “Obsession” levels of word of mouth, continually notching up at the box office this coming weekend as it continues to add screens, and the film already tripled its screen count in Weekend 2. But a long theatrical run and an awards push could realistically get it into the 70s and help it push for the Top 5 among docs.

You can see everything, yes, but even we didn’t necessarily see that one coming.

#3621. The Newest 4K Amazon Fire TV Stick Is 50% Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Amazon has cut the price of its latest Fire TV Stick 4K in half, dropping it to $29.99 from $59.99 and bringing it to its lowest price ever, according to price trackers, with a 90-day free trial of Amazon Music included in the purchase. At this price, it's an affordable way to bring 4K streaming to an older television or give a sluggish smart TV a performance upgrade. And because this streaming stick measures just 3.6 inches and draws power directly from your TV's USB port using the included cable, it can sit out of sight behind your screen without taking up another wall outlet.

Despite its small size, it supports 4K streaming over Wi-Fi 6, along with HDR10, HDR10+, and HLG for improved contrast and color on compatible televisions. What you don't get is Dolby Vision or Dolby Atmos audio. If either is important to your home theater setup, the Fire TV Stick 4K Plus, currently $39.99, is worth considering for the extra $10. Otherwise, there's little to complain about when it comes to performance, with PCMag reporting that apps respond almost instantly and 4K streams load quickly with almost no blurry, low-resolution moments.

The redesigned Alexa voice remote is another improvement, with a more comfortable shape, an easier-to-find home button, and dedicated shortcuts for major streaming services. You can also use Alexa to search for shows, launch apps, or control compatible smart home devices. The software experience is less impressive, with advertisements and recommendations dominating the home screen, leaving relatively little room for your actual apps. There's also no support for Apple AirPlay or Google Cast, so sharing content directly from your phone isn't particularly convenient. Still, if you're comfortable with Amazon's interface and don't need the missing Dolby formats, the Fire TV Stick 4K delivers responsive streaming and a useful remote for just $30.

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker.
